He is bidden to hold its tail, because the people which now refuses to believe will gather itself to the faith in the uttermost part of the Lord's body, that is, in the later time of the Church, 194Anamely at the end of the world; holding the tail of the snake, because him whom it had before despised as mortal, it will confess in the last part of the Church to be its own Redeemer. Presently the serpent returns into a rod, because as soon as the Jewish people shall have believed in Christ, he will presently appear unto judgment in the power of his Godhead: so that now the serpent is a rod, because he who on earth was a man despised will, coming from heaven, be seen as God above the angels.

(ISID.) The serpent persuaded man to death. Death therefore comes from the serpent; the rod in the serpent is Christ in death. Moses was afraid and fled: for when Christ was dead, the disciples were afraid, falling back from the hope in which they had been. He took hold of the tail, that is, of the hinder parts, and it was made a rod: because, first slain, afterwards, when all was accomplished, 194Bhe returned by rising again to that which he had been, where, death being swallowed up by life, nothing of the serpent appeared in him. Or the tail of the serpent is the end of the world, because thus the mortality of the Church is rolled onward through slippery times; some go, others come, by death, as it were by the serpent, through whom death was sown: but at the end of the world, as at the tail, we return to the hand of God, and being laid hold of we shall be restored; and when the last enemy, death, has been destroyed, we who rise again shall be at the right hand of God a rod of the kingdom.

VERS. 6, 8.-- . . . Put forth thy hand, etc. (PROCOP.) Some assert that that portent came to pass in order to show that the law is not laid down for the just man. Although afterwards he ratifies laws by what manner the disease of leprosy is to be examined and the unclean man declared, yet very many 194Claws are so delivered as though they were given to fools and to little children. So far does God accommodate himself to our weakness!

(ISID.) The hand is white, that is, unclean, for whiteness in the skin is leprosy, not fairness. For the very inheritance of God, that is, the people, being sent forth from him, was made unclean. Whence Psalm LXXIII: Why turnest thou away thy hand and thy right hand, etc. But the hand was called back into the bosom, and returned to its own colour: so the Jewish people, estranged from the bosom of God, remained unclean without; but being called back, it will return to its former colour when it shall have acknowledged the Saviour. For blindness in part hath happened in Israel (Rom. XI).

VERS. 9, 10.-- Take water, etc. (ISID.) The water signifies the people; whence Apocalypse XVII: The waters which thou sawest are peoples and nations. The people therefore is turned into 194Dblood, that is, into faith in the blood of Christ.

VERS. 10.-- I beseech thee, Lord, etc. (AUG. Quaest. in Exod. ) As to what Moses says to the Lord: I pray thee, Lord, I am not eloquent since yesterday nor since the day before, nor since thou hast begun to speak to thy servant, it is understood that he could believe himself to be made eloquent suddenly by the will of God, when he says: nor since thou hast begun to speak to thy servant, as though showing that it could have come to pass that one who had not been eloquent before yesterday and the day before should suddenly be made so from the time when the Lord began to speak with him.

(ORIG. homil. 3 in Exod.) While Moses was in Egypt, and was being instructed in all the wisdom of the Egyptians, he was not of a slender voice, nor slow of tongue, nor did he declare 195Ahimself uneloquent. For he was, as regards the Egyptians, of a sonorous voice and of incomparable eloquence. But when he heard the voice of God and received the utterances of the Lord, he perceives his voice to be slender and thin and slow, and his tongue hindered, and he pronounces himself dumb, when he begins to know the true Word, which was in the beginning with God (Joan. I): for though an untaught man be compared with dumb animals, he will seem eloquent; but if with the learned and the eloquent, foolish and dumb. But if anyone should regard the word of God and the divine wisdom, weighing it far more highly than it is weighed among us, before God he will profess himself a dumb animal. Whence Psalm LXXII: As a beast of burden was I driven before thee. [Vulg. Ps 72:23 ut jumentum factus sum apud te, "as a beast of burden was I made before thee"] Since therefore he advanced so far in understanding as to know himself, which is a great part of wisdom, 195Bthe divine condescension rewarded him, saying: I will open thy mouth, etc. Blessed are they whose mouth God opens that they may speak. Whence Psalm LXXX: Open wide thy mouth, and I will fill it. Likewise Paul says to the Ephesians VI: That speech may be given me in the opening of my mouth. The mouth, then, of those who speak true things God opens; the mouth of those who speak a lie, false witness, buffooneries, filthiness, and of whisperers too and detractors, and of those who speak idle things, the devil opens. Of Judas it is related in John XIII, that Satan entered into him, and put it into his heart to betray him. His mouth therefore he opened, that he might speak with the Pharisees how he might betray him for money received. Now it is no small grace to discern the mouth which God opens and that which the devil opens. God also opens the ears 195Cof the saints to hear the divine words; whence: The Lord will open my ear. He opens the eyes, as he opened the eyes of Agar, and she saw a well of living water; whence Eliseus: Open, Lord, the eyes of thy servant, that he may see, for there are more with us (IV Reg. VI). But the ear which is opened by the instruction of the Lord is sometimes open, sometimes shut; whence: Thou shalt not receive a vain hearing (Eccli. XLV). If vain, profane, filthy things are spoken, he who knows the instruction of the Lord shuts his ear and says: But I was as one dumb, who opened not his mouth, and as one deaf I heard not (Psal. XXXVII). But if the things spoken pertain to the profit of the soul, if the discourse is of God, if it teaches morals, invites to virtues, cuts away vices, let the ears lie open and the whole door of the soul. And it is to be noted that the law used the greatest moderation 195Din saying: Thou shalt not receive a vain hearing; it did not say, Thou shalt not hear; but, Thou shalt not receive. For we often hear vain things: for Marcion and Valentinus, and all who dispute against the Creator, speak vain things; yet we frequently hear them that we may contradict them, but we do not receive them, because the devil opens their mouth. May the Lord vouchsafe to open our mouth, that we may be able to refute the gainsayer, and to stop the mouth which the devil opens.

VERS. 11.-- Who made the mouth of man? etc. (AUG., Quaest. 8.) There are those who calumniate God, or rather the Scripture of the Old Testament, because God said that he made the dumb. What then do they say of Christ, who says openly in John X: I am come, that they who see not may see; 196Aand they who see may be made blind. And who, save one who does not know, would believe that anything can befall a man in respect of bodily defects which God does not will? But that he wills the whole justly no one doubts.

VERS. 12, 13.-- Go therefore, etc. (AUG.) Allegorically. As to what the Lord says to Moses: But now go thou, and I will open thy mouth and instruct thee what thou art to speak, [Vulg. Ex 4:12 Perge igitur, et ego ero in ore tuo: doceboque te quid loquaris, "Go therefore, and I will be in thy mouth, and I will teach thee what thou shalt speak"] it appears that not only the instruction of the mouth, but the very opening of it also, belongs to the grace of God. For he did not say, Open thy mouth, and I will instruct thee; but he promises both, I will open, and I will instruct. And in Psalm LXXX: Open wide thy mouth, and I will fill it up. Where he signifies in man the will of receiving what God gives to him that wills, so that to the beginning of the will belongs, Open wide thy mouth, but to the grace of God, 196Band I will fill it.

VERS. 14.-- The Lord being angry with Moses, said, etc. (AUG. Quaest. 10 in Exod.) He seems, as to one distrusting, not to have given the fullest faculty, etc., as far as whose figure Moses bears as it were in the midst between God and Aaron, and Aaron between Moses and the people.

VERS. 15-18.-- And I will be in thy mouth and in his mouth, etc. (ORIG.) Not only to Moses is it promised that his mouth shall be opened by the Lord, but to Aaron also. For it is said: I will open thy mouth and his mouth, and I will instruct you what ye are to do.

VERS. 19.-- The Lord therefore said, etc. (AUG., Locut. in Gen., tom. III.) Now after many days the king of Egypt died. And the Lord said to Moses in 196CMadian: Go, proceed into Egypt, [Vulg. Ex 4:19 Vade, et revertere in Aegyptum, "Go, and return into Egypt"] etc. Many kinds of expression are to be noted in these words. For first he says: Go, proceed into Egypt, as though one were not enough; then, they are all dead who sought thy life, whereas the Scripture had said that only the king of Egypt was dead: of whom alone it had before been said that he sought to kill Moses. Or did he himself die last after the other enemies? And if it is so, it is not a manner of speech, but the sense.

VERS. 20.-- Moses therefore took, etc. (AUG., Quaest. 12 in Exod.) As to what is said, that Moses set his wife and his little ones upon a beast, that he might go into Egypt, but that afterwards Jethro his father-in-law met him there with them, when he had led the people out of Egypt: it is to be understood that, after that slaying which was to come 196Dfrom the angel, the wife of Moses or of the child returned with the little ones. For some have thought that the angel terrified him for this cause, lest the female sex should accompany him to the hindrance of the ministry divinely laid upon him.

VERS. 21-23.-- I will harden his heart. (AUG., Serm. 88.) What is, I will harden his heart, save that, when my grace shall have been absent, his own wickedness hardens him? Just as, whenever water is bound fast by exceeding cold, it is loosed when the heat of the sun comes upon it, and when the sun departs it is hardened again: so by the cold of sins the charity of many grows cold, and they are hardened as it were into ice; and when the warmth of the divine mercy shall have come upon them, they are loosed. This we recognize in Pharao, who, as often as 197Athe scourges were removed from him, hardened himself and raised himself up against God; as often as he was afflicted, he made supplication. There is not therefore iniquity in God. Whence it was not so much the power as the patience of God that made Pharao to be hardened. Which thing we too are wont to practise with our home-born servants, whom, when they sin frequently, we indulge, saying: I have made thee such as thou art, I by sparing thee have nourished thy insolence and thy negligence.

VERS. 24.-- The Lord met him, etc. (AUG., Quaest. 11 in Exod.) Another reading: And it came to pass on the way at the resting-place, an angel met him, and sought to kill him: and Sephora, taking a pebble, circumcised the foreskin of her son, and fell down at his feet and said: The blood of the circumcision of my child hath stood, and he departed from him because of this which she said: The blood of the 197Bcircumcision hath stood. First it is asked whom the angel wished to slay, etc., as far as by a great mystery, if I am not mistaken.

VERS. 25, 26.-- Thou art to me a spouse of blood, etc. According to history, the Gentile mother, wondering at the newness of the thing, could say to Moses: Thou art to me a spouse of blood; as if to say, the rite of thy nation compels me to shed the blood of my son, which among other nations is not the custom. But mystically Sephora signifies the Church from among the Gentiles, which strips the foreskin of her son, that is, of the Gentile people, with a most sharp stone, that is, with the teaching of the Holy Spirit, or with that rock of which it is said in II Corinthians X: Now the rock was Christ, that it may be cleansed from all defilement of flesh and spirit: And that he may put off the old man with his deeds (Col. III). For the blood of circumcision stands 197Cwhen this corruptible shall put on incorruption.

VERS. 27-31.-- And the Lord said, etc. (ORIG., hom. 32.) Aaron met Moses, and he went out of Egypt. But it matters where he meets Moses, whose mouth is to be opened by the Lord: and he meets him in the mount of God. Deservedly is his mouth opened who meets him in the mount of God. Peter, James and John in the mount deserved both to see the Lord Jesus transfigured, and Moses and Elias with him in glory. Thou also, unless thou shalt have ascended the mount of God, and there met Moses, that is, unless thou shalt have ascended the lofty ascent of the law, unless thou shalt have seized the summit of spiritual understanding, there is no door opened to thee by the Lord. If thou shalt have stood in the lowly place of the letter, thou hast not met Moses in the mount of God; God has not 197Dopened thy mouth, nor instructed thee what it behoves thee to speak; unless Aaron had met Moses in the mount, unless he had seen his understanding to be lofty, he would not speak to him the words of God, nor would he have delivered to him the power of the signs, nor have made him a sharer of so great a mystery.

CAPUT V.

VERS. 1.-- . . . . . Dimitte populum meum. (ORIG., hom. 3 in Exod.) Non vult Moses ut populus in Aegypto positus serviat Deo, sed ut exeat in desertum, et ibi serviat. Dum enim quis in tenebrosis saeculi actibus manet, et in negotiorum obscuritate versatur, non potest servire Deo. Non enim potestis servire Deo et mammonae (Matth. VI). Exeundum est 198Aergo de Aegypto: relinquendus est mundus, non loco, sed animo, si volumus servire Domino. Inde Joannes ait: Filioli, nolite diligere mundum, neque ea quae in mundo sunt (I Joan. II).

VERS. 2.-- Qui est Dominus? Superbiam diaboli exprimit, qui subdi Deo contemnens, ait: Similis ero Altissimo (Isa. XIV); et quasi de Domini nativitate dubitans, dicit:

Si Filius Dei es, dic ut lapides isti panes fiant (Matth. IV). Postea vero quasi coacte daemones clamaverunt: Quid venisti ante tempus torquere nos? Scimus quia es Filius Dei (Matth. VIII). Post tormenta sciunt, quae ante scire noluerant. Pharao quoque verberatus pro se supplicari ad Dominum rogat. Quis est dominus, ut audiam vocem ejus? etc. Vides 198Bquid agit cruda superbia; videbitis paulo post quantum proficit in flagellis: quantum melior verberatus efficitur; qui modo dicit, Nescio Dominum, verberatus dicturus est: Orate pro me Dominum. Nemo ergo ita ineruditus sit divinae disciplinae, ut flagella divina perniciem putet et poenalem interitum. Ecce etiam Pharao durissimus proficit verberatus: ante enim verbera Dominum nescit, verberatusque pro se rogat supplicari; et proficiens in poenis, scit agnoscere cur puniatur. Nescio, ait, Dominum. In Evangelio verberatus emendat hanc vocem, quia daemones clamaverunt: Scimus te quis sis: tu es Filius Dei vivi. Post flagella etiam Israel dimittet, et etiam urgebit exire. Nulla enim societas luci ad tenebras, nulla pars fideli cum infideli (II Cor. VI).

198CVERS. 3.-- Deus Hebraeorum vocavit nos, etc. (AUG. in Exod. ) Quaeritur quomodo populo dicatur, etc., usque ad ut justum judicium consequatur.

(ORIG.) Moraliter. Iter tridui de Aegypto proficiscimur, si ita nos ab omni inquinamento animae, et corporis, ac spiritus conservemus, ut integer spiritus noster, et anima, et corpus, in diem Christi servetur.

(ORIG.) Tridui iter proficiscimur de Aegypto, si rationabilem, moralem, naturalem sapientiam, de rebus mundialibus auferentes, ad instituta divina convertimus. Tridui iter de Aegypto proficiscimur, si purificantes in nobis dicta, facta vel cogitata (tria sunt enim haec per quae homines peccare possunt), 198Defficiamur mundi corde, ut Deum videre possimus. Princeps vero Aegypti ubi videt se vehementius urgeri ut dimittat populum Dei, secundo loco cupit impetrare ut non longius abeant, ne totum triduum proficiscantur, dicens: Ne longe abeatis. Non vult longe a se fieri populum Dei. Vult enim, si non in facto, vel in sermone peccent; si non in sermone, vel in cogitatione. Non vult ut totum triduum a se proficiscantur. Vult in nobis vel unum diem, videlicet, in aliis duos, in aliis totum triduum possidere. Beati illi qui ab eo totum triduum recedunt.

Viam trium dierum in solitudinem, et sacrificemus Deo, etc. (ISID.) Viam trium dierum ambulamus exeuntes de Aegypto, ut ad locum perveniamus ubi 199Aimmolare debemus Domino. Haec via Christus est, qui ait Joan. XIV: Ego sum via, veritas et vita. Qui enim confessus fuerit Christum ore, et crediderit corde quod Deus illum suscitavit tertia die, salvus erit, perveniens ad locum in quo immoletur sacrificium laudis.

(STRAB.) Viam trium dierum, etc. Tertia mansione, postquam transierunt mare Rubrum, pervenerunt Ethan, qui locus etiam Mara vocatur, propter amaras aquas. Vel trium dierum, id est, trium mensium, quia tertio mense postquam de Aegypto exierunt, pervenerunt Sina, ubi sacrificaverunt, et legem acceperunt, ubi erectum est et tabernaculum.

(ORIG., hom. 3 in Exod.) Nunc quoque Moses, quem habemus nobiscum (habemus enim Mosen et 199Bprophetas), id est, lex Dei vult te educere de Aegypto, si audias eam; vult te longe facere a Pharaone, eruere de opere luti et palearum: si tamen audias, et spiritualiter intelligas; non vult te in carnis et tenebrarum actibus permanere, sed exire ad eremum, ad locum scilicet perturbationibus et fluctibus saeculi vacuum, et ad quietem silentii. Verba enim sapientiae in silentio et quiete discuntur. In hoc loco poteris Domino immolare legem Dei, et virtutem divinae vocis agnoscere. Ideo cupit Moses te ejicere de fluctibus negotiorum et strepitu populorum, et de Aegypto, id est de tenebris ignorantiae, ut legem Dei audias et lucem scientiae capias.

VERS. 4-8.-- Quare, Moses et Aaron, sollicitatis, 199Cetc. (ORIG., ibid.) Dum secum est populus, et lutum aut laterem operatur, dum in paleis occupatur, non putat esse perversum, sed recte incedere. Si vero dicat, Volo ire dierum trium viam, et servire Domino, perverti populum dicit per Mosen et Aaron. Hodie quoque si Moses et Aaron, id est propheticus et sacerdotalis sermo animam sollicitet ad servitutem Dei, exire de saeculo, renuntiare omnibus quae possidet, attendere legi et verbo Dei: continuo audies unanimes et amicos Pharaonis dicentes: Videte quomodo seducuntur homines, et pervertuntur adolescentes, ne laborent, ne militent, ne agant aliquid quod prosit, relictis rebus necessariis ineptias sectantur et otium. Quid est servire Deo? Laborare nolunt et otii occasiones quaerunt: haec erant tunc 199Dverba Pharaonis, haec et nunc amici ejus loquuntur, nec solum verbis, sed et verberibus persequuntur. Flagellari jubet scribas Hebraeorum, paleas non dari, opus exigi; haec passi sunt patres, et nunc Dei populus patitur. Qui se ex integro principi hujus mundi tradiderunt, prospere agunt; omnia, ut putant, feliciter eveniunt: servis autem Dei etiam victus parvus et humilis non succedit. Hae sunt enim paleae quae dabantur a Pharaone; unde quidam fatigati dicunt ad Pharaonem: Quid tu affligis populum tuum? Superati enim verberibus a fide cadunt, et confitentur se esse Pharaonis populum, non enim omnes qui ex Israel, hi sunt Israelitae.

VERS. 9-13.-- Opprimantur operibus et expleant 200Aea, etc. Mystice. Ex quo loqui coepit Moses ad Pharaonem magis affligitur populus Dei: ex quo in animam sermo Dei pervenit, diabolus acrius insurgit, et majora vitia immittit. Prius enim quam sermo Dei veniret, qui argueret vitia, in pace durabant, at ubi venit, turbatio magna consurgit.

VERS. 14-20.-- Flagellatique sunt qui, etc. Filii Israel gravius attriti sunt, postquam de Aegypto egredi statuerunt; quia qui mundo et pravis operibus renuntiare volunt, saevioribus tentationibus a diabolo impugnantur.

VERS. 21.-- Videat Dominus, etc. (GREG. l. XXIX, Moral., cap. 14.) Cum lux divina cor humanum illustrat, usque ad quasi fetere se in oculis Pharaonis 200Bdolet.

Quoniam fetere, etc. (ORIG. Hom. 3.) Allegorice. Verum dicunt, quamvis nesciant, sicut Caiphas, qui ait: Expedit ut unus moriatur pro populo (Joan. XVIII); quid diceret nesciebat. Sicut enim Apostolus ait: Christi bonus odor sumus, aliis in vitam, aliis in mortem, ita propheticus sermo suavis odor est credentibus; dubiis vero et incredulis, et qui fatentur se populum Pharaonis, odor exsecrabilis efficitur. Ipse quoque Moses dicit Domino: Ex quo locutus sum cum Pharaone, afflixisti populum tuum; quia antequam sermo Dei audiatur, non est tribulatio, vel tentatio: sed ubi signum belli tuba praedicationis ostenderit, tribulationum pugna consurgit. Ex quo ergo loqui coepit Moses et Aaron ad Pharaonem, 200Caffligitur populus Dei. Ex quo in animam tuam sermo Dei prolatus est, certamen intra te virtutibus contra vitia suscitatur, quae prius intra te in pace durabant. Sed ubi facere coepit sermo Dei cujusque discrimen, turbatio magna consurgit, et sine foedere nascitur bellum: quia injustitia justitiae non convenit, nec sobrietati ebrietas, vel veritati mendacium. Ideo non turbemur si videmur odor exsecrabilis Pharaoni. Exsecratio enim vitii dicitur virtus. Sed sicut stetit Moses ante Pharaonem, stemus et nos; non flectamur, succincti lumbos in veritate, et calceati pedes in praeparatione Evangelii pacis (Ephes. VI). Stamus autem confidenter, Dominum deprecamur, ut statuat pedes nostros supra firmam petram, ne moveantur pedes nostri. Stemus autem, id est, 200Dobsistamus, secundum illud I Petr. V: Cui resistite fortes in fide: ut cito conteratur Satanas sub pedibus nostris. Quanto enim nos constanter et fortiter stamus, tanto Pharao infirmius. Si autem infirmi vel dubii esse coeperimus, validior fiet. Cum enim elevaret Moses manus, vincebatur Amalec; cum quasi lassas dejiceret, invalescebat. Nos ergo in virtute crucis Christi extollamus brachia, et levemus in oratione sanctas manus, ut Dei auxilium mereamur. Hinc Jacobus ait: Resistite diabolo et fugiet a vobis (Jac. III). Agamus ergo ut non solum fugiat, sed conteratur sub pedibus nostris exstinctus in profundo abyssi.

Nos autem si recedamus de Aegypto vitiorum, 201Afluctus saeculi tanquam iter solidum evadimus per Jesum Christum.

VERS. 22, 23.-- Domine, cur afflixisti populum istum? (AUG.) Non sunt haec verba contumaciae vel indignationis, sed inquisitionis et orationis, sicut ex his apparet quae Dominus respondit. Non enim arguit infidelitatem ejus, sed quid sit facturus aperuit.

CHAPTER V.

VERS. 1.-- . . . . . Let my people go. (ORIG., hom. 3 in Exod.) Moses does not will that the people, being set in Egypt, should serve God, but that it should go out into the desert, and there serve him. For while a man remains in the dark deeds of the world, and is engaged in the obscurity of business, he cannot serve God. For ye cannot serve God and mammon (Matth. VI). We must go out therefore 198Afrom Egypt: the world must be left behind, not in place but in mind, if we wish to serve the Lord. Whence John says: Little children, love not the world, nor the things which are in the world (I Joan. II).

VERS. 2.-- Who is the Lord? He expresses the pride of the devil, who, scorning to be made subject to God, says: I will be like the Most High (Isa. XIV); and as though doubting of the Lord's nativity, says:

If thou be the Son of God, say that these stones be made bread (Matth. IV). But afterwards, as though under constraint, the demons cried out: Why art thou come to torment us before the time? We know that thou art the Son of God (Matth. VIII). After torments they know what before they had refused to know. Pharao also, when he had been scourged, asks that supplication be made for him to the Lord. Who is the lord, that I should hear his voice? etc. Thou seest 198Bwhat raw pride does; you will see a little later how much it profits under scourges; how much better a man is made when he has been beaten; he who now says, I know not the Lord, when he has been beaten will say: Pray to the Lord for me. Let no one therefore be so untaught in the divine discipline as to think the divine scourges a ruin and a penal destruction. Behold, even Pharao, most hardened, profits when he is beaten: for before the stripes he knows not the Lord, and when beaten he asks that supplication be made for him; and profiting under punishments, he knows how to acknowledge why he is punished. I know not, he says, the Lord. In the Gospel, being beaten, he amends this utterance, for the demons cried out: We know thee who thou art: thou art the Son of the living God. After the scourges he will let Israel go also, and will even urge it to depart. For there is no fellowship of light with darkness, no part of the faithful with the unbeliever (II Cor. VI).

198CVERS. 3.-- The God of the Hebrews hath called us, etc. (AUG. in Exod. ) It is asked how it is said to the people, etc., as far as that he may obtain a just judgment.

(ORIG.) Morally. We set out on a three days' journey from Egypt, if we so keep ourselves from every defilement of soul and of body and of spirit, that our whole spirit, and soul, and body may be preserved unto the day of Christ.

(ORIG.) We set out on a three days' journey from Egypt, if, taking away rational, moral and natural wisdom from worldly things, we turn them to the divine ordinances. We set out on a three days' journey from Egypt, if, purifying in ourselves things said, done or thought (for these are the three by which men can sin), 198Dwe are made clean of heart, that we may be able to see God. But the prince of Egypt, when he sees himself the more vehemently pressed to let the people of God go, desires in the second place to obtain that they go not farther off, that they may not set out for the whole three days, saying: Go not far away. He does not will the people of God to be far from him. For he wills that, if not in deed, at least in speech they should sin; if not in speech, at least in thought. He does not will that they should set out from him for the whole three days. He wills to possess in some of us one day, in others two, in others the whole three days. Blessed are they who withdraw from him the whole three days.

A journey of three days into the wilderness, and let us sacrifice to God, etc. (ISID.) We walk a journey of three days going out of Egypt, that we may come to the place where 199Awe ought to offer sacrifice to the Lord. This way is Christ, who says in John XIV: I am the way, the truth and the life. For whosoever shall have confessed Christ with the mouth, and believed with the heart that God raised him up on the third day, shall be saved, coming to the place in which a sacrifice of praise may be offered.

(STRAB.) A journey of three days, etc. At the third encampment, after they had crossed the Red Sea, they came to Ethan, which place is also called Mara, because of the bitter waters. Or of three days, that is, of three months, because in the third month after they went out of Egypt they came to Sina, where they sacrificed and received the law, where the tabernacle also was set up.

(ORIG., hom. 3 in Exod.) Now also Moses, whom we have with us (for we have Moses and 199Bthe prophets), that is, the law of God, wills to lead thee out of Egypt, if thou wilt hear it; it wills to set thee far from Pharao, to rescue thee from the work of clay and of straw: if only thou wilt hear, and understand spiritually; it does not will thee to remain in the deeds of the flesh and of darkness, but to go out to the desert, namely to a place empty of the disturbances and waves of the world, and to the quiet of silence. For the words of wisdom are learned in silence and quiet. In this place thou shalt be able to offer to the Lord the law of God, and to acknowledge the power of the divine voice. Therefore Moses desires to cast thee out of the waves of business and the noise of peoples, and out of Egypt, that is, out of the darkness of ignorance, that thou mayest hear the law of God and receive the light of knowledge.

VERS. 4-8.-- Why, Moses and Aaron, do ye stir up, 199Cetc. (ORIG., ibid.) While the people is with him, and works clay or brick, while it is occupied with straw, he does not think it to be perverted, but to walk rightly. But if it should say, I wish to go a three days' journey, and to serve the Lord, he says the people is perverted through Moses and Aaron. Today also, if Moses and Aaron, that is, the prophetic and the priestly word, should stir up a soul to the service of God, to go out of the world, to renounce all that it possesses, to attend to the law and the word of God: straightway thou wilt hear the friends and confederates of Pharao saying: See how men are seduced, and youths are perverted, that they may not labour, may not serve as soldiers, may not do anything that profits; leaving necessary things behind, they follow after trifles and idleness. What is it to serve God? They are unwilling to labour and seek occasions of idleness: these were then 199Dthe words of Pharao, these his friends speak now also, and not only with words, but they persecute with stripes as well. He commands the scribes of the Hebrews to be scourged, straw not to be given, work to be exacted; these things the fathers suffered, and now the people of God suffers them. They who have wholly given themselves over to the prince of this world prosper; all things, as they think, fall out happily: but to the servants of God even a small and lowly sustenance does not come. For these are the straws which were given by Pharao; whence certain men, being wearied, say to Pharao: Why dost thou afflict thy people? For being overcome by stripes they fall from the faith, and confess themselves to be the people of Pharao, for not all who are of Israel, these are Israelites.

VERS. 9-13.-- Let them be oppressed with works and let them fulfil 200Athem, etc. Mystically. From the time when Moses began to speak to Pharao the people of God is the more afflicted: from the time when the word of God comes into the soul, the devil rises up more sharply, and sends in greater vices. For before the word of God came, which should reprove vices, they endured in peace; but when it came, a great disturbance arises.

VERS. 14-20.-- And they were scourged who, etc. The children of Israel were more grievously worn down after they had determined to go out of Egypt; because they who wish to renounce the world and evil works are assailed by the devil with fiercer temptations.

VERS. 21.-- Let the Lord see, etc. (GREG. l. XXIX, Moral., cap. 14.) When the divine light illumines the human heart, as far as he grieves that he as it were stinks in the eyes of Pharao. 200B

Because to stink, etc. (ORIG. Hom. 3.) Allegorically. They speak truth, although they know it not, like Caiphas, who said: It is expedient that one should die for the people (Joan. XVIII); he knew not what he was saying. For as the Apostle says: We are the good odour of Christ, to some unto life, to others unto death, so the prophetic word is a sweet odour to them that believe; but to the doubting and unbelieving, and to those who confess themselves the people of Pharao, it is made an execrable odour. Moses himself also says to the Lord: From the time when I spoke with Pharao, thou hast afflicted thy people; because before the word of God is heard there is no tribulation or temptation: but when the trumpet of preaching shall have shown the signal of war, the battle of tribulations arises. From the time therefore when Moses and Aaron began to speak to Pharao, 200Cthe people of God is afflicted. From the time when the word of God has been uttered into thy soul, a contest is stirred up within thee of the virtues against the vices, which before endured within thee in peace. But when the word of God has begun to make the distinction between one thing and another, a great disturbance arises, and without treaty a war is born: because injustice does not agree with justice, nor drunkenness with sobriety, nor lying with truth. Therefore let us not be troubled if we seem an execrable odour to Pharao. For the execration of vice is called virtue. But as Moses stood before Pharao, let us also stand; let us not be bent, having our loins girt about with truth, and our feet shod with the preparation of the Gospel of peace (Ephes. VI). And we stand confidently, we beseech the Lord that he set our feet upon a firm rock, lest our feet be moved. But let us stand, that is, 200Dlet us withstand, according to that of I Peter V: Whom resist ye, strong in faith: that Satan may quickly be crushed under our feet. For by how much the more steadfastly and bravely we stand, by so much the more weakly Pharao. But if we shall have begun to be weak or doubting, he will become stronger. For when Moses lifted up his hands, Amalec was overcome; when he let them down as though weary, he grew strong. Let us therefore in the power of the cross of Christ lift up our arms, and raise in prayer holy hands, that we may deserve the help of God. Hence James says: Resist the devil and he will fly from you (Jac. III). Let us act therefore that he may not only fly, but be crushed under our feet, extinguished in the depth of the abyss.

But if we withdraw from the Egypt of vices, 201Awe escape the waves of the world as it were by a solid path, through Jesus Christ.

VERS. 22, 23.-- Lord, why hast thou afflicted this people? (AUG.) These are not words of contumacy or of indignation, but of inquiry and of prayer, as appears from those things which the Lord answered. For he did not reprove his unbelief, but disclosed what he was about to do.

CAPUT VI.

VERS. 3-6.--. . . Et nomen meum Adonai non indicavi eis. Sicut tibi. Per successiones enim aetatum crevit divinae cognitionis augmentum. Apparuit Dominus Abrahae; locutus est facie ad faciem cum Mose; David vero ait psalm. CXVIII: Super senes intellexi, etc. Apostoli Dei Filium praesentem cernebant, et quidquid volebant, interrogabant, quibus 201Betiam dicitur Lucae II: Multi reges et prophetae, etc.

VERS. 7, 8.-- De ergastulo Aegyptiorum. Ergastulum et ergasterium, id est operatorium: scilicet ubi rei ad opera facienda religabantur.

VERS. 9-11.-- Non acquieverunt ei, etc. Nota Dei clementiam: non creditur a filiis Israel, et tamen monere non cessat. Contemnitur a Pharaone, et longanimiter exspectat.

VERS. 12, 13.-- Ecce filii, etc. (AUG. Quaest. 16 in Exod.) LXX. Ecce ego gracili voce sum et quomodo exaudiet me Pharao. Non videtur tamen propter multitudinem populi excusare se de vocis gracilitate, sed etiam propter unum hominem: mirum si tam gracilis vocis fuit, ut nec ab uno posset audiri. An 201Cforte regius fastus non permittebat eos de proximo loqui.

VERS. 14-19.-- Isti sunt principes, etc. (AUG. Quaest. 15.) Volens Scriptura originem Moysi demonstrare, quod ejus actio expetebat, a primogenito Jacob, id est Ruben, coepit; inde ad Simeon, inde ad Levi: ultra progressum non est, quia ex Levi Moses. Hi autem commemorantur, qui jam memorati fuerant in illis septuaginta quinque in quibus Israel intraverat in Aegyptum. Non enim primam, vel secundam, sed tertiam tribum, id est Leviticam, voluit Deus esse sacerdotalem.

VERS 20.-- Accepit autem Amram uxorem Jochabed, etc. (STRAB.) Amram interpretatur pater excelsus, qui significat Christum; Jochabed, Dei gratia, 201Dquae significat Ecclesiam: Ex Christo et Ecclesia nascitur Moses, id est lex spiritualis, et Aaron, scilicet verum sacerdotium.

Accepit autem, etc. Haec de tribu Juda fuit, soror Naason, qui fuit princeps tribus Judae in deserto: nondum enim praeceptum erat ut quisque, de tribu sua uxorem acciperet. Ideo autem hae tribus, id est Levitica et regalis, jam tunc conjungebantur, ut intelligeretur Christus rex et sacerdos futurus ex eis.

VERS. 21-26.-- Filii quoque Isaar, etc. (STRAB.) Quaeritur cur Core, quem pro iniquitate terra vivum absorbuit, in genealogia justorum scriptus sit? Sed haereticos cum catholicis in Ecclesia significat futuros

202AVERS. 27-30.-- Iste Moyses. (STRAB.) Hoc ex suo addit Esdras, qui omnem bibliothecam post captivitatem Babylonicam, cunctis libris a Nabuchodonosor succensis reparavit; vel ipse Moses de se tanquam de alio loquitur.

CHAPTER VI.

VERS. 3-6.--. . . And my name Adonai I did not show them. As to thee. For through the successions of the ages the increase of divine knowledge grew. The Lord appeared to Abraham; he spoke face to face with Moses; but David says in Psalm CXVIII: I have understood above the ancients, etc. The Apostles beheld the Son of God present, and asked whatever they wished, to whom 201Bit is also said in Luke II: Many kings and prophets, etc.

VERS. 7, 8.-- Out of the workhouse of the Egyptians. Ergastulum and ergasterium, that is, a workshop: namely where the accused were bound fast for the doing of works.

VERS. 9-11.-- They did not acquiesce in him, etc. Note the clemency of God: he is not believed by the children of Israel, and yet he ceases not to admonish. He is contemned by Pharao, and long-sufferingly he waits.

VERS. 12, 13.-- Behold the children, etc. (AUG. Quaest. 16 in Exod.) The LXX. Behold I am of a slender voice, and how shall Pharao hear me. Yet he does not seem to excuse himself for the slenderness of his voice on account of the multitude of the people, but on account of one man also: it is a marvel if he was of so slender a voice that he could not be heard even by one. Or 201Cperhaps the royal disdain did not permit them to speak from near at hand.

VERS. 14-19.-- These are the princes, etc. (AUG. Quaest. 15.) Scripture, wishing to show the origin of Moses, which his action required, began from the firstborn of Jacob, that is, from Ruben; thence to Simeon, thence to Levi: it did not go on further, because from Levi is Moses. And these are commemorated who had already been mentioned among those seventy-five with whom Israel had entered into Egypt. For God willed not the first, nor the second, but the third tribe, that is, the Levitical, to be the priestly one.

VERS 20.-- And Amram took to wife Jochabed, etc. (STRAB.) Amram is interpreted lofty father, who signifies Christ; Jochabed, the grace of God, 201Dwho signifies the Church: from Christ and the Church is born Moses, that is, the spiritual law, and Aaron, namely the true priesthood.

And he took, etc. She was of the tribe of Juda, sister of Naason, who was prince of the tribe of Juda in the desert: for it had not yet been commanded that each man, should take a wife from his own tribe. And these tribes, that is, the Levitical and the royal, were even then joined together, that it might be understood that Christ was to be king and priest from them.

VERS. 21-26.-- The sons also of Isaar, etc. (STRAB.) It is asked why Core, whom the earth swallowed alive for his iniquity, is written in the genealogy of the just? But it signifies that heretics will be with catholics in the Church

202AVERS. 27-30.-- This is Moses. (STRAB.) This Esdras adds of his own, who restored the whole library after the Babylonian captivity, all the books having been burnt by Nabuchodonosor; or Moses himself speaks of himself as of another.

CAPUT VII.

VERS. 1-6.-- Dixitque Dominus ad Moysen: Ecce constitui te dominum, etc. (AUG. Quaest. 17 in Exod., tom III.) Notandum quod cum ad populi mitteretur, non ei dictum est: Ecce dedi te Deum populo, et frater tuus erit tuus propheta; sed, frater tuus loquetur tibi ad populum. Dictum est etiam erit os tuum, et tu illi in his quae ad Deum; non dictum est, tu illi Deus. Pharaoni autem dicitur Moses datus deus, et secundum analogiam propheta Mosi est datus 202BAaron, sed ad Pharaonem. Hic insinuatur nobis ea loqui prophetas Dei quae audiunt ab eo; nihilque aliud esse prophetam Dei, nisi enuntiatorem verborum Dei hominibus, qui Deum vel non possunt, vel non merentur audire.

(GREG. hom. 8 super Ezech.) Aliquando Deus nuncupative, aliquando essentialiter dicitur. Nuncupative, ut hic: Constitui te deum Pharaonis. Et alibi: Applica illum ad deos. Et alibi: Deus stetit in synagoga deorum, etc. Essentialiter sicut hic: Ego sum Deus Abraham, Deus Isaac, et Deus Jacob, quod volens Paulus discernere ait: Ex quibus Christus secundum carnem natus est, qui est Deus benedictus in saecula. Nuncupative enim dicitur Deus inter omnia, essentialiter autem super omnia; ut ergo ostenderet 202CChristum naturaliter Deum, non tantum Deum, sed Deum super omnia esse memoravit. Justus enim quilibet Deus est, sed inter omnia, quia nuncupative: Christus autem super omnia, quia naturaliter Deus.

(AUG. Quaest. 18 in Exod.) Assidue dicit Deus, etc. usque ad indurabo, id est, quam durum sit demonstrabo.

VERS. 7, 8.-- Erat autem Moses octoginta annorum, etc. (STRAB.) Propter circumcisionem scilicet, quae octava die celebrari jussa est: eamdem enim significationem habet octogenarius quam et octonarius infra eum minor. Erat autem Moses octoginta annorum. Quando Moses et Aaron locuti sunt ad Pharaonem, Moses erat octogenarius, Aaron vero 202Doctoginta trium annorum: quia lex per praedicatores ad gentes oblata circumcisionem spiritualem docuit, et ipsi perfecte mundari studebant, et sic fidem sanctae Trinitatis praedicabant.

VERS. 9.-- Cum dixerit vobis Pharao, etc. (AUG. Quaest. 19 et 20.) Hic non erat opus voce, pro cujus gracilitate videbatur Mosi datus Aaron, sed virga erat projicienda ut draco fieret. Cur hoc ergo Moses ipse non fecit, nisi quia mediatio Aaron inter Mosen et Pharaonem magnae fidei figuram gerit. Hoc quoque notandum quod scriptum est: Projecit Aaron virgam suam: forte si dixisset projecit virgam, nulla quaestio esset; quod vero addit suam, cum eam Moses dederit, non frustra forsitan dictum est. An 203Aerat utrique illa virga communis, ut cujuslibet eorum diceretur, verum diceretur?

(STRAB.) Virga Mosi Christum significat, qui ex regali stirpe carnem sumpsit. Unde eadem virga in colubrum conversa, omnes virgas maleficorum devoravit, quia mors Christi mortem nostram consumpsit.

(ORIG. hom. 4 in Exod.) Apostolus dicit I Cor. XIV: Aemulamini spiritualia dona magis autem ut prophetetis. Tentemus et nos aemulationem bonorum suscipere, a Domino vero plenitudinem muneris exspectare. Pro hoc enim dicitur psal. LXXX: Aperi os tuum et implebo illud; pro illo vero: Punge oculum, et producit lacrymam; punge cor, et producit sensum. Puto ergo quod Moses ad Aegyptum veniens, et virgam ferens, 203Bqua castigat et verberat Aegyptum decem plagis, lex Dei sit, qua mundum decem plagis, id est decem mandatis decalogi, corripiat et emendet. Virga qua haec geruntur, et Aegyptus subigitur, et Pharao superatur, crux Christi est, per quam mundus et princeps mundi superatur, id est diabolus, cum principibus et potestatibus. Virga projecta fit draco vel serpens, et devorat Aegyptiorum magorum serpentes. Serpens vero pro sapientia vel prudentia poni solet; unde Matth. X: Estote prudentes sicut serpentes, etc. Et Gen. III: Serpens erat prudentior cunctis animalibus, etc. Crux ergo Christi, cujus praedicatio stulta videbatur, quam Moses, id est lex, continet (secundum illud Joan. V: De me enim ille scripsit ), postquam in terram projecta est, id est ad fidem hominum venit, 203Cconversa est in sapientiam, et in tantam quae omnem sapientiam Aegyptiorum, id est hujus mundi, devoraret. Intuere enim quomodo stultam Deus fecit sapientiam hujus mundi, postquam manifestavit Christum crucifixum esse Dei virtutem et sapientiam: et universus jam mundus comprehensus est ab eo, qui dixit I Cor. III: Comprehendam sapientes in astutia eorum.

VERS. 10.-- Tulitque Aaron, etc. (ISID. in Gen., tom. V.) Tulitque virgam coram Pharaone, etc., usque ad: Ero mors tua, o mors, etc.

Quae versa est in colubrum. Lex Mosi quasi insipida erat ut virga, sed versa in colubrum, id est in spiritualem intelligentiam, potabilis effecta est et 203Ddelectabilis.

VERS. 11.-- Vocavit autem Pharao sapientes, etc. Sciendum quia vel diabolicis figmentis spectantium oculos deludebant, ut res in sua natura manentes aliud viderentur; vel, secundum Augustinum, daemones, quamvis mali naturam suam non amiserunt: ideo dum per incantationem eorum malefici aliquid efficere conantur, discurrunt per mundum, et subito semina eorum, de quibus hoc agitur, afferunt. Sicque ex illis, permittente Deo, novas rerum species producunt.

Similiter. In prima plaga dicit cum aqua vertitur in sanguinem; et in secunda, cum ranae ebulliunt; et in tertia cum ciniphos ingruunt; in quarta, cum 204Acynomyia educitur; in quinta, cum manus Domini fit super pecora Aegyptiorum, dicuntur similia.

VERS. 12.-- Dracones. Apo tou δέρκειν dicuntur, id est videre, quia vident acutius caeteris animalibus.

Sed devoravit, etc. (AUG. Quaest. 21.) Si dictum esset, Absorbuit draco Aaron virgas eorum, intelligeretur verus draco Aaron phantastica figmenta non absorbuisse, sed virgas, etc., usque ad Deus vero solus creator est verus, qui causas ipsas et rationes seminarias rebus inseruit.

VERS. 13, 14.-- Induratumque, etc. Induravit Deus cor Pharaonis, quia diabolum ita induravit post peccatum, ut poenitentiae compunctione nunquam emolliatur, sicut in Job XLI dicitur: Cor ejus indurabitur quasi lapis.

204BVERS. 15, 16.-- Vade ad eum mane, ecce egredietur, etc. (ORIG. hom. 4.) Primo quidem non intrat Moses ad Pharaonem, sed occurrit ei exeunti ad aquas; postea vero intrat; post haec nec intrat, sed accersitus accedit: quia sive nobis in verbo Dei et assertione religionis certamen est cum Pharaone, sive obsessas ab eo animas eripere conamur, et est in nobis in disputatione luctamen, non prima fronte ingredi debemus ad ultima quaestionum loca, sed occurrendum est nobis adversario, et ad aquas suas, id est, auctoritates gentium philosophorum, ut ipsos primo arguamus et errasse doceamus; deinde ingrediendum ad interiora certaminis. Dicit enim Dominus: Nisi quis prius alligaverit fortem, non potest 204Cintrare in domum ejus et vasa ejus diripere (Marc. XII). Prius ergo ligandus est fortis et quaestionum vinculis constringendus, et ita introeundum ad diripienda vasa, et liberandas animas quas deceptione et fraude possederat. Quod cum saepius fecerimus, et steterimus contra ipsum, succincti scilicet lumbos in veritate, et stantes in Domino, et viriliter agentes, artifex antiquus et callidus victum se simulabit et cedere, ut negligentiores nos faciat ad certamen. Poenitentiam quoque simulabit, deprecabitur nos discedere, sed non longe: vult nos aliqua parte sibi esse vicinos. Sed nisi ab eo longius recedamus, et mare transeamus, et dicamus cogitatione, locutione, et opere: Quantum distat ortus ab occidente, tantum fecit a nobis iniquitates nostras, 204Dsalvi esse non possumus. Oremus ergo misericordiam Dei, ut et nos eripiat de terra Aegypti, de potestate tenebrarum, et Pharaonem cum exercitu suo tanquam plumbum demergat in aqua vehementissima. Nos autem liberati cum gaudio et exsultatione hymnum cantemus Domino. Gloriose enim honorificatus est, etc.

(ISID.) Plagae in Aegypto corporaliter gestae sunt, sed in nobis spiritualiter geruntur. Aegyptus enim saeculi forma est.

VERS. 17-19.-- Aquam fluminis, etc. (STRAB.) Aqua in sanguinem versa mundanam sapientiam significat, in qua omnis qui manserit necatur, sicut in sanguine piscis. Mundana enim sapientia stultum 205Aputat Deum pro hominibus mori; huic ergo quicunque adhaeserit vivere non poterit.

(ORIG.) Aqua fluminis vertitur in sanguinem, cui Hebraeorum pueros tradiderant necandos, ut auctoribus sceleris poculum redderet sanguinis: et cruorem polluti gurgitis, quem parricidali caede maculaverant, potando sentirent. Allegorice quoque aquae Aegypti erratica et lubrica philosophorum dogmata sunt, quos parvulos sensu et intelligentia deceperunt: at ubi crux Christi lumen veritatis ostendit, necis suorum in poenas et reatum sanguinis exiguntur. Unde: Omnis sanguis qui effusus est super terram, requiretur a generatione hac (Matth. XXIII).

VERS. 20, 21.-- Quae versa est in sanguinem, etc. (RAB.) Primum praeceptum de colendo uno Deo: 205BNon erunt tibi dii alii praeter me: prima plaga, aqua in sanguinem versa. Compara primum praeceptum primae plagae: aqua, de qua generantur omnia, significat unum Deum, ex quo omnia; sanguis, carnem mortalem. Aqua ergo versa est in sanguinem, quia commutaverunt gloriam Dei incorruptibilis in similitudinem imaginis corruptibilis hominis, et haec quidem commutatio fit in corde impiorum.

In sanguinem, etc. Quia qui in rerum causis carnaliter sentiunt, hujusmodi correptionibus arguuntur, ut ex qualitate poenarum agnoscant suum errorem.

VERS. 22, 23.-- Et induratum est cor Pharaonis. (AUG. Quaest. 22.) Videtur propterea cor Pharaonis induratum fuisse, quia incantatores Aegyptiorum 205Csimilia fecerunt. Sed sequentia docent quanta fuerit illa obduratio, etiam cum incantatores defecerunt.

VERS. 24, 25.-- Foderunt autem, etc. Quia gentilitas confusa de meditatione saecularis philosophiae, cum videt nihil vitale nec salubre esse in illa, studet investigando circumquaque quaerens haustum sapientiae, nec invenit, donec perveniat ad eum qui ait: Qui sitit, veniat ad me et bibat (Joan. VII).

CHAPTER VII.

VERS. 1-6.-- And the Lord said to Moses: Behold I have appointed thee lord, etc. (AUG. Quaest. 17 in Exod., tom III.) It is to be noted that when he was sent to the people, it was not said to him: Behold I have given thee as God to the people, and thy brother shall be thy prophet; but, thy brother shall speak for thee to the people. It was said also shall be thy mouth, and thou to him in those things which are unto God; it was not said, thou to him God. But to Pharao Moses is said to be given as god, and by analogy Aaron is given 202Bas prophet to Moses, but towards Pharao. Here it is intimated to us that the prophets of God speak those things which they hear from him; and that a prophet of God is nothing else than one who announces the words of God to men, who either cannot or do not deserve to hear God.

(GREG. hom. 8 super Ezech.) Sometimes God is spoken of by appellation, sometimes essentially. By appellation, as here: I have appointed thee god of Pharao. And elsewhere: Bring him unto the gods. And elsewhere: God hath stood in the synagogue of gods, etc. Essentially, as here: I am the God of Abraham, the God of Isaac, and the God of Jacob, which Paul, wishing to distinguish, says: Of whom Christ was born according to the flesh, who is God blessed for ever. [Vulg. Rom 9:5 qui est super omnia Deus benedictus in saecula, "who is over all things, God blessed for ever"] For by appellation God is said to be among all things, but essentially above all things; that therefore he might show 202CChrist to be naturally God, he recorded him to be not only God, but God above all things. For any just man whatever is God, but among all things, because by appellation: but Christ is above all things, because he is naturally God.

(AUG. Quaest. 18 in Exod.) God says continually, etc. as far as I will harden, that is, I will show how hard it is.

VERS. 7, 8.-- Now Moses was eighty years old, etc. (STRAB.) On account of circumcision, namely, which was commanded to be celebrated on the eighth day: for the number eighty has the same signification as the number eight which is less than it. Now Moses was eighty years old. When Moses and Aaron spoke to Pharao, Moses was eighty years old, but Aaron 202Deighty-three years old: because the law offered by preachers to the Gentiles taught spiritual circumcision, and they themselves were zealous to be perfectly cleansed, and so they preached the faith of the holy Trinity.

VERS. 9.-- When Pharao shall say to you, etc. (AUG. Quaest. 19 et 20.) Here there was no need of a voice, for the slenderness of which Aaron seemed to have been given to Moses, but the rod was to be cast down that it might become a dragon. Why then did Moses not do this himself, save because the mediation of Aaron between Moses and Pharao bears the figure of a great mystery. This also is to be noted, that it is written: Aaron cast down his rod: perhaps if it had said, he cast down the rod, there would be no question; but that it adds his own, whereas Moses had given it, is perhaps not said in vain. Or 203Awas that rod common to them both, so that if it were called the rod of either of them, it would be truly called so?

(STRAB.) The rod of Moses signifies Christ, who took flesh from a royal stock. Whence that same rod, turned into a snake, devoured all the rods of the sorcerers, because the death of Christ consumed our death.

(ORIG. hom. 4 in Exod.) The Apostle says in I Corinthians XIV: Be emulous of spiritual gifts, but rather that ye may prophesy. Let us also try to take up the emulation of good things, but to await from the Lord the fulness of the gift. For for the one it is said in Psalm LXXX: Open thy mouth and I will fill it; [Vulg. Ps 80:11 Dilata os tuum, et implebo illud, "Open wide thy mouth, and I will fill it"] but for the other: Prick the eye, and it brings forth a tear; prick the heart, and it brings forth understanding. I think therefore that Moses coming to Egypt, and bearing the rod 203Bwith which he chastises and beats Egypt with ten plagues, is the law of God, by which it may reprove and amend the world with ten plagues, that is, with the ten commandments of the decalogue. The rod by which these things are done, and Egypt is subdued, and Pharao overcome, is the cross of Christ, through which the world and the prince of the world is overcome, that is, the devil, with the principalities and powers. The rod when cast down becomes a dragon or a serpent, and devours the serpents of the Egyptian magicians. But the serpent is wont to be set for wisdom or prudence; whence Matthew X: Be ye prudent as serpents, etc. And Genesis III: The serpent was more prudent than all the animals, [Vulg. Gen 3:1 serpens erat callidior cunctis animantibus terrae, "the serpent was more crafty than all the living things of the earth"] etc. The cross therefore of Christ, whose preaching seemed foolish, which Moses, that is, the law, contains (secundum illud Joan. V: De me enim ille scripsit ), after it was cast down upon the earth, that is, came to the faith of men, 203Cwas turned into wisdom, and into so great a wisdom as should devour all the wisdom of the Egyptians, that is, of this world. For behold how God made foolish the wisdom of this world, after he manifested Christ crucified to be the power and wisdom of God: and now the whole world has been laid hold of by him who said in I Corinthians III: I will take the wise in their own craftiness.

VERS. 10.-- And Aaron took, etc. (ISID. in Gen., tom. V.) And he took the rod before Pharao, etc., as far as: I will be thy death, O death, etc.

Which was turned into a snake. The law of Moses was as it were tasteless like a rod, but turned into a snake, that is, into spiritual understanding, it was made drinkable and 203Ddelightful.

VERS. 11.-- And Pharao called the wise men, etc. It is to be known that either by diabolical feignings they deluded the eyes of the beholders, so that things remaining in their own nature seemed to be something else; or, according to Augustine, the demons, although evil, have not lost their own nature: therefore while by their incantation the sorcerers attempt to effect something, they run through the world, and suddenly bring the seeds of those things of which the matter is in hand. And so from these, God permitting, they produce new species of things.

Similarly. In the first plague he says, when the water is turned into blood; and in the second, when the frogs boil forth; and in the third, when the gnats assail; in the fourth, when 204Athe dog-fly is brought forth; in the fifth, when the hand of the Lord is upon the cattle of the Egyptians, they are said to be like things.

VERS. 12.-- Dragons. They are so called Apo tou δέρκειν, that is, to see, because they see more sharply than other animals.

But it devoured, etc. (AUG. Quaest. 21.) If it had been said, The dragon of Aaron swallowed up their rods, it would be understood that the true dragon of Aaron had not swallowed up the phantom feignings, but the rods, etc., as far as but God alone is the true creator, who has implanted in things their very causes and seminal reasons.

VERS. 13, 14.-- And it was hardened, etc. God hardened the heart of Pharao, because he so hardened the devil after his sin that he is never softened by the compunction of repentance, as is said in Job XLI: His heart shall be hardened as a stone.

204BVERS. 15, 16.-- Go to him in the morning, behold he will go forth, etc. (ORIG. hom. 4.) First indeed Moses does not enter in to Pharao, but meets him as he goes out to the waters; but afterwards he enters in; after these things he neither enters in, but being summoned he approaches: because whether our contest with Pharao is in the word of God and in the assertion of religion, or whether we strive to snatch from him the souls he besets, and there is in us a wrestling in disputation, we ought not at the first onset to go in to the uttermost places of the questions, but we must meet the adversary, and at his own waters, that is, the authorities of the philosophers of the Gentiles, that we may first convict them and show that they have erred; then we must go in to the inner parts of the contest. For the Lord says: Unless a man first bind the strong one, he cannot 204Center into his house and plunder his vessels (Marc. XII). First therefore the strong one must be bound and constrained with the bonds of questions, and so we must enter in to plunder the vessels, and to free the souls which he had possessed by deception and fraud. And when we shall have done this often, and shall have stood against him, girt namely as to our loins with truth, and standing in the Lord, and acting manfully, the ancient and crafty artificer will feign himself conquered and to give way, that he may make us more negligent for the contest. He will feign repentance also, he will entreat us to depart, but not far off: he wishes us to be near to him in some part. But unless we withdraw farther from him, and cross the sea, and say in thought, in speech, and in deed: As far as the east is distant from the west, so far hath he removed our iniquities from us, 204Dwe cannot be saved. Let us therefore pray for the mercy of God, that he may snatch us also out of the land of Egypt, out of the power of darkness, and may sink Pharao with his army like lead in the most violent water. But let us, being set free, sing a hymn to the Lord with joy and exultation. For he is gloriously honoured, etc.

(ISID.) The plagues were wrought bodily in Egypt, but in us they are wrought spiritually. For Egypt is the figure of the world.

VERS. 17-19.-- The water of the river, etc. (STRAB.) The water turned into blood signifies worldly wisdom, in which everyone who has remained is slain, as a fish in blood. For worldly wisdom thinks it foolish 205Athat God should die for men; whosoever therefore shall have clung to it will not be able to live.

(ORIG.) The water of the river is turned into blood, to which they had delivered the children of the Hebrews to be slain, that it might render to the authors of the crime a cup of blood: and that by drinking they might feel the gore of the polluted flood which they had stained with parricidal slaughter. Allegorically also the waters of Egypt are the erring and slippery teachings of the philosophers, who deceived those little in sense and understanding: but when the cross of Christ showed the light of truth, they are exacted for the punishments of the slaying of their own and for the guilt of blood. Whence: All the blood which hath been shed upon the earth shall be required of this generation (Matth. XXIII).

VERS. 20, 21.-- Which was turned into blood, etc. (RAB.) The first precept is concerning the worship of one God: 205BThou shalt not have other gods before me [Vulg. Ex 20:3 reads Non habebis deos alienos coram me; Migne prints Non erunt tibi dii alii praeter me, the form Origen expounds, and the gloss's enumeration turns on it] : the first plague, the water turned into blood. Compare the first precept with the first plague: water, from which all things are generated, signifies the one God, from whom are all things; blood, mortal flesh. The water therefore was turned into blood, because they changed the glory of the incorruptible God into the likeness of the image of corruptible man, and this change indeed takes place in the heart of the impious.

Into blood, etc. Because they who feel carnally about the causes of things are reproved by corrections of this sort, that from the quality of the punishments they may recognize their own error.

VERS. 22, 23.-- And the heart of Pharao was hardened. (AUG. Quaest. 22.) It seems that the heart of Pharao was hardened for this cause, that the enchanters of the Egyptians 205Cdid like things. But what follows teaches how great that hardening was, even when the enchanters failed.

VERS. 24, 25.-- And they dug, etc. Because the Gentile world, confounded by the meditation of secular philosophy, when it sees that there is nothing living nor wholesome in it, is zealous by searching to seek on every side a draught of wisdom, and finds it not, until it comes to him who says: He that thirsteth, let him come to me and drink (Joan. VII).

CAPUT VIII.

VERS. 2-6.-- Ecce ego percutiam omnes terminos tuos ranis, etc. (STRAB.) Tria sunt genera ranarum: unum fluviale et vocale, alterum ejus quae calamita dicitur, de qua fertur quod si in os latrantis canis 205Dprojicitur, statim obmutescit; tertium ejus quae rubeta dicitur, eo quod in vepribus agat.

(ORIG.) Ranae significant carmina poetarum, quae inani et inflata modulatione, velut ranarum sonis et cantibus, mundo deceptionis fabulas intulerunt: ad nihil enim animal illud utile est, nisi quod sonum vocis improbis et importunis clamoribus reddit.

(RAB.) Secundum praeceptum: Non accipies nomen Dei tui in vanum. Nomen Dei veritas est: ipse enim ait: Ego sum veritas. Qui loquitur veritatem, de Deo loquitur; qui enim loquitur mendacium, de suo loquitur. Veritatem enim loqui est rationabiliter loqui: vanitatem loqui, strepere est. Secundum ergo 206Apraeceptum est de dilectione veritatis, cui contraria est dilectio iniquitatis. Loquitur veritas, perstrepit vanitas. Huic contraria est secunda plaga, ranarum abundantia, quarum loquacitas vanitatem significat. Amatores veritatis non accipiunt nomen Dei in vanum, quia a veritate in vanitatem non recedunt. Qui autem veritati contradicunt, et vanitate decepti decipiunt, ranae sunt, taedium inferentes auribus, non cibum mentibus. Audi homines rationabiliter loquentes psal. XVIII: Non sunt loquelae neque sermones quorum non audiantur voces eorum. Audi ranas: Vana locuti sunt unusquisque ad proximum suum, etc.

VERS. 7-9.-- Fecerunt autem malefici, etc. (AUG., quaest. 23.) Quaeritur unde, si jam ubique 206Bfactum erat. Sed similis quaestio est, unde aquam in sanguinem verterint, si jam tota aqua Aegypti in sanguinem versa fuerat? Sed intelligendum est regionem ubi filii Israel habitabant, talibus plagis non fuisse percussam, et inde potuerunt incantatores haurire aquam, quam in sanguinem verterent, vel ranas educere ad ostentationem magicae potentiae; quanquam potuerunt etiam, postquam illa compressa sunt, facere: sed Scriptura narrando conjunxit quod postea fieri potuit.

VERS. 10-14.-- Qui respondit: Cras. (THEOD.) Pharao cum scire vellet an Moysi et Aaron preces aliquid apud Deum valerent (illos enim per noctem quiescere existimabat), ait: Cras, ut ipsemet ad fluvium profectus, videret an ranae cessassent. 206CQuamobrem Deus, ut Mosi crederetur, non tantum eo ipso tempore quo Moses constituerat, ranas cessare fecit, sed et eas quae in terra erant interire jussit, ut ne easdem rediisse existimarent.

VERS. 15.-- Videns autem Pharao, etc. (AUG. Quaest. 24.) Hic apparet non ideo tantum Pharaonem obduratum quod incantatores similia fecerunt, etc., usque ad non dixit munera, vel praemia, sed judicia tua.

VERS. 16, 17.-- Extende virgam, etc. Mystice. Moses virga castigans Aegyptum legem significat, quae mundum decem plagis mandatorum corripit. Virga quoque per quam Aegyptus corrigitur, et Pharao superatur, crux Christi est, per quam mundus vincitur et diabolus superatur vel triumphatur. Virga projecta fit serpens, qui sapientiam significat, secundum 206Dillud Matth. X: Estote prudentes sicut serpentes, etc. Virga serpentes magorum devoravit, et crux Christi, cujus praedicatio videtur stultitia, omnem sapientiam mundi superat. Unde: De me enim ille scripsit (Joan. V). Virga in terram projecta versa est in serpentem, quia cum mysterium crucis ad fidem hominum venit, sapientiam mundi stultam fecit.

Et sint sciniphes, etc. (ORIG.) Hoc animal pennis suspenditur per aera volitans, sed ita subtile et minutum, ut oculi visum, nisi acute cernentis, effugiat. Corpus tamen cum insederit, acerbissimo terebrat stimulo, ut quem volantem videre quis non valet, sentiat stimulantem. Hoc ergo animal arti dialecticae 207Acomparatur, quae minutis et subtilibus verborum stimulis animas terebrat, et tanta calliditate circum venit, ut deceptus nec videat nec intelligat unde decipiatur.

(RAB.) Moraliter. Tertium praeceptum: Memento diem sabbati sanctificare. Quo insinuatur vacatio et tranquillitas mentis, quam bona conscientia facit. Ibi sanctificatio, quia ibi Spiritus Dei. Unde: Super quem requiescet Spiritus meus nisi super humilem et quietum? etc. (Isa. LXVI). Inquieti ergo a Spiritu sancto resiliunt, provocatores rixarum, amatores calumniarum, nec admittunt quietem sabbati spiritualis. Esto ergo mansuetus, non sit tumultus in corde tuo, volitantibus per corruptionem phantasmatibus et pungentibus te. Audi Deum dicentem psal. XLV: Vacate et 207Bvidete quoniam ego sum Deus. Tu per inquietudinem vacare non vis, et caecatus exigis videre quod non potes. Attende enim contrariam plagam: sciniphes nati sunt in terra Aegypti de limo, muscae scilicet minutissimae inquietissimae, inordinate volantes, in oculos ruentes, et negantes hominibus requiem; dum abiguntur, redeunt; sic phantasmata inquietorum. Praeceptum tene, plagam cave.

VERS. 18.-- Erantque sciniphes. Sanguis est doctrina philosophorum: ranae, figmenta poetarum; sciniphes, sophismata dialecticorum: quae omnia mundum deceperunt.

VERS. 19.-- Digitus Dei est hic, etc. (ISID. ex Aug. ep. 119.) In tertio signo magi deficiunt, dicentes: Digitus Dei est: qui typum haereticorum et animositatem 207Chabuerunt. Unde: Sicut Jamnes et Mambres restiterunt Moysi, ita et hi resistunt veritati, homines mente corrupti, reprobi circa fidem, etc. (II Tim. III). Hi autem qui per mentis corruptionem inquieti fuerunt, in tertio signo defecerunt, fatentes sibi adversum Spiritum sanctum, qui erat in Mose. Tertio enim ponitur loco Spiritus, qui est digitus Dei: qui sicut placatus requiem praestat mitibus et humilibus corde, ita iratus immites et superbos per inquietudinem exagitat, quam muscae gravissimae significaverunt, sub quibus magi defecerunt, dicentes: Digitus Dei est hic, ne intelligant unde decipiantur.

(STRAB.) Notandum quia tertio signo victi sunt magi, quia omnis perfidia et mundana sapientia, vel philosophia, fide Trinitatis vincitur. Cum enim 207Dphilosophiam invenerint, et de machina totius mundi disputaverint, ad cognitionem Trinitatis pervenire nequiverunt. Per digitum Spiritus sanctus intelligitur, non quod minor sit Patre et Filio, sed quia in digito plurimae divisiones sunt, et per Spiritum sanctum datur divisio gratiarum.

(AUG., quaest. 25 in Exod., tom. III.) Quod dixerunt magi ad Pharaonem, etc., usque ad, quamvis de Filio non tacuerunt.

VERS. 20.-- Haec dicit Dominus, etc. (RAB.) Quartum praeceptum: Honora patrem tuum et matrem tuam. Huic contraria est quarta plaga, scilicet cynomyia, id est, canina musca: caninum est parentes non agnoscere; unde catuli eorum caeci nascuntur.

208AVERS. 21.-- Omne genus muscarum. (ISID.) Mystice. Quarto loco Aegyptus muscis percutitur. Musca autem insolens et inquietum animal est, in qua insolentes curae carnalium desideriorum figurantur. Aegyptus vero muscis percutitur, quia corda eorum qui saeculum diligunt, desideriorum inquietudinibus feriuntur. LXX: cynomyiam, id est, muscam caninam, posuerunt: per quam canini mores significantur, in quibus humanae mentis voluptas, et libido carnis arguitur. Potest etiam forensis eloquentia hominum significari per muscam caninam, qui velut canes alterutrum se lacerant.

(PROCOP.) Deus non magnis belluis utitur in expugnanda Aegypto, sed contemptissimarum bestiolarum et insectorum ministerium accersit: ne scilicet 208Billarum potentiae ascriberetur effectus. Abjectis illis et infirmis bestiolis tribuit vires inexpugnabiles, adeo ut desperet Aegyptus se posse illis bestiolis resistere, et clamitet: Digitus Dei hic est: Nam manum Dei non feret universus mundus conglobatus.

(ORIG.) Cynomyia haereticorum [ Al., Cynicorum] secta, qui ad reliquas deceptionis suae improbitates, voluptatem et libidinem summum praedicant bonum. Quoniam igitur per haec singula prius deceptus est mundus, merito adveniens sermo et lex Dei hujusmodi correptionibus arguit, ut ex qualitate poenarum qualitates proprii cognoscant erroris.

VERS. 22, 23.-- Faciamque mirabilem, etc. (AUG., quaest. 26.) Hoc in prioribus signis intelligendum est 208Cfactum esse, ut terra in qua habitabat populus Dei nullis talibus plagis vexaretur. Opportunum autem fuit ut ibi hoc aperte poneretur; unde jam incipiunt signa, quibus magi similia non conati sunt facere. Pro certo est enim quia ubique fuerant sciniphes in regno Pharaonis, non autem fuerant in terra Gessen vel Gesen: ibi conati sunt magi similia facere, nec potuerunt: quousque ergo deficerent, nihil de illius terrae separatione dictum est, sed ex quo coeperunt ea fieri quae illi nec auderent conari.

VERS. 24, 25.-- Et venit musca gravissima, etc. (AUG., ibid.) Musca pro multitudine muscarum posita. Nescio enim quomodo per loquendi consuetudinem imbutis sensibus hominum: saepe plus videtur quod singulariter, quam quod pluraliter, dicitur; 208Dverbi gratia: Est illic miles, quam sunt milites illic; est illic piscis, plus significat quam sunt illic pisces.

VERS. 26-31.-- Abominationes. Perversi abominantur bona Aegyptii, id est, cives hujus saeculi abominantur oves, id est, simplicitatem bonorum. Sicut enim boni abominantur mala, ita mali bona. Unde abominantur boni virum impium, et impii eos qui in recta sunt via.

(AUG. Quaest. 28.) In eremum dicebat Moyses se velle ire ubi Aegyptii non viderent abominationes suas. Hoc autem intelligendum est mystice significari, quod de pastoribus diximus, qui erant Aegyptiis abominabiles: et ideo separatam terram Israelitae 209Areceperunt in Aegypto. Sic enim sacrificia Israelitarum abominationes sunt Aegyptiis, sicut iniquis vita justorum.

(STRAB.) Historice. Aegyptii abominantur oves et eas adorant propter Jovem Ammonem, qui cum capite arietino pingitur. Item: bovem colunt Aegyptii, qui consecratus est Api, sicut vacca Isidi. Apis enim erat maximum numen Aegyptiorum, qui semel in anno certo tempore apparebat sacerdotibus eorum. Illi vero cum hymnis et laudibus eum prosequentes, quasi lymphatici prophetabant.

(GREG., lib. XVIII Moral., c. 28, tom. II.) Aegyptus muscis percutitur, etc., usque ad Vacate et videte, quoniam ego sum Deus.

VERS. 32.-- Et ingravatum est, etc. (AUG., quaest. 209B29.) Cum ablata esset locusta, dictum est de Pharaone: Ingravavit Pharao cor suum etiam in hoc tempore, et noluit dimittere populum. Certe nunc non dictum est, Ingravatum est cor Pharaonis, sed ingravavit Pharao cor suum. Sic utique in omnibus plagis. A voluntate hominis est origo vitiorum. Moventur autem causis corda hominum, alia sic, alia vero sic, etiam non diversis causis, saepe diverso modo, secundum proprias qualitates, quae ex voluntatibus veniunt.

CHAPTER VIII.

VERS. 2-6.-- Behold, I will strike all thy borders with frogs, etc. (STRAB.) There are three kinds of frogs: one of the river and vocal; another of that which is called the calamite, of which it is reported that if it be thrown into the mouth of a barking 205Ddog, the dog at once falls dumb; a third of that which is called the bramble-frog, because it lives among briars.

(ORIG.) Frogs signify the songs of the poets, which by an empty and inflated modulation, like the sounds and singing of frogs, have brought the fables of deception into the world; for that creature is good for nothing, save that it gives back the sound of its voice in shameless and importunate clamours.

(RAB.) The second precept: Thou shalt not take the name of thy God in vain. [Vulg. Ex 20:7 Non assumes nomen Domini Dei tui in vanum, "Thou shalt not take the name of the Lord thy God in vain"] The name of God is truth, for he himself says: I am the truth. He who speaks truth, speaks of God; for he who speaks a lie, speaks of his own. For to speak truth is to speak rationally; to speak vanity is to make a din. The second 206Aprecept, therefore, is concerning the love of truth, to which the love of iniquity is contrary. Truth speaks, vanity clamours. Contrary to this is the second plague, the abundance of frogs, whose loquacity signifies vanity. The lovers of truth do not take the name of God in vain, because they do not withdraw from truth into vanity. But those who contradict the truth, and, deceived by vanity, deceive others, are frogs, bringing weariness to the ears, not food to minds. Hear men speaking rationally, psalm XVIII: There are no speeches nor languages whose voices are not heard. Hear the frogs: They have spoken vain things, every one to his neighbour, etc.

VERS. 7-9.-- And the magicians did so, etc. (AUG., quaest. 23.) It is asked whence they did it, if it had already 206Bbeen done everywhere. But there is a like question: whence did they turn water into blood, if all the water of Egypt had already been turned into blood? But it is to be understood that the region where the children of Israel dwelt had not been struck with such plagues, and thence the enchanters were able to draw water which they might turn into blood, or to bring forth frogs for the display of magical power; although they were also able to do it after those things had been checked. But Scripture in narrating joined together what could have been done afterwards.

VERS. 10-14.-- He answered: To-morrow. (THEOD.) Pharaoh, wishing to know whether the prayers of Moses and Aaron had any power with God (for he supposed that they rested through the night), said: To-morrow, that he himself, having gone out to the river, might see whether the frogs had ceased. 206CWherefore God, that Moses might be believed, not only made the frogs cease at that very time which Moses had appointed, but also commanded those which were upon the land to perish, lest they should think that the same had returned.

VERS. 15.-- But Pharaoh seeing, etc. (AUG. Quaest. 24.) Here it appears that Pharaoh was not hardened only because the enchanters did the like, etc., as far as he did not say gifts, or rewards, but thy judgments.

VERS. 16, 17.-- Stretch forth thy rod, etc. Mystically. Moses chastising Egypt with the rod signifies the law, which rebukes the world with the ten plagues of the commandments. The rod also, by which Egypt is corrected and Pharaoh overcome, is the cross of Christ, by which the world is conquered and the devil overcome, or triumphed over. The rod cast down becomes a serpent, which signifies wisdom, according to 206Dthat of Matthew X: Be ye wise as serpents, etc. The rod devoured the serpents of the magicians, and the cross of Christ, whose preaching seems foolishness, overcomes all the wisdom of the world. Whence: For he wrote of me (Joan. V). The rod cast upon the earth was turned into a serpent, because when the mystery of the cross came to the faith of men, it made the wisdom of the world foolish.

And let there be sciniphs, etc. (ORIG.) This creature is upheld by wings, flitting through the air, but so fine and minute that it escapes the sight of the eye, unless of one who sees keenly. Yet when it has settled upon the body, it bores with a most bitter sting, so that he who is not able to see it flying feels it stinging. This creature therefore is compared to the art of dialectic, 207Awhich bores souls with minute and subtle stings of words, and encompasses them with such craft that the man deceived neither sees nor understands whence he is deceived.

(RAB.) Morally. The third precept: Remember to sanctify the day of the sabbath. [Vulg. Ex 20:8 Memento ut diem sabbati sanctifices, "Remember that thou keep holy the sabbath day"] By which is intimated the leisure and tranquillity of mind which a good conscience makes. There is sanctification, because there is the Spirit of God. Whence: Upon whom shall my Spirit rest, save upon the humble and the quiet? etc. (Isa. LXVI). [Vulg. Is 66:2 ad quem autem respiciam, nisi ad pauperculum, et contritum spiritu, et trementem sermones meos, "but to whom shall I have respect, but to him that is poor and little, and of a contrite spirit, and that trembleth at my words"] The restless, therefore, spring back from the Holy Spirit, provokers of quarrels, lovers of calumnies, nor do they admit the rest of the spiritual sabbath. Be thou therefore meek; let there be no tumult in thy heart, with phantasms flitting through corruption and stinging thee. Hear God saying in psalm XLV: Be still and 207Bsee that I am God. Thou through restlessness wilt not be still, and, blinded, thou demandest to see what thou canst not. For mark the contrary plague: the sciniphs were born in the land of Egypt out of the slime, flies namely most minute and most restless, flying without order, rushing into the eyes, and denying rest to men; while they are driven off, they return: so are the phantasms of the restless. Hold the precept, beware the plague.

VERS. 18.-- And there were sciniphs. Blood is the doctrine of the philosophers; frogs, the fictions of the poets; sciniphs, the sophisms of the dialecticians: all which have deceived the world.

VERS. 19.-- This is the finger of God, etc. (ISID. ex Aug. ep. 119.) At the third sign the magicians fail, saying: It is the finger of God: who had the type of heretics and their animosity. 207CWhence: As Jamnes and Mambres resisted Moses, so these also resist the truth, men corrupted in mind, reprobate concerning the faith, etc. (II Tim. III). But these, who through corruption of mind were restless, failed at the third sign, confessing that the Holy Spirit, who was in Moses, was against them. For the Spirit is set in the third place, who is the finger of God: and he, as when appeased he affords rest to the meek and humble of heart, so when angered he harries the unmeek and the proud with restlessness, which the most grievous flies signified, under which the magicians failed, saying: This is the finger of God, that they might not understand whence they were deceived.

(STRAB.) It is to be noted that the magicians were overcome at the third sign, because all perfidy and worldly wisdom, or philosophy, is overcome by faith in the Trinity. For although 207Dthey discovered philosophy and disputed concerning the fabric of the whole world, they were not able to attain to the knowledge of the Trinity. By the finger the Holy Spirit is understood, not that he is less than the Father and the Son, but because in a finger there are very many divisions, and through the Holy Spirit the division of graces is given.
