(AUG., quaest. 25 in Exod., tom. III.) What the magicians said to Pharaoh, etc., as far as, although they were not silent concerning the Son.

VERS. 20.-- Thus saith the Lord, etc. (RAB.) The fourth precept: Honour thy father and thy mother. Contrary to this is the fourth plague, namely the cynomyia, that is, the dog-fly: it is doglike not to acknowledge one's parents; whence their whelps are born blind.

208AVERS. 21.-- Every kind of flies. (ISID.) Mystically. In the fourth place Egypt is struck with flies. Now the fly is an insolent and restless creature, in which are figured the insolent cares of carnal desires. But Egypt is struck with flies, because the hearts of those who love the world are smitten with the restlessnesses of desires. The Seventy set down cynomyia, that is, the dog-fly: by which doglike manners are signified, in which the pleasure of the human mind and the lust of the flesh are reproved. The forensic eloquence of men also can be signified by the dog-fly, men who like dogs tear one another.

(PROCOP.) God does not use great beasts in the storming of Egypt, but summons the ministry of the most contemptible little creatures and insects: namely, lest 208Bthe effect should be ascribed to the power of those beasts. To those abject and feeble little creatures he granted unconquerable strength, so far that Egypt despaired of being able to resist those little creatures, and cried out: This is the finger of God: For the whole world massed together will not bear the hand of God.

(ORIG.) The dog-fly is the sect of the heretics [ Al., Cynicorum], who, upon the remaining shamelessnesses of their deception, preach pleasure and lust as the highest good. Since therefore the world was first deceived by each of these, deservedly the word and law of God, at its coming, reproves with rebukes of this kind, that from the quality of the punishments they may know the qualities of their own error.

VERS. 22, 23.-- And I will make wonderful, etc. (AUG., quaest. 26.) This is to be understood to have been 208Cdone in the earlier signs also, that the land in which the people of God dwelt was vexed by no such plagues. But it was opportune that this should be set down openly there; whence now begin the signs which the magicians did not attempt to do the like of. For it is certain that the sciniphs had been everywhere in the kingdom of Pharaoh, but had not been in the land of Gessen or Gesen: there the magicians attempted to do the like, and were not able: until therefore they should fail, nothing was said concerning the separation of that land, but from the time when those things began to be done which they would not even dare to attempt.

VERS. 24, 25.-- And there came a most grievous fly, etc. (AUG., ibid.) The fly is put for the multitude of flies. For I know not how it is that, to the senses of men steeped in the custom of speaking, often more seems to be said by what is said in the singular than by what is said in the plural; 208Dfor example: There is a soldier there, than There are soldiers there; There is a fish there signifies more than There are fishes there.

VERS. 26-31.-- Abominations. The perverse Egyptians abominate good things, that is, the citizens of this world abominate sheep, that is, the simplicity of the good. For as the good abominate evils, so the evil abominate goods. Whence the good abominate the ungodly man, and the ungodly those who are in the right way.

(AUG. Quaest. 28.) Moses said that he wished to go into the desert where the Egyptians would not see their abominations. But this is to be understood as mystically signifying what we have said of the shepherds, who were abominable to the Egyptians; and therefore the Israelites 209Areceived a separate land in Egypt. For thus the sacrifices of the Israelites are abominations to the Egyptians, as the life of the just is to the wicked.

(STRAB.) Historically. The Egyptians abominate sheep and adore them because of Jupiter Ammon, who is painted with a ram's head. Likewise: the Egyptians worship the ox, which is consecrated to Apis, as the cow to Isis. For Apis was the greatest deity of the Egyptians, who once in the year at a fixed time appeared to their priests. And they, attending him with hymns and praises, prophesied like men frenzied.

(GREG., lib. XVIII Moral., c. 28, tom. II.) Egypt is struck with flies, etc., as far as Be still and see, that I am God.

VERS. 32.-- And it was made heavy, etc. (AUG., quaest. 209B29.) When the locust had been taken away, it was said of Pharaoh: Pharaoh made his own heart heavy at this time also, and would not let the people go. Certainly it is not said now, The heart of Pharaoh was made heavy, but Pharaoh made his own heart heavy. So assuredly in all the plagues. From the will of man is the origin of vices. But the hearts of men are moved by causes, some in this way, others in that, even by causes not diverse, often in a diverse manner, according to their own qualities, which come from their wills.

CAPUT IX.

VERS. 1-6.-- Dixit autem Dominus ad Moysen, etc. (PROCOP.) Primi inter jumenta computantur equi, qui sunt mendaces seu falsi ad salutem. Deinde jumenta 209Cimmunda, quae nec ungulas findunt, nec ruminant, currui tamen junguntur. Deinceps cameli, qui quamvis propter ruminationem quasi mundi reputentur, tamen quia eorum ungula non sulcatur, censentur immundi. Verumtamen partim mundae partim immundae conditionis cameli perhibentur. Caeterum quia haec plaga, ut parum noxia propter vilitatem immundorum animalium credebatur, transit ad boves, dehinc ad placidissimum animalium, ovem.

(ORIG., hom. 4.) Quinto loco animalium nece vel pecudum Aegyptus verberatur. In quo vecordia vel stultitia mortalium arguitur, qui tanquam irrationabilia pecora cultum et vocabulum Dei imposuerunt figuris, non solum hominum sed pecudum ligno et lapidibus impressis: Ammonem Jovem in ariete 209Dvenerantes, Anubim in cane, Apim in tauro, et caetera portenta deorum, quae Aegyptus miratur, ut quibus cultum credebant esse divinum, in his viderent miserandum supplicium.

(RAB. in Exod., tom. II.) Quintum praeceptum est: Non moechaberis, etc., usque ad attende plagam: si pecus es, saltem mori time.

VERS. 7.-- Ingravatumque est, etc. (AUG., quaest. 30.) Si pecora Israelitarum morerentur, videretur causa competens quare cor ejus gravaretur ad contemptum Dei, tanquam si magi ejus pecora Israelitarum occidissent; sed unde debuit ad timendum vel credendum moveri, ingravatum est, et illa ingravatio etiam hucusque progressa est.

210AVERS. 8.-- Tollite plenas, etc. (AUG., quaest. 31.) Alia littera: Sumite plenas manus vobis favillae de fornace, et asperget Moyses, etc. Superiora signa virga fiebant, quam non Moyses, sed Aaron extendebat super aquam, vel ad terram percutiebat. Nunc vero interpositis duobus signis, de cynomyia scilicet et mortibus pecorum, ubi nec Aaron nec Moyses aliquid manu operantur, dicitur ut Moyses favillam spargat in coelum; ambo jubentur sumere, sed ille spargere non in terram sed in coelum: tanquam Aaron (qui datus erat ad populum) terram percutere deberet, vel in aquam sive in terram manum extendere; Moyses vero, qui erat in his quae ad Dominum, favillam jubetur spargere. Quid duo illa superiora signa, ubi nec Moyses nec Aaron aliquid 210Bmanu operantur? Quid sibi vult ipsa diversitas? Neque enim nihil.

(RAB.) Sextum praeceptum: Non occides; sexta plaga, pustulae in corpore, vesicae bullientes et scatentes, incendia vulnerum et favillae fornacis. Ardent enim homicidae ira, insania. Si posses videre animas homicidarum, plus plangeres eas quam putrescentia corpora ulceratorum.

VERS. 9-12.-- (ORIG., ibid.) Vulnera et vesicae cum fervore in sexto verbere producuntur. In ulceribus dolosa purulentaque malitia arguitur; in vesicis tumens et inflata superbia: in fervore, ira et furoris insania.

VERS. 13-15.-- Induravitque Dominus cor, etc. (ORIG., ibid.) Nota quia in sexta plaga non dicitur, 210CInduratum est cor Pharaonis, sed aliquid terribilius additur; scriptum est enim quia induravit Dominus cor Pharaonis, etc. Rursus in septima cum grando et ignis Aegyptum vastat, induratur quidem cor Pharaonis, sed non a Domino. In octava vero, ubi locusta producitur, dicitur Dominus indurare cor Pharaonis. Et in nona cum tenebrae palpabiles fiunt, tandem vero cum, exstinctis primogenitis, Israelitae discessissent, ita dicitur: Et induravit Dominus cor Pharaonis regis Aegypti et servorum ejus, et insecutus est eos. Sed et cum Moyses de terra Madian ad Aegyptum mittitur et praecipitur ei facere prodigia, additur: Facies ea in conspectu Pharaonis: Ego autem indurabo cor Pharaonis, etc. Hinc primum 210Ddictum est a Domino: Ego indurabo cor Pharaonis, etc. Secundo quoque, enumeratis principibus, Israel dicitur: Ego indurabo cor Pharaonis, etc. Non frustra facta est ista varietas, ut nunc dicatur Dominus indurasse cor Pharaonis, nunc sponte esse induratum. Video Paulum tanquam intelligentem quid differat, induratum esse cor Pharaonis, et indurasse Dominum cor Pharaonis dicere Rom. II: An divitias bonitatis ejus, et patientiae et longanimitatis contemnis? Ignoras quoniam benignitas Dei ad poenitentiam te adducit, etc. Hic sine dubio eum qui sponte induratus est, culpat. Alibi vero, velut quaestionem super hoc proponens, dicit: Ergo cui vult miseretur, et quem vult indurat, etc. Addit etiam his: O homo, tu qui es qui respondeas Deo (Rom. IX). Per quod de 211Aeo qui a Domino dicitur induratus, non tam quaestionis solutione, quam apostolica auctoritate respondit, pro incapacitate auditorum, sicut alibi ait (II Cor. XII): Et audivit arcana verba, quae non licet homini loqui. Unde in sequentibus eum qui non tam studiorum merito quam sciendi cupiditate secretioribus se quaestionibus curiosius [curiosus] immergit, deterret dicens Rom. IX: O homo, quis es qui respondeas Deo.

(RAB.) Septimum praeceptum: Non furaberis; septima plaga, grando in fructibus. Nemo enim habet injustum lucrum sine justo damno. Dum furaris, acquiris vestem, amittis fidem: lucrum visibile, damnum invisibile: lucrum de tua caecitate, damnum de Domini nube. Qui enim malo desiderio forinsecus 211Bfurantur, de judicio intrinsecus grandinantur, et ager cordis eorum devastatur.

VERS. 16, 17.-- Idcirco autem posui te, etc. (AUG., quaest. 32.) Haec verba Apostolus posuit, etc., usque ad ad eorum utilitatem ergo Pharao est servatus.

VERS. 18.-- En pluam cras, etc. (ORIG.) Hucusque per errorum suorum figuras mundo supplicia temperantur: post haec veniunt verbera de supernis.

VERS. 19-21.-- Mitte ergo jam nunc, etc. (AUG., quaest. 35 in Exod., tom. III.) Hoc non tam indignanter quam misericorditer praemonere videtur Deus, etc., usque ad quoniam mortem pecorum minatus est Deus, quamvis id Scriptura tacuerit.

(PROCOP.) Caeterum providentia divina factum est 211Cne jumenta interirent omnia. Nam opus erat jumentis quae traherent currus quibus Pharao postmodum insecuturus erat fugientes, ut eos in solitudine trucidaret. Verum divina providentia aliter totum gubernavit negotium.

VERS. 22.-- Extende manum, etc. (PROCOP.) Admonendus est hoc loco lector, Mosem non juberi omnia miranda facere virga, sed saltem quaedam: quaedam quoque praedicere, ut culices et jumentorum interitum: quaedam manuum ministerio peragere, ut dum accipit fuliginem de camino et grandinem concitat. Sed quae horum est causa? Ne vulgus opinetur magica superstitione virgam esse factam, et ipsi mirificos illos effectus assignarent.

VERS. 23-29.-- Extenditque Moyses virgam, etc. 211D(ORIG., ubi supra. ) Illud quoque notandum, quod quaedam verbera inferre dicitur Aaron Pharaoni vel Aegypto, quaedam Moses, quaedam vero ipse Dominus. In prima enim plaga, ubi aquas vertit in sanguinem, elevat Aaron virgam et percutit aquam. In secunda percutit aquas et educit ranas. In tertia extendit manum et percutit virga pulverem terrae et fiunt sciniphes. In quarta dicitur Dominus fecisse, ut veniret cynomyia. In quinta quoque cum exstinguuntur pecora, dicitur fecisse verbum hoc. In sexta vero Moses aspergit favillam, et fiunt ulcera et vesicae. In septima elevat manum in coelum et fiunt voces et grando scilicet, etc. In octava similiter extendit manus in coelum, et Dominus inducit ventum, et adducit 212Alocustas. Sed et in nona extendit manum in coelum, et fiunt tenebrae et caligo. In decima vero finis et perfectio totius operis ministratur a Domino. Sicut enim dicitur: Et Dominus percussit omne primogenitum in terra Aegypti.

(IDEM.) Est alia observanda differentia: in prima enim plaga cum aqua vertitur in sanguinem, nondum dicitur Mosi ut intraret ad Pharaonem, sed, vade in occursum ejus, etc. In secunda vero, constanter et fideliter ministrata prima, dicitur ad eum: Intra ad Pharaonem, etc. In tertia vero sciniphes inferuntur, magi cedunt confitentes, quia digitus Dei est hic. In quarta vero vigilare jubetur Moses mane, et stare contra Pharaonem procedentem ad aquas, cum cynomyia replentur Aegyptiorum domus. 212BIn quinta cum pecora delentur, ad Pharaonem intrare jubetur. In sexta contemnitur Pharao, nec dicitur quod intraverint Moses et Aaron ad eum, quia facta sunt ulcera et vesicae cum fervore etiam super maleficos, et non poterant resistere Mosi. In septima vigilare jubetur valde mane, et stare contra Pharaonem, cum grandinem et ignem producit et voces. In octava intrare praecipitur ad eum cum locusta producitur. In nona rursus contemnitur Pharao, et Mosi [Moses] praecipitur ad coelum extendere manus, ut fiant tenebrae palpabiles: nec intrat, sed vocatur ad Pharaonem. Similiter in decima, cum primitiva delentur, et cogitur cum festinatione exire de Aegypto.

Et Dominus dedit tonitrua, etc. (ID., homil., 4.) 212CVide temperamentum divinae correctionis: non cum silentio verberat, sed dat voces, et doctrinam coelitus mittit, per quam possit mundus castigatus culpam suam agnoscere. Dat grandinem per quam tenera vastantur adhuc nascentia vitiorum. Dat et ignem, sciens esse spinas et tribulos quos debebat depasci, de quo dicit Dominus Luc. XII: Ignem veni mittere in terra (Luc. XII). Per hunc ignem incentiva voluptatis et libidinis consumuntur.

(IDEM.) Sunt alia multa observanda, in quibus divina indicatur sapientia. Primo enim non flectitur Pharao cum aqua in sanguinem vertitur. In secundo parum molliri videtur: Vocavit enim Moysem et Aaron, dicens: Orate pro me Dominum, etc. In tertio 212Dmagi cedunt, dicentes: Digitus Dei est hic, etc. In quarto per cynomyiam verberatus, dicit: Euntes immolate Domino Deo, etc. In quinto, ubi neces pecudum esse dicuntur, non cedit, sed amplius induravit. Similiter in sexto super plaga ulcerum. In septimo vero, cum grandine vastatur et ignibus, vocavit Moysen et Aaron, dicens: Peccavi etiam nunc, Dominus justus, ego autem et populi mei iniqui, etc. In octavo cum locustis urgeretur, festinanter vocavit Mosen et Aaron dicens: Peccavi ante Dominum Deum vestrum et in vos. Suscipite peccatum meum, etiam nunc orate ad Dominum Deum vestrum. In nono, cum tenebris suffunditur, vocat Mosen et Aaron, dicens: Ite et servite Domino Deo vestro. In decimo, cum primitiva exstinguuntur, vocat eos nocte et dicit: 213ASurgite, exite de populo meo, vos et filii Israel, etc. Et adjungit: Benedicite autem et me. Non possunt haec explicari nisi eodem spiritu quo gesta sunt, quia, secundum Apostolum (I Cor. XIV), spiritus prophetarum prophetis subjectus est; non ergo quibuscunque ad explanandum dicta prophetarum, sed prophetis dicuntur esse subjecta.

VERS. 30-35.-- Novi autem quod, etc. (AUG., quaest. 35 in Exod.) De fragore coeli et grandine exterritus erat Pharao. Unde rogabat Mosen ut oraret pro eo, confitens iniquitatem suam et populi sui. Sed Moses alium timorem quaerebat. Facile enim est poenam timere: sed non est hoc Deum timere, illo scilicet pietatis timore, de quo Jacob dicit: Nisi Deus patris mei Abraham, et timor Isaac adfuisset 213Bmihi, nunc me inanem dimisisses (Gen. XXXI).

CHAPTER IX.

VERS. 1-6.-- And the Lord said to Moses, etc. (PROCOP.) Among the beasts horses are reckoned first, which are deceitful or false unto salvation. Then the unclean 209Cbeasts, which neither divide the hoof nor chew the cud, yet are yoked to the chariot. Next camels, which although on account of their chewing the cud they are reckoned as it were clean, yet, because their hoof is not cloven, are judged unclean. Nevertheless camels are declared to be partly of clean, partly of unclean condition. But since this plague was believed to be but little harmful, because of the vileness of the unclean animals, it passes on to oxen, and thence to the gentlest of animals, the sheep.

(ORIG., hom. 4.) In the fifth place Egypt is scourged with the slaughter of animals, or of cattle. In which the senselessness or folly of mortals is reproved, who like irrational cattle laid the worship and the name of God upon figures, not only of men but of beasts, stamped upon wood and stones: worshipping Ammon Jupiter in a ram, 209DAnubis in a dog, Apis in a bull, and the other monstrous gods which Egypt admires; so that in those to which they believed divine worship to belong, they might behold a pitiable punishment.

(RAB. in Exod., tom. II.) The fifth precept is: Thou shalt not commit adultery, etc., as far as mark the plague: if thou art a beast, at least fear to die.

VERS. 7.-- And it was made heavy, etc. (AUG., quaest. 30.) If the cattle of the Israelites had died, there would seem a fitting cause why his heart should be weighed down to the contempt of God, as though his magicians had slain the cattle of the Israelites; but from that whence he ought to have been moved to fear or to believe, it was made heavy, and that heaviness has gone forward even to this point.

210AVERS. 8.-- Take handfuls, etc. (AUG., quaest. 31.) Another reading: Take to you handfuls of ashes out of the furnace, and let Moses sprinkle it, etc. The earlier signs were done by the rod, which not Moses but Aaron stretched over the water, or struck upon the ground. But now, two signs having been interposed, namely of the dog-fly and the deaths of the cattle, where neither Aaron nor Moses work anything with the hand, it is said that Moses should sprinkle ashes toward heaven; both are bidden to take, but he to sprinkle, not upon the earth but toward heaven: as though Aaron (who had been given to the people) ought to strike the earth, or to stretch out his hand upon the water or upon the earth; but Moses, who was in those things which pertain to the Lord, is bidden to sprinkle the ashes. What of those two earlier signs, where neither Moses nor Aaron work anything 210Bwith the hand? What does the diversity itself mean? For neither is it nothing.

(RAB.) The sixth precept: Thou shalt not kill; the sixth plague, pustules upon the body, blisters boiling and swarming, the burnings of wounds and the ashes of the furnace. For murderers burn with anger, with madness. If thou couldst see the souls of murderers, thou wouldst bewail them more than the putrefying bodies of the ulcerated.

VERS. 9-12.-- (ORIG., ibid.) Wounds and blisters with burning are brought forth at the sixth stroke. In the ulcers a crafty and purulent malice is reproved; in the blisters a swelling and inflated pride; in the burning, anger and the madness of fury.

VERS. 13-15.-- And the Lord hardened the heart, etc. (ORIG., ibid.) Note that in the sixth plague it is not said, 210CThe heart of Pharaoh was hardened, but something more terrible is added; for it is written that the Lord hardened the heart of Pharaoh, etc. Again in the seventh, when hail and fire lay Egypt waste, the heart of Pharaoh is indeed hardened, but not by the Lord. But in the eighth, where the locust is brought forth, the Lord is said to harden the heart of Pharaoh. And in the ninth, when the darkness becomes palpable; and at last, when, the firstborn being destroyed, the Israelites had departed, it is thus said: And the Lord hardened the heart of Pharaoh king of Egypt and of his servants, and he pursued them. And also, when Moses is sent from the land of Madian into Egypt and is commanded to do wonders, it is added: Thou shalt do them in the sight of Pharaoh: but I will harden the heart of Pharaoh, etc. Hence first 210Dit was said by the Lord: I will harden the heart of Pharaoh, etc. A second time also, the princes having been enumerated, it is said to Israel: I will harden the heart of Pharaoh, etc. Not in vain was that variety made, that now the Lord is said to have hardened the heart of Pharaoh, now that it was hardened of its own accord. I see Paul, as one understanding what difference there is between the heart of Pharaoh's having been hardened and the Lord's having hardened the heart of Pharaoh, saying in Romans II: Or despisest thou the riches of his goodness, and of patience, and of longsuffering? Knowest thou not that the benignity of God leadeth thee to penance, etc. Here without doubt he blames him who was hardened of his own accord. But elsewhere, as it were proposing a question upon this, he says: Therefore on whom he will he hath mercy, and whom he will he hardeneth, etc. He adds also to these: O man, thou who art, who repliest against God (Rom. IX). [Vulg. Rom 9:20 O homo, tu quis es, qui respondeas Deo, "O man, who art thou that repliest against God", which the Vulgate sets as a question; Migne's tu qui es puts a relative where the interrogative stood, and he prints the same verse with quis eleven lines below at 0211A] By which, concerning 211Ahim who is said to have been hardened by the Lord, he answers not so much by the solution of the question as by apostolic authority, because of the incapacity of his hearers, as he says elsewhere (II Cor. XII): And he heard secret words, which it is not granted to man to utter. Whence in what follows he deters the man who, not so much by the merit of his studies as by the lust of knowing, plunges himself too curiously [curiosus] into the more secret questions, saying in Romans IX: O man, who art thou that repliest against God.

(RAB.) The seventh precept: Thou shalt not steal; [Vulg. Ex 20:15 Non furtum facies, "Thou shalt not commit theft"; Migne's Non furaberis is the wording of Rom 13:9, not that of the Decalogue in Exodus] the seventh plague, hail upon the fruits. For no one has unjust gain without just loss. While thou stealest, thou gainest a garment, thou losest faith: gain visible, loss invisible: gain out of thy blindness, loss out of the Lord's cloud. For those who steal outwardly by evil desire 211Bare hailed upon inwardly out of judgment, and the field of their heart is laid waste.

VERS. 16, 17.-- And therefore have I set thee up, etc. (AUG., quaest. 32.) The Apostle set down these words, etc., as far as Pharaoh therefore was preserved for their profit.

VERS. 18.-- Behold I will cause it to rain to-morrow, etc. (ORIG.) Thus far the punishments are tempered to the world through the figures of its own errors: after these come stripes from on high.

VERS. 19-21.-- Send therefore now, etc. (AUG., quaest. 35 in Exod., tom. III.) God seems to forewarn of this not so much in indignation as in mercy, etc., as far as since God had threatened the death of the cattle, although Scripture has been silent of it.

(PROCOP.) Moreover it was brought about by divine providence 211Cthat the beasts should not all perish. For there was need of beasts to draw the chariots with which Pharaoh was afterwards to pursue the fugitives, that he might slaughter them in the wilderness. But divine providence governed the whole business otherwise.

VERS. 22.-- Stretch forth thy hand, etc. (PROCOP.) The reader is to be advised in this place that Moses is not bidden to do all the wonders with the rod, but only certain of them; certain also to foretell, as the gnats and the destruction of the beasts; certain to accomplish by the ministry of the hands, as when he takes soot from the furnace and stirs up the hail. But what is the cause of these things? Lest the common people should suppose that the rod had been made by magical superstition, and should assign to it those wondrous effects.

VERS. 23-29.-- And Moses stretched forth the rod, etc. 211D(ORIG., ubi supra. ) This also is to be noted, that Aaron is said to inflict certain stripes upon Pharaoh or upon Egypt, Moses certain, and certain the Lord himself. For in the first plague, where he turns the waters into blood, Aaron lifts up the rod and strikes the water. In the second he strikes the waters and brings forth frogs. In the third he stretches out his hand and strikes the dust of the earth with the rod, and there are made sciniphs. In the fourth the Lord is said to have done it, that the dog-fly should come. In the fifth also, when the cattle are destroyed, he is said to have done this thing. But in the sixth Moses sprinkles the ashes, and there are made ulcers and blisters. In the seventh he lifts his hand toward heaven and there are made thunders and hail, namely, etc. In the eighth likewise he stretches his hands toward heaven, and the Lord brings on a wind, and brings in 212Athe locusts. And in the ninth also he stretches his hand toward heaven, and there are made darkness and gloom. But in the tenth the end and completion of the whole work is administered by the Lord. For thus it is said: And the Lord struck every firstborn in the land of Egypt.

(IDEM.) There is another difference to be observed: for in the first plague, when the water is turned into blood, it is not yet said to Moses that he should go in to Pharaoh, but, go forth to meet him, etc. But in the second, the first having been ministered constantly and faithfully, it is said to him: Go in to Pharaoh, etc. But in the third the sciniphs are brought in, the magicians yield, confessing that this is the finger of God. But in the fourth Moses is bidden to watch in the morning, and to stand before Pharaoh as he goes forth to the waters, when the houses of the Egyptians are filled with the dog-fly. 212BIn the fifth, when the cattle are destroyed, he is bidden to go in to Pharaoh. In the sixth Pharaoh is despised, nor is it said that Moses and Aaron went in to him, because ulcers and blisters with burning were made even upon the magicians, and they could not resist Moses. In the seventh he is bidden to watch very early, and to stand before Pharaoh, when he brings forth hail and fire and thunders. In the eighth he is commanded to go in to him when the locust is brought forth. In the ninth again Pharaoh is despised, and Moses [Moses] is commanded to stretch his hands toward heaven, that palpable darkness may be made: nor does he go in, but he is called to Pharaoh. Likewise in the tenth, when the firstborn are destroyed, and he is compelled to go out of Egypt with haste.

And the Lord gave thunders, etc. (ID., homil., 4.) 212CSee the moderation of the divine correction: he does not scourge in silence, but gives voices, and sends doctrine from heaven, by which the chastised world may be able to acknowledge its own fault. He gives hail, by which the tender things of the vices, still being born, are laid waste. He gives fire also, knowing that there are thorns and thistles which had to be grazed down; of which the Lord says in Luke XII: I came to send fire in the earth (Luc. XII). By this fire the incitements of pleasure and of lust are consumed.

(IDEM.) There are many other things to be observed, in which the divine wisdom is shown. For at the first Pharaoh is not bent, when the water is turned into blood. At the second he seems a little softened: For he called Moses and Aaron, saying: Pray to the Lord for me, etc. At the third 212Dthe magicians yield, saying: This is the finger of God, etc. At the fourth, scourged by the dog-fly, he says: Go and sacrifice to the Lord your God, etc. At the fifth, where the slaughters of the cattle are said to be, he does not yield, but hardened himself the more. Likewise at the sixth, over the plague of ulcers. But at the seventh, when he is laid waste with hail and with fires, he called Moses and Aaron, saying: I have sinned this time also; the Lord is just, but I and my peoples are wicked, etc. At the eighth, when he was pressed with locusts, he hastily called Moses and Aaron, saying: I have sinned before the Lord your God, and against you. Take up my sin, and even now pray to the Lord your God. At the ninth, when he is overspread with darkness, he calls Moses and Aaron, saying: Go and serve the Lord your God. At the tenth, when the firstborn are destroyed, he calls them by night and says: 213AArise, go out from my people, you and the children of Israel, etc. And he adds: And bless me also. These things cannot be explained save by the same Spirit by which they were done, because, according to the Apostle (I Cor. XIV), the spirits of the prophets are subject to the prophets; therefore the sayings of the prophets are said to be subject, not to any whomsoever for the explaining of them, but to prophets.

VERS. 30-35.-- But I know that, etc. (AUG., quaest. 35 in Exod.) Pharaoh had been terrified by the crash of heaven and by the hail. Whence he begged Moses to pray for him, confessing his own iniquity and that of his people. But Moses was seeking another fear. For it is easy to fear punishment; but this is not to fear God, with that fear of piety, namely, of which Jacob says: Unless the God of my father Abraham, and the fear of Isaac had been present with 213Bme, thou wouldst now have sent me away empty (Gen. XXXI).

CAPUT X.

VERS. 1-3-- . . . Ego enim induravi cor ejus, etc. (AUG., quaest. 36 in Exod.) Haec non ita dixit tanquam opus habeat Deus cujusquam malitia, sed sic intelligendum est ac si diceret: Ego patiens fui super eum et servos ejus, ut non eos auferrem, ut ordine superveniant signa mea super eos; quia enim patientia Dei obstinatior fiebat malus animus, pro eo quod patiens in eo fui, dicitur, ingravavi cor ejus. Plagae Aegyptiorum eruditio est filiorum Israel, ne similiter indurentur, et similia patiantur. Multato pestilente sapientior erit parvulus.

(ORIG.) Possunt decem plagae quibus Aegyptus per 213CIsraelitas verberatur, Romani regni comparari temporibus, quia haec in figura nostri facta sunt. Christianus et Israeliticus populus unius Dei est, una utriusque causa subdita fuit, Synagoga Aegyptiis, Ecclesia Romanis. Persecuti sunt Aegyptii, persecuti sunt et Romani. Ibi decies contradicitur Mosi, hic decem edicta contra Christum. Diversae ibi plagae Aegyptiorum, diversae hic calamitates Romanorum. Ibi primum sanguis de puteis et fluminibus manat, hic prima sub Nerone plaga, ut ubique morientium sanguis esset, vel morbis in Urbe corruptus, vel bellis in orbe profusus. Ibi secunda plaga, ranae in penetralibus, inedia pene habitatores exstinxit: hic sub Domitiano satellites effrenati, jussu cruenti 213Dprincipis, et inopia pene omnes Romanos cives dispersit. Ibi tertia plaga sciniphes, musculae saevissimae, quae media aestate per loca squalida gregatim volant, capillisque hominum setisque pecudum urente morsu inferuntur: hic tertia plaga sub Trajano Judaeos excitavit, qui cum ante dispersi, quasi non essent, quiescerent, repentino calore permoti, grassati sunt. Praeterea multae urbes iisdem temporibus corruerunt terrae motu. Ibi quarta plaga, muscae caninae, alumnae putredinis, vermium matres; hic sub Marco Antonio lues multis infusa provinciis, Italiam et urbem Romam, exercitumque Romanorum per diversa hiberna dispersum, morte dissolutum, putredini et vermibus dedit. Ibi quinta plaga, pecorum et jumentorum: hic sub Severo persecutore civilibus 214Abellis propria viscera et adjumenta reipublicae, id est, plebes provinciarum et legiones militum, comminutae sunt. Ibi sexta plaga, vesicae turgentes ulceraque manantia: hic plaga quae post Maximini persecutionem fuit, qui specialiter episcopos et clericos, omissa turba populari, Ecclesiarum primates cruciari imperaverat, intumescens crebro ira et perfidia non per vulgi caedem, sed per vulnera morientium principum et potentium exaltata est. Ibi septima plaga, coacta in aere grando, quae hominibus et jumentis fuit exitio: hic sub Gallo et Volusiano, qui Decio persecutori successerunt, pestis infusa est per omnia spatia Romani regni, et omne prope genus humanum dedit neci. Ibi octava correctio, excitatae undique locustae: hic excitatae undique 214Bgentes Romanum orbem caedibus et incendiis vastaverunt. Ibi nona plaga, tenebrae tractabiles, plus periculi comminantes quam facientes: hic Aureliano persecutore decernente, diris turbinibus terribile fulmen sub ipsius pedibus ruit, ostendens quid faceret tantus ultor, nisi patiens esset; quanquam intra sex menses tres interfecti sunt imperatores, Aurelianus, Tacitus et Florianus. Ibi decima et ultima plaga, primogenitorum interfectio: hic omnium perditio idolorum, quae primitus facta in primis amabant. Ibi rex potentiam Dei probavit, et sensit, et timuit, et populum Dei liberum dimisit: hic quoque haec omnia fecit, et etiam credidit.

(IDEM.) Ibi populus Dei non est ad servitutem retractus, nec hic postea ad idololatriam coactus. Ibi 214CAegyptiorum vasa pretiosa Hebraeis tradita sunt: hic Ecclesiae Christianorum pretiosa paganorum templa cesserunt.

(IDEM.) Aegyptiis post decem plagas dimissos Hebraeos persequentibus maris irruit interitus: nos quoque peregrinantes persecutio gentilium manet, donec mare Rubrum, id est ignem judicii, Christo duce et judice transeamus. Qui vero Christianos persequuntur, cum rege suo Antichristo, in stagno ignis aeterni submergentur.

VERS. 4-12.-- Ecce ego inducam cras locustam, etc. (RAB.) Octavum praeceptum: Falsum testimonium non dices: octava plaga, locusta, animal scilicet dente noxium. Falsus enim testis nocet mordendo et consumit mentiendo. Unde: Si mordetis 214Det consumitis, videte ne ab invicem consumamini (Gal. V).

(ORIG.) hom. 4 in Exod.) Puto per hoc genus plagae dissidentis semper, a se et discordantis humani generis inconstantiam confutari. Locusta enim, cum regem non habeat, secundum Scripturam, ordinatum ducit exercitum: homines vero, cum rationales facti sunt, neque se regere ordinate potuerunt, neque Dei regentis patienter moderamina pertulerunt.

(STRAB.) Per locustam, quae regem non habet, significatur temeritas illorum qui, licet diversis poenis multati, semper in duritia sua manent, tanquam nullum habeant rectorem.

215ALocustam. (ISID.) Locustae pro mobilitate levitatis accipiuntur tanquam vagae et salientes animae pro voluptate saeculi.

VERS. 13-18.-- Et extendit Moses virgam. (RAB.) Quaedam prodigia fiunt per Mosen, quaedam per Aaron, quaedam vero per ipsum Dominum; ut cognoscamus in quibusdam per sacrificia sacerdotum et obsecrationes pontificum nos esse purgandos, quos Aaron persona designat: in quibusdam per scientiam legis emendandos, quod Mosi designat officium. In difficilioribus autem ipsius Domini virtute egemus.

(GREG., lib. XXXI, Moral., cap. 20). Exhibitae coelitus Aegypti plagae, etc., usque ad cum vanis laudibus quorumdam jam quasi fortium opera enervant.

215BVERS. 19, 20.-- Non remansit ne una quidem, etc. (AUG.) Beneficium certe Dei commemoravit Scriptura, quod abstulit locustas, et prosecuta dixit indurasse Dominum cor Pharaonis, beneficio scilicet suo et patientia, qua ille fiebat obstinatior dum ei parceretur: sicut omnia mala corda hominum patientia Dei male utendo durescunt.

VERS. 21.-- Extende manum, etc. (AUG.) Tertio dicitur ad Mosen: Extende manum tuam in coelum, ut fieret etiam plaga tenebrarum. Nunquam autem dictum est ad Aaron ut extenderet manus in coelum. Quod ergo dictum est ad Mosen: Extende manum tuam super terram Aegypti, et ascendat locusta super terram, credo id significari, etiam minus posse qui plus potest; non autem continuo cui minora conceduntur, 215Cposse majora.

(ORIG.) Nona plaga tenebrae sunt, ut mentis eorum caecitas arguatur, vel ut intelligant divinae dispensationis et providentiae obscurissimas esse rationes. Posuit enim Deus tenebras latibulum suum (Psal. XVII): quas illi audacter et temere perscrutantes, et alia ex aliis asserentes, in crassas et palpabiles errorum tenebras devoluti sunt.

(STRAB.) Per tenebras insipientia eorum [Judaeorum] ostenditur, qui affligebantur et ignorabant ad quem finem eorum poenae devenirent. Etiam vel per tenebras arcanum divini judicii significatur, quo justos misericorditer salvat, impios juste condemnat.

215DVERS. 24-29.-- Oves tantum, etc. (AUG. in Locutionibus in Exod., l. 52.) Graecus habet, praeter oves et boves relinquite, et est inusitata locutio, nisi post distinctionem inseratur, relinquite; et subaudiatur, ista, ut sit sensus: Ite praeter oves et boves, et relinquite ista. Solet enim talis eclipsis fieri crebro in locutionibus Scripturarum.

(RAB.) Nonum praeceptum: Non concupisces uxorem proximi tui: nona plaga, densae tenebrae. Est enim quaedam moechia etiam in appetendo uxorem alienam quando cuivis non sufficit sua, etiam alienam tentare. Vere densae sunt tenebrae; nihil enim tam dolet patiens, nihil tam nolit pati faciens: qui hoc facit vere caecatur furore horribili.

CHAPTER X.

VERS. 1-3-- . . . For I have hardened his heart, etc. (AUG., quaest. 36 in Exod.) He did not say this as though God had need of anyone's malice, but it is thus to be understood, as if he should say: I have been patient over him and his servants, that I might not take them away, so that my signs might come upon them in order; for because the evil mind was becoming the more obstinate through the patience of God, for this, that I was patient over him, it is said, I have made his heart heavy. The plagues of the Egyptians are the instruction of the children of Israel, lest they be hardened in like manner, and suffer like things. When the pestilent man is punished, the little one shall be the wiser.

(ORIG.) The ten plagues with which Egypt is scourged through 213Cthe Israelites can be compared to the times of the Roman empire, because these things were done in a figure of us. The Christian and the Israelite people are of one God; the cause of both was one and made subject, the Synagogue to the Egyptians, the Church to the Romans. The Egyptians persecuted, and the Romans also persecuted. There Moses is contradicted ten times, here ten edicts against Christ. There diverse plagues of the Egyptians, here diverse calamities of the Romans. There first blood flows from the wells and rivers; here the first plague under Nero, that everywhere there was the blood of the dying, either corrupted by diseases in the City, or poured out by wars throughout the world. There the second plague, frogs in the inner chambers; famine almost destroyed the inhabitants: here under Domitian unbridled henchmen, at the command of a bloody 213Dprince, and want, dispersed almost all the Roman citizens. There the third plague, sciniphs, most savage little flies, which in midsummer fly in swarms through squalid places, and are borne with a burning bite into the hair of men and the bristles of cattle: here the third plague stirred up the Jews under Trajan, who, whereas before, being scattered, they were quiet as though they were not, being moved by a sudden heat, went about ravaging. Besides, many cities in those same times fell down by an earthquake. There the fourth plague, dog-flies, nurslings of rottenness, mothers of worms; here under Marcus Antoninus a pestilence poured into many provinces gave over to rottenness and to worms Italy and the city of Rome, and the army of the Romans scattered through diverse winter quarters and dissolved by death. There the fifth plague, of cattle and of beasts of burden: here under Severus the persecutor, by civil 214Awars, the very bowels and supports of the commonwealth, that is, the commons of the provinces and the legions of soldiers, were broken in pieces. There the sixth plague, swelling blisters and running ulcers: here the plague which followed the persecution of Maximinus, who had commanded that especially the bishops and clerics, the popular crowd being passed over, the chief men of the Churches, should be tortured; and, swelling frequently with anger and perfidy, it was exalted not through the slaughter of the common people, but through the wounds of dying princes and men of power. There the seventh plague, hail gathered in the air, which was destruction to men and to beasts: here under Gallus and Volusianus, who succeeded Decius the persecutor, a pestilence was poured through all the spaces of the Roman empire, and gave nearly the whole human race to death. There the eighth correction, locusts stirred up on every side: here nations stirred up on every side 214Blaid waste the Roman world with slaughters and burnings. There the ninth plague, darkness that might be handled, threatening more of peril than working it: here, while Aurelian the persecutor was decreeing, amid dreadful whirlwinds a terrible thunderbolt fell at his very feet, showing what so great an avenger would do, were he not patient; although within six months three emperors were slain, Aurelian, Tacitus and Florianus. There the tenth and last plague, the slaying of the firstborn: here the destruction of all the idols, which, being made first, they loved among the first. There the king proved the power of God, and felt it, and feared, and let the people of God go free: here also he did all these things, and even believed.

(IDEM.) There the people of God was not drawn back to servitude, nor here afterwards compelled to idolatry. There 214Cthe precious vessels of the Egyptians were handed over to the Hebrews: here the precious temples of the pagans passed over to the Church of the Christians.

(IDEM.) Upon the Egyptians, pursuing the Hebrews who had been let go after the ten plagues, the destruction of the sea rushed in: upon us also, as we journey, the persecution of the Gentiles remains, until we pass through the Red Sea, that is, the fire of judgment, with Christ as leader and as judge. But those who persecute Christians shall be drowned, with their king Antichrist, in the pool of eternal fire.

VERS. 4-12.-- Behold I will bring in the locust to-morrow, etc. (RAB.) The eighth precept: Thou shalt not bear false witness: [Vulg. Ex 20:16 Non loqueris contra proximum tuum falsum testimonium, "Thou shalt not bear false witness against thy neighbour"] the eighth plague, the locust, a creature namely harmful with the tooth. For a false witness harms by biting and consumes by lying. Whence: If you bite 214Dand consume one another, take heed lest you be consumed one of another (Gal. V). [Vulg. Gal 5:15 comeditis, "devour"; the gloss's own verb two lines above is consumit, which follows Migne's reading and not the Vulgate's]

(ORIG.) hom. 4 in Exod.) I think that by this kind of plague the inconstancy of the human race, ever at variance and discordant with itself, is refuted. For the locust, although it has no king, according to the Scripture, leads an ordered army: but men, although they were made rational, were neither able to govern themselves in order, nor did they patiently bear the guidance of God who governs.

(STRAB.) By the locust, which has no king, is signified the rashness of those who, although punished with diverse penalties, always remain in their own hardness, as though they had no ruler.

215AThe locust. (ISID.) Locusts are taken for the mobility of levity, as souls wandering and leaping after the pleasure of the world.

VERS. 13-18.-- And Moses stretched forth the rod. (RAB.) Certain wonders are done through Moses, certain through Aaron, and certain through the Lord himself; that we may know that in some things we are to be cleansed through the sacrifices of the priests and the supplications of the pontiffs, whom the person of Aaron designates; in some to be amended through the knowledge of the law, which the office of Moses designates. But in the more difficult things we have need of the power of the Lord himself.

(GREG., lib. XXXI, Moral., cap. 20). The plagues of Egypt shown forth from heaven, etc., as far as when with empty praises they unnerve the works of certain men who are now as it were strong.

215BVERS. 19, 20.-- There remained not so much as one, etc. (AUG.) Scripture certainly recalled a benefit of God, in that he took away the locusts, and going on it said that the Lord had hardened the heart of Pharaoh, namely by his own benefit and patience, by which that man was becoming the more obstinate while he was being spared: as all evil hearts of men grow hard by ill using the patience of God.

VERS. 21.-- Stretch forth thy hand, etc. (AUG.) A third time it is said to Moses: Stretch forth thy hand toward heaven, that the plague of darkness also might be made. But never was it said to Aaron that he should stretch his hands toward heaven. What therefore was said to Moses: Stretch forth thy hand over the land of Egypt, and let the locust come up upon the land, I believe this to be signified, that he who can do the greater can do the less also; but not at once that he to whom the lesser things are granted 215Ccan do the greater.

(ORIG.) The ninth plague is darkness, that the blindness of their mind may be reproved, or that they may understand that the reasons of the divine dispensation and providence are most obscure. For God hath put darkness his covert (Psal. XVII): and they, boldly and rashly searching it out, and asserting one thing from another, have been rolled down into the thick and palpable darkness of errors.

(STRAB.) By the darkness their folly [Judaeorum] is shown, who were afflicted and did not know to what end their punishments would come. Or also by the darkness is signified the secret of the divine judgment, by which he mercifully saves the just and justly condemns the ungodly.

215DVERS. 24-29.-- Only the sheep, etc. (AUG. in Locutionibus in Exod., l. 52.) The Greek has, besides sheep and oxen leave ye, and it is an unusual manner of speech, unless after a pause there be inserted, leave ye; and there be understood, these things, so that the sense may be: Go, besides sheep and oxen, and leave these things. For such an ellipsis is wont to be made often in the manners of speech of the Scriptures.

(RAB.) The ninth precept: Thou shalt not covet thy neighbour's wife: [Vulg. Ex 20:17 Non concupisces domum proximi tui, nec desiderabis uxorem ejus, "Thou shalt not covet thy neighbour's house, neither shalt thou desire his wife"; Migne's wording here is that of Dt 5:21, not that of the Decalogue in Exodus] the ninth plague, thick darkness. For there is a certain adultery even in desiring another man's wife, when his own does not suffice a man, and he attempts another's also. Truly the darkness is thick; for nothing so grieves the sufferer, nothing does the doer so refuse to suffer: he who does this is truly blinded with a horrible fury.

CAPUT XI.

  • 216A

VERS. 2.-- Dices ergo omni plebi, etc. (AUG., quaest. 94 in Exod., tom. III.) Non debet hinc quisque exemplum sumere, etc., usque ad ut nec isto damno ferirentur, qui aliis plagis percussi non erant.

Ut postulet vir, etc. (GREG., lib. XXVIII Moral., cap. 17.) Mentes usui vitae carnalis inhaerentes, etc., usque ad major fortitudo spiritus nuntiatur.

VERS. 3, 4.-- Dabit autem Dominus gratiam populo suo coram Aegyptiis, etc. Qui scilicet habitabant cum Hebraeis in terra Gessen, et pro ipsis novem plagis laesi non erant, ideoque eos diligebant: decima plaga afflicti sunt, cum discessissent Hebraei ab eis.

216BVERS. 5, 6.-- Et morietur omne primogenitum, etc. (ORIG. hom. 4) Ad ultimum primitivorum infertur interitus, in quo fortassis est aliquid supra nostram intelligentiam, quod commissum ab Aegyptiis videatur in Ecclesiam primitivorum, quae ascripta est in coelis. Unde etiam exterminator angelus ad hoc ministerium mittitur, qui parcit illis tantum qui utrosque postes agni sanguine signatos habuerunt. Delentur iterum primogenita Aegyptiorum: sive eos principatus et potestates, et mundi hujus rectores tenebrarum dicamus, quos adventu suo Christus traduxit, id est captivos duxit et captivavit, cum in ligno crucis triumphavit: sive auctores et inventores falsarum religionum, quas Christi veritas cum suis exstinxit auctoribus. Principatus, et potestates, 216Cet rectores tenebrarum harum, primogenita sunt Aegyptiorum quod [ Al., qui] in ultima plaga, cum scilicet finis mundi advenerit cum separabit oves ab haedis, grana a paleis, damnabuntur, amissa vagandi et tentandi licentia.

(ORIG.) Moraliter. Anima, dum in hoc mundo in erroribus vivit, ignorantia veritatis, in Aegypto posita est: si appropinquare coepit lex Dei, aquas in sanguinem convertit, id est fluidam et lubricam juventutis vitam convertit ad sanguinem Veteris vel Novi Testamenti. Deinde educit lex ex ea vanam et inanem loquacitatem adversum Dei providentiam, ranarum querelae similem. Purgat etiam malignas cogitationes ejus, et sciniphum mordacitati similes calliditatis aculeos discutit. Libidinum quoque morsus 216Dcynomyiae spiculis similes depellit: stultitiamque et intellectum pecudibus similem per quam homo, cum in honore esset, non intellexit, sed comparatus est jumentis insipientibus, etc. (Psal. XLVIII.) Arguit ulcera peccatorum, arrogantiae tumorem, et fervorem furoris exstinguit. Adhibet post haec voces filiorum tonitrui, id est evangelicas et apostolicas doctrinas, et castigationem grandinis, ut luxuriam voluptatesque coerceat; et simul ignem poenitentiae, ut dicat: Nonne cor nostrum ardens erat in nobis (Luc. XXIV)? Nec non locustarum ab ea subducit exempla, quibus depascantur omnes inquieti et turbidi motus ejus, ut discat omnia secundum ordinem facere. Ubi vero sufficienter fuerit castigata pro moribus, cum auctorem 217Averberum senserit, confitens quia digitus Dei est, et parum agnitionis acceperit: tunc praecipue gestorum suorum tenebras videt et errorum caliginem, et tunc merebitur ut exstinguantur in ea primogenita Aegypti. Omnis enim anima cum ad supplementum aetatis venerit, et velut naturalis lex coeperit in ea jura sua defendere, primos motus secundum desiderium carnis producit, quos ex concupiscentiae vel irae fomite vis incentiva commoverit. Unde quasi privilegium singulare de Christo propheta dicit: Butyrum et mel manducabit; priusquam faciat aut proferat maligna, eliget bonum (Isa. VII); quoniam priusquam sciat puer bonum aut malum, resistet malitiae, ut eligat quod bonum est. David autem de se dicit: Delicta juventutis meae et ignorantias 217Bmeas ne memineris (Psal. XXIV). Quia ergo hi primi animae motus secundum carnem prolati in peccatum ruunt, merito in morali loco primogenita Aegyptiorum primitiva ponuntur, quia exstinguuntur si reliquae vitae conversatio emendatiorem dirigat cursum. Sic ergo in anima, quam lex divina ab erroribus susceptam castigat, primogenita Aegyptiorum delentur, nisi post haec omnia in infidelitate perdurans, nolit jungi Israeliticae plebi, ut exeat de profundo et evadat incolumis, sed in iniquitate permaneat, et descendat tanquam plumbum in aquam validissimam. Iniquitas enim, secundum Zachariam (ZAC. V), sedet super talentum plumbi: et ideo qui permanet in iniquitate, tanquam plumbum demergitur in profondum.

217CVERS. 7.-- Apud omnes, etc. (AUG. in libro Locutionum. ) Elegantissima locutio est: per canem quippe extremum significavit, vel hominum, vel pecorum: ita commendavit quanta in Hebraeis quies futura esset, Aegyptiis acerba orbitate clamantibus.

VERS. 9, 10.-- Non audiet vos Pharao, etc. (AUG., quaest. 40 in Exod.) Tanquam opus fuerit Pharaonis inobedientia ut signa illa multiplicarentur: quae utiliter fiebant ad terrendum populum Dei, atque ipsa discretione ad pietatem informandum. Sed hoc Dei fuit, malitia illius cordis bene utentis, non Pharaonis Dei patientia male abutentis.

CHAPTER XI.

  • 216A

VERS. 2.-- Thou shalt tell therefore all the people, etc. (AUG., quaest. 94 in Exod., tom. III.) No one ought to take an example from this, etc., as far as that not even by this loss should those be smitten who had not been struck by the other plagues.

That every man ask, etc. (GREG., lib. XXVIII Moral., cap. 17.) Minds cleaving to the use of the carnal life, etc., as far as a greater fortitude of spirit is announced.

VERS. 3, 4.-- And the Lord will give favour to his people in the sight of the Egyptians, etc. Those, namely, who dwelt with the Hebrews in the land of Gessen, and for their sake had not been hurt by the nine plagues, and therefore loved them: by the tenth plague they were afflicted, when the Hebrews had departed from them.

216BVERS. 5, 6.-- And every firstborn shall die, etc. (ORIG. hom. 4) At the last the destruction of the firstborn is brought in, in which there is perhaps something above our understanding, which seems to have been committed by the Egyptians against the Church of the firstborn, which is enrolled in the heavens. Whence also the destroying angel is sent to this ministry, who spares those only who had both door-posts marked with the blood of the lamb. Again the firstborn of the Egyptians are destroyed: whether we call them principalities and powers, and the rulers of the darkness of this world, whom Christ by his coming led forth, that is, led captive and made captive, when he triumphed upon the wood of the cross; or the authors and inventors of false religions, which the truth of Christ has extinguished together with their authors. Principalities, and powers, 216Cand the rulers of these darknesses, are the firstborn of the Egyptians, which [ Al., qui] in the last plague, namely when the end of the world shall have come, when he shall separate the sheep from the goats, the grain from the chaff, shall be condemned, the licence of wandering and of tempting being lost.

(ORIG.) Morally. The soul, while it lives in this world in errors, in ignorance of the truth, is placed in Egypt: if the law of God has begun to draw near, it turns the waters into blood, that is, it turns the fluid and slippery life of youth to the blood of the Old or of the New Testament. Then the law draws out of it the vain and empty loquacity against the providence of God, like to the croaking of frogs. It purges also its malignant thoughts, and shakes off the stings of craftiness that are like to the biting of the sciniphs. The bites of lusts also, 216Dlike to the darts of the dog-fly, it drives away; and the folly and the understanding like to that of cattle, through which man, when he was in honour, did not understand, but was compared to senseless beasts, etc. (Psal. XLVIII.) It reproves the ulcers of sins, the swelling of arrogance, and extinguishes the burning of fury. After these it applies the voices of the sons of thunder, that is, the evangelical and apostolic doctrines, and the chastisement of hail, that it may restrain luxury and pleasures; and at the same time the fire of penitence, that it may say: Was not our heart burning within us (Luc. XXIV)? Nor does it fail to draw off from it the examples of the locusts, by which all its restless and turbid motions may be grazed down, that it may learn to do all things according to order. But when it shall have been sufficiently chastised for its manners, when it shall have perceived the author 217Aof the stripes, confessing that it is the finger of God, and shall have received a little of acknowledgment: then chiefly it sees the darkness of its own deeds and the gloom of its errors, and then it shall deserve that the firstborn of Egypt be destroyed in it. For every soul, when it shall have come to the fulness of age, and as it were the natural law shall have begun to defend its own rights within it, brings forth the first motions according to the desire of the flesh, which the inciting force has stirred up out of the tinder of concupiscence or of anger. Whence as a singular privilege the prophet says of Christ: He shall eat butter and honey; before he shall do or bring forth evil things, he shall choose the good (Isa. VII); [Vulg. Is 7:15 Butyrum et mel comedet, ut sciat reprobare malum, et eligere bonum, "He shall eat butter and honey, that he may know to refuse the evil and to choose the good"] since before the child knows good or evil, he shall resist malice, that he may choose that which is good. But David says of himself: The sins of my youth and my ignorances 217Bremember not (Psal. XXIV). Because therefore these first motions of the soul, brought forth according to the flesh, rush into sin, deservedly in the moral sense the firstborn of the Egyptians are set down as the first things, because they are extinguished if the conversation of the remaining life directs a more amended course. So therefore in the soul, which the divine law, having taken it up out of its errors, chastises, the firstborn of the Egyptians are destroyed, unless, enduring after all these things in unbelief, it refuse to be joined to the people of Israel, so as to come out of the deep and escape unharmed, but remain in iniquity, and go down as lead into the mightiest water. For iniquity, according to Zacharias (ZAC. V), sits upon a talent of lead: and therefore he who remains in iniquity is sunk like lead into the deep profondum.

217CVERS. 7.-- Among all, etc. (AUG. in libro Locutionum. ) It is a most elegant manner of speech: for by the dog he signified the last, whether of men or of cattle: thus he set forth how great a quiet there should be among the Hebrews, while the Egyptians cried out in bitter bereavement.

VERS. 9, 10.-- Pharaoh shall not hear you, etc. (AUG., quaest. 40 in Exod.) As though there had been need of the disobedience of Pharaoh that those signs might be multiplied: which were profitably done for the terrifying of the people of God, and for forming it to piety by that very discrimination. But this was of God, who used well the malice of that heart, not of Pharaoh, who ill abused the patience of God.

CAPUT XII.

217DVERS. 2-4.-- Mensis iste, etc. (RAB.) Hic est Aprilis, qui apud Hebraeos Nisam, apud Macedones Ξανθικός dicitur, et dicitur Aprilis quasi aperilis, quia tunc aperitur terra proferendis virgultis herbis ac floribus: vel Aprilis a Venere, quasi Aphroditis; Venus enim Graece Aphrodite dicitur. Hunc Hebraei primum habent, quia XV Kalendarum ejus prima dies saeculi fuit: quarta decima vero, dies secunda; tertia decima, tertia: duodecima, quarta: qua sol et luna condita sunt, et tunc primum aequinoctium fuit; sol enim in oriente, luna in occidente, sphaeram mundi ex aequo dividebant.

(STRAB.) Hic est Nisan, secundum Hebraeos, in quo factus est mundus, cujus plenilunium post aequinoctium 218Avernale semper est attendendum: in quo populus Israel a servitute Aegypti liberatur, et genus humanum sanguine Christi a potestate diaboli eripitur. Decima die mensis tollitur agnus, quarto decimo immolatur: quia in lege passio Christi figuratur, Evangelii gratia coruscante impletur; denarius enim, propter decalogum, legem significat; quaternarius vero, quatuor evangelia.

VERS. 5, 6.-- Erit autem, etc. (AUG., quaest. 42 in Exod., tom. III.) Movere potest illa locutio, etc., usque ad quid illo praecepto figuratum sit.

Juxta quem ritum, etc. Allegorice. Quia de justis et peccatoribus Christus carnem assumpsit, ut nemo de venia desperaret. Vel quia Christus Judaeis haedus erat, dicentibus: Hic homo peccator est (Joan. 218BIX), nobis autem agnus absque macula. Vel quia in similitudine carnis peccati apparuit et pro peccato mortuus est, cum peccatum non haberet (I Petr. II).

(STRAB.) Decima die Aprilis Hierosolymam venit, sequenti vero die exceptus est a turbis cum ramis palmarum et divinis laudibus. Tribus reliquis custoditus est a Judaeis usque ad quartam decimam diem, id est, usque ad quintam feriam, qua cum apostolis coenavit, et post traditus est.

VERS. 7.-- Et sument de sanguine ejus, etc. (GREG., homil. 22 in Evang., tom. II.) Sanguis ponitur, quando non solum ore corporis, sed etiam ore cordis hauritur, etc., usque ad quia crucem passionis ejus in fronte portamus.

VERS. 8.-- Et edent carnes, etc. (GREG., ibid.) 218CNocte agnum comedimus, etc., usque ad de fermento enim laudem immolat, qui Deo sacrificium de rapina parat.

Cum lactucis, etc. (GREG., ibid.) Lactucae agrestes valde amarae sunt, etc., usque ad ut dispensari omnia per Spiritus sancti potentiam sciat.

VERS. 9-11.-- Caput cum pedibus, etc. (GREG., ibid.) Caput Christi Deus (I Cor. XI). Caput ergo agni vorare, etc., usque ad nemo in hujus vitae itinere torpeat, ne in patria locum perdat.

(GREG. NYSS., de Vita Mosis. ) Illis rerum involucris monet ut per haec aenigmata considerare et intelligere velimus transitum quemdam esse hanc vitam nostram. Nam postea quam ingressi ad eam sumus, necessitate quadam ad exitum semper compellimur, 218Dad quem paratos nos esse vult manibus et pedibus reliquoque ad iter apparatu. Ne igitur spinis in hoc arduo vivendi itinere pedes laedantur, non nudis pedibus, sed durioribus calceis communiti itinerare debemus. Spinae autem, quae pedibus infixae non solum retinent retardantque, verum etiam enecant, peccata sunt, a quibus durities calceorum defendit, continens videlicet, tenuis ac dura vita, quae debilitat atque frangit spinarum acumina, quibus a parvo tenuique principio ad anteriora, usque peccata sic ingrediuntur, ut poenitus occidant. Ad hanc vitam faciendam talaris vestis et effusa, id est voluptuosa et vitiosa vivendi ratio, quae latissime patet, impedimento est: quare cingulo quodam trahenda 219Aest et coarctanda, ne huc atque illuc circumfluat. Cingulus vero modestia est et diligens vivendi ratio, quae nos monet ad necessitatem, non ad voluptatem, rebus uti. Baculus quo feras repellimus, et nonnunquam sustentamur, spes est. Ex igne autem solum cibus nobis propositus est, quoniam fides, quae per cibum illum significatur, ardentissima esse debet, nihil humidum aut labile possidens.

Phase. Hebraice est Pesach פסח , Graece Πάσχα, id est, transitus; quia a filiis Israel ex Aegypto migrantibus primum celebratum est: nobis quoque migrandum est de transitoriis ad aeterna, de terrenis ad coelestia.

VERS. 12, 13.-- In cunctis diis, etc. Ferunt Hebraei quia omnia idola Aegypti confracta sunt nocte 219Billa et templa, vel motu terrae, vel fluminis Nili inundatione. Allegorice vero nobis egredientibus ex Aegypto errorum idola corruunt, et omnis perversorum dogmatum cultura quatitur.

VERS. 14-17.-- Habebitis autem hanc diem, etc. (AUG., in Exod. quaest. 43, tom. III.) Et facietis diem hunc in progenies vestras legitimum, aeternum vel aeternalem; quod Graece dicitur αἰὼνιον, non sic accipiendum est, etc., usque ad omni tempore praesentis vitae simplicitas servanda, malitia cavenda.

VERS. 18-20.-- Primo mense quarta decima, etc. (RAB.) Mystice. Vespera quarti decimi diei primi mensis, in quo Pascha celebratur, et agni immolatio perficitur, depositionem veteris hominis significat, et initium novi, qui in baptismo Christi mortem 219Cfigurante inchoatur. Unde: Quicunque baptizati sumus in Christo Jesu, in mortem ipsius baptizati sumus, etc. (Gal. III). A vespera ergo quarti decimi diei primi mensis usque ad vesperam vicesimi primi integram, scilicet septimanam, azyma jubemur [jubentur] comedere, quia a perceptione baptismi usque ad finem vitae simpliciter et sine dolo debemus vivere. Unde Petrus: Deponentes omnem malitiam et omnem dolum et simulationem, sicut modo geniti infantes, etc. (I Petr. II).

VERS. 21.-- Seniores. Nota quod non pueris, non juvenibus, mandata Dei primo communicat, sed senioribus, quia in senibus est sapientia, et in multo tempore prudentia.

VERS. 22.-- Fasciculumque hyssopi, etc. (STRAB.) 219DHyssopus herba est humilis, nascens in petris, purgans pectus et pulmonem: et significat veram humilitatem, cujus exemplum Christus nobis in semetipso praebuit. Fasciculum ergo hyssopi sanguine tingimus, quando, memores humilitatis Christi, eum imitari studemus.

Tingite in sanguine, etc. (AUG., quaest. 46 in Exod.) Quaeritur quem sanguinem dicat juxta ostium, cum illius agni sanguinem velit intelligi, cujus immolatione Pascha fit. An praecipit eo modo consequenter fieri, quamvis hoc tacuerit, ut idem agnus juxta ostium occidatur? An, quod est credibilius, ideo dixit ex sanguine qui est juxta ostium, quia ille qui liniturus est, vas ipsum in quo sanguinem excepit, 220Ajuxta ostium positurus est, ut ad manum habeat dum tingit.

VERS. 23-29.-- Et non sinet percussorem, etc. (STRAB.) Utrum percussor bonus angelus an malus fuerit, non necesse est inquiri: quicunque enim fuit, praecepto Domini obedivit. Sciendum tamen quod tales pestes mali angeli facere solent, permissione Dei.

Decimum praeceptum est: Non concupisces rem proximi tui, etc. Huic contraria est decima plaga, mors primogenitorum. Alio praecepto furtum prohibetur, quo et rapina damnatur: pejus enim est eripere violenter quam latenter. Auferre ergo volens aperte vel occulte praeceptum suum habet: concupiscere autem rem proximi, etiamsi justa successione 220Bquaeras, non licet. Qui enim volunt res alienas tanquam juste possidere, et haeredes quaerunt morientibus illis esse. Cum laudas rem relictam quasi jure possidentem, Deus damnat injuste cupientem. Qui enim quaeris ab aliquo haeres fieri, non vis ut habeat haeredes, in quibus nihil charius primogenitis est. Jure ergo in primogenitis tuis puniris, qui cupiendo res alienas, quasi juris umbram perquiris, et, quae meliora sunt, cordis primogenita perdis. Primogenita quidem fides est. Nemo enim bona facit, in quo fides non praecessit. Omnia opera bona sunt filii spirituales, sed inter hos primogenita fides. Quisquis ergo rem occulte cupis alienam, interius fidem perdis. Servis enim non charitate, sed fraude, velut amans eum quem mori desideras, ut succedas.

220CVERS. 30-33.-- Neque enim erat domus, etc. (AUG., quaest. 44 in Exod.) Cum primogeniti tantum morerentur, quomodo nulla erat domus in qua non esset mortuus? An et hoc divinitus fuerat procuratum, ut in omnibus domibus essent primogeniti, in quibus percuterentur Aegyptii, etiam qui habitabant in terra Gessen? Hominum quippe erant hic, vel animalium, non terrae; id est, homines et animalia primogenita moriebantur, occulto et angelico percussu: non aliquid in terra vel in coelo factum erat, sicut rana, vel locusta, vel tenebrae. A talibus enim plagis cum terra Gessen fuisset aliena, perveniebat beneficium ad Aegyptios qui in ea cum Hebraeis morabantur: hac vero omnes percussi sunt.

VERS. 34.-- Conspersam farinam, etc. (AUG.) 220DMystice. Sine fermento proficiscuntur filii Israel de Aegypto; ut exeuntes de hoc saeculo abjiciamus fermentum malitiae et nequitiae, et epulemur in azymis sinceritatis et veritatis.

VERS. 35, 36.-- Et petierunt, etc. (AUG., quaest. 45.) Hoc jam factum fuerat ante mortes primogenitorum. Sed nunc per recapitulationem repetitur, nam narratum est quando factum est. Modo enim quomodo posset fieri ut in tanto luctu ista accommodarentur: nisi forte quis dicat etiam ista plaga non fuisse percussos Aegyptios qui cum Hebraeis habitabant.

Vasa aurea, etc. Haec doctrinas significant, quae ex ipsa consuetudine gentium non inutili studio discuntur; vel animas pretiosas ex gentibus, tanquam 221Avasa aurea et argentea; cum vestibus, id est, corporibus: quae se adjungunt populo Dei, ut semel de hoc saeculo tanquam de Aegypto liberentur.

VERS. 37-45.-- Profectique sunt, etc. (ORIG. hom. 5 in Exod.). Qui de Aegypto parat proficisci, qui cupit obscuros mundi actus et errorum tenebras relinquere, primo ei ex Ramesse proficiscendum est, quae interpretatur commotio tineae. Si vis venire in hoc ut Deus sit tibi dux, et sequatur petra, quae escam spiritalem et potum tribuat, exi de Ramesse, ne thesaurizes ubi tinea exterminat, et fures effodiunt, etc.

Si vis perfectus esse, vade et vende omnia quae habes, et da pauperibus, et habebis thesaurum in coelo, et veni, sequere me (Matth. VI), hoc est proficisci de Ramesse, et sequi Christum.

221BDe Ramesse in Socoth, etc. (ORIG. ibid.) Videamus quis sit castrorum locus ad quem venitur. De Ramesse venerunt in Socoth, quod interpretatur tabernacula. Cum ergo, relinquens Aegyptum, discusseris tineas totius corruptelae, et vitiorum incitamenta rejeceris, habitabis in tabernaculis, quibus scilicet nolumus exspoliari sed supervestiri. Habitatio autem tabernaculorum expeditum indicat esse eum qui festinat ad Deum. Sed nec in hoc standum est: movenda sunt etiam castra de Socoth, et festinandum est in Othon, quod interpretatur signa eis, quia hic scilicet audies quod Deus antecedat eos per diem in columna nubis, et per noctem in columna ignis; non hoc in Ramesse, nec in Socoth, quae secunda sunt castra. Tertia sunt in quibus fiunt signa divina: hoc 221Cerat triduum ad quod festinabant Moses et Aaron, et contradicebat Pharao dicens: Non eatis longius. Nolebat ad locum signorum pervenire filios Israel, ut possent tertii diei frui mysteriis. Unde Ose. VI: Resuscitabit nos Deus post biduum, et in die tertia resurgemus, et vivemus in conspectu ejus. Prima dies nobis Christi passio; secunda, qua in infernum descendit; tertia, qua resurrexit: ideo in tertia die antecedebat eos per diem in columna nubis; per noctem vero, in columna ignis. Necessarium est ergo ut qui baptizantur in Christo, in morte ipsius baptizentur, et cum ipso sepeliantur, et die tertia resurgant, quos scilicet secum suscitavit et fecit consedere in coelestibus (Rom. VI).

Sexcenta fere millia, etc. (AUG., quaest. 47.) Quaeritur 221Dutrum ad tantum numerum pervenerunt Hebraei per eos annos in Aegypto, etc., usque ad id est, post mortem Joseph dura servitute oppressi sunt.

VERS. 46-51.-- Nec illius, etc. Os Christi in cruce non est confractum, quia videntes eum jam mortuum, non fregerunt ejus crura: vel robur divinitatis in carne per blasphemiam non corrumpitur, quia non est purus homo, sed verus Deus.

CHAPTER XII.

217DVERS. 2-4.-- This month, etc. (RAB.) This is April, which among the Hebrews is called Nisam, among the Macedonians Ξανθικός; and it is called Aprilis as it were aperilis, because then the earth is opened for the bringing forth of shoots, herbs and flowers: or Aprilis from Venus, as it were Aphroditis; for Venus in Greek is called Aphrodite. The Hebrews hold this month as the first, because the fifteenth of its Kalends was the first day of the world: the fourteenth, the second day; the thirteenth, the third; the twelfth, the fourth, on which the sun and the moon were made, and then was the first equinox; for the sun in the east and the moon in the west divided the sphere of the world equally.

(STRAB.) This is Nisan, according to the Hebrews, in which the world was made, whose full moon is always to be looked for after the vernal 218Aequinox: in which the people of Israel is freed from the servitude of Egypt, and the human race is snatched by the blood of Christ from the power of the devil. On the tenth day of the month the lamb is taken, on the fourteenth it is sacrificed: because in the law the passion of Christ is figured, and, the grace of the Gospel flashing forth, it is fulfilled; for the number ten, because of the decalogue, signifies the law, but the number four the four gospels.

VERS. 5, 6.-- And it shall be, etc. (AUG., quaest. 42 in Exod., tom. III.) That manner of speech may disturb a man, etc., as far as what was figured by that precept.

According to which rite, etc. Allegorically. Because Christ took flesh of the just and of sinners, that no one might despair of pardon. Or because Christ was a kid to the Jews, who said: This man is a sinner (Joan. 218BIX), but to us a lamb without spot. Or because he appeared in the likeness of sinful flesh and died for sin, whereas he had no sin (I Petr. II).

(STRAB.) On the tenth day of April he came to Jerusalem, and on the following day he was received by the crowds with branches of palms and with divine praises. Through the three remaining days he was watched by the Jews until the fourteenth day, that is, until the fifth day of the week, on which he supped with the apostles, and afterwards was betrayed.

VERS. 7.-- And they shall take of the blood thereof, etc. (GREG., homil. 22 in Evang., tom. II.) The blood is set on when it is drunk not only with the mouth of the body, but also with the mouth of the heart, etc., as far as because we bear the cross of his passion upon the forehead.

VERS. 8.-- And they shall eat the flesh, etc. (GREG., ibid.) 218CBy night we eat the lamb, etc., as far as for he offers praise out of leaven who prepares for God a sacrifice out of rapine.

With wild lettuces, etc. (GREG., ibid.) Wild lettuces are exceeding bitter, etc., as far as that he may know that all things are dispensed through the power of the Holy Spirit.

VERS. 9-11.-- The head with the feet, etc. (GREG., ibid.) The head of Christ is God (I Cor. XI). To devour therefore the head of the lamb, etc., as far as let no one be sluggish on the journey of this life, lest he lose his place in the fatherland.

(GREG. NYSS., de Vita Mosis. ) By those wrappings of things he admonishes us that through these riddles we should be willing to consider and to understand that this our life is a certain passing over. For after we have entered upon it, we are always driven by a certain necessity toward the going out, 218Dfor which he wills us to be prepared with hands and feet and the remaining equipment for the journey. Lest therefore our feet be hurt by thorns on this steep journey of living, we ought to travel not with bare feet, but fortified with harder shoes. But the thorns, which, fixed in the feet, not only hold back and retard but also kill, are sins, from which the hardness of the shoes defends: namely a continent, spare and hard life, which weakens and breaks the points of the thorns, by which, from a small and slender beginning, sins so make their way onward to the things before them, that they slay utterly. To the leading of this life an ankle-length and flowing garment, that is, a voluptuous and vicious manner of living, which lies open most widely, is a hindrance: wherefore it is to be drawn in 219Awith a certain girdle and constrained, lest it flow about hither and thither. But the girdle is modesty and a careful manner of living, which admonishes us to use things for necessity, not for pleasure. The staff with which we drive off wild beasts, and by which we are sometimes supported, is hope. But the food set before us is only from the fire, since faith, which is signified by that food, ought to be most burning, possessing nothing moist or slippery.

Phase. In Hebrew it is Pesach פסח , in Greek Πάσχα, that is, a passing over; because it was first celebrated by the children of Israel as they went out of Egypt: we also must pass over from things transitory to things eternal, from things earthly to things heavenly.

VERS. 12, 13.-- In all the gods, etc. The Hebrews report that all the idols of Egypt were broken in pieces on that night, 219Band the temples also, either by an earthquake or by an inundation of the river Nile. But allegorically, as we go out of Egypt, the idols of errors fall down, and all the cultivation of perverse doctrines is shaken.

VERS. 14-17.-- And you shall have this day, etc. (AUG., in Exod. quaest. 43, tom. III.) And you shall keep this day in your generations as lawful, everlasting or eternal; which in Greek is called αἰὼνιον, is not to be taken thus, etc., as far as at every time of the present life simplicity is to be kept, malice to be shunned.

VERS. 18-20.-- In the first month, the fourteenth day, etc. (RAB.) Mystically. The evening of the fourteenth day of the first month, on which the Pasch is celebrated and the immolation of the lamb is accomplished, signifies the putting off of the old man and the beginning of the new, which is begun in baptism, 219Cthat figures the death of Christ. Whence: As many of us as are baptized in Christ Jesus, are baptized into his death, etc. (Gal. III). From the evening therefore of the fourteenth day of the first month until the evening of the twenty-first, a whole week namely, we are commanded [jubentur] to eat unleavened bread, because from the receiving of baptism until the end of life we ought to live simply and without guile. Whence Peter: Laying aside all malice and all guile and dissimulation, as newborn babes, etc. (I Petr. II).

VERS. 21.-- The ancients. Note that he communicates the commandments of God first not to boys, not to young men, but to the elders, because in the aged is wisdom, and in length of time prudence.

VERS. 22.-- And a bunch of hyssop, etc. (STRAB.) 219DHyssop is a lowly herb, growing upon rocks, purging the breast and the lung: and it signifies true humility, of which Christ afforded us the example in himself. We dip therefore the bunch of hyssop in the blood, when, mindful of the humility of Christ, we study to imitate him.

Dip it in the blood, etc. (AUG., quaest. 46 in Exod.) It is asked what blood he calls that which is beside the door, since he would have understood the blood of that lamb by whose immolation the Pasch is made. Or does he command that it be done in that manner consequently, although he has been silent of this, that the same lamb be slain beside the door? Or, which is the more credible, he therefore said of the blood which is beside the door, because he who is to anoint is about to set 220Abeside the door the very vessel in which he received the blood, that he may have it at hand while he dips.

VERS. 23-29.-- And he will not suffer the destroyer, etc. (STRAB.) Whether the smiter was a good angel or an evil one, it is not necessary to inquire: for whoever he was, he obeyed the command of the Lord. Yet it is to be known that evil angels are wont to work such plagues, by the permission of God.

The tenth precept is: Thou shalt not covet thy neighbour's goods, etc. [Vulg. Ex 20:17 Non concupisces domum proximi tui … nec omnia quae illius sunt, "Thou shalt not covet thy neighbour's house … nor any thing that is his"] Contrary to this is the tenth plague, the death of the firstborn. By another precept theft is forbidden, by which rapine also is condemned: for it is worse to snatch away by violence than by stealth. He therefore who wishes to take away openly or secretly has his own precept: but to covet a neighbour's goods, even if thou seek it by a just succession, 220Bis not lawful. For those who wish to possess other men's goods as it were justly, seek also to be heirs to them when they die. When thou praisest a thing left behind, as though the man possessed it by right, God condemns him who desires it unjustly. For thou who seekest to be made heir to another dost not wish that he should have heirs, among whom nothing is dearer than the firstborn. By right therefore art thou punished in thine own firstborn, thou who, by coveting other men's goods, seekest out as it were a shadow of right, and losest the firstborn of the heart, which are the better things. The firstborn indeed is faith. For no one does good things in whom faith has not gone before. All good works are spiritual sons, but among these faith is the firstborn. Whosoever therefore thou art that secretly covetest another's goods, thou losest faith within. For thou servest not by charity but by fraud, as one loving him whom thou desirest to die, that thou mayest succeed him.

220CVERS. 30-33.-- For there was not a house, etc. (AUG., quaest. 44 in Exod.) Since only the firstborn were dying, how was there no house in which there was not one dead? Or had this also been divinely provided, that in all the houses there should be firstborn, in whom the Egyptians should be struck, even those who dwelt in the land of Gessen? For here they were of men, or of animals, not of the land; that is, men and animals that were firstborn were dying, by a hidden and angelic stroke: not anything had been done in the earth or in the heaven, as the frog, or the locust, or the darkness. For since the land of Gessen had been free from such plagues, the benefit reached the Egyptians who dwelt in it with the Hebrews: but by this plague all were struck.
