For the priest of Madian could not go up into the mount of God, nor yet go down into Egypt, nor could the wife of Moses; but now she comes to him with her sons. For no one could go down into Egypt, and undergo the contests of the Egyptians, save an approved athlete, and one who, striving in the contest, refrains himself from all things. Moses, because a great and mighty athlete, went down into Egypt to the contests and the exercises of the virtues; Abraham too and Jacob, who 244Cby his very name is an athlete, when he had gone down into Egypt, was made as the stars of heaven in multitude. To others it turns out contrariwise to have gone down into Egypt. I know that Jeroboam, fleeing from Solomon, went down into Egypt, and rent and corrupted the people of God; because going down into Egypt he received from king Susachim as wife Tethimene, the sister of his wife.

VERS. 12-15.-- Jethro therefore offered, etc. (AUG. in Locutionibus, locut. 85.) And Jethro the kinsman of Moses took holocausts, etc. He put took for offered: or is it perhaps a matter of the sense, not of the manner of speech, so that he is understood to have offered what he is understood to have taken; although Moses is not read to have sacrificed, nor Aaron, nor any one of the Hebrews who were led out of the land of Egypt: yet nevertheless it was said above that Moses set up an altar. But Jethro 244Dwe read to have been a priest of Madian, that is, of the nation of Madian. But it is a wonder if at his coming Moses began to sacrifice, and not rather he himself who was a priest.

And they came, etc. (ORIG., ubi supra. ) Another reading: There came, he says, the elders, Aaron, and all Israel, to eat bread with the kinsman of Moses in the sight of God. Not all eat bread in the sight of God, but they who are elders, who are the older men, that is, who are perfect, proved in their merits, who whether they eat or drink, do it to the glory of God. All therefore that the saints do, they do in the sight of God: the sinner flees from the sight of God. Adam, after he had sinned, fled from the face of the Lord, and being questioned answered: 245AI heard thy voice, O Lord, and I hid myself, because I was naked (Gen. III). [Vulg. Gn 3:10 has vocem tuam audivi in paradiso: et timui, eo quod nudus essem, et abscondi me, "I heard thy voice in paradise; and I was afraid, because I was naked, and I hid myself"] Cain went out from the face of the Lord, because he was unworthy of the sight of the Lord. Therefore they who receive a fuller knowledge of God, if they do evil, do it before God. Whence psalm I: To thee only have I sinned, etc. He who does evil before God straightway repents, and says, I have sinned. He who departs from the sight of God knows not how to be converted and to purge his sin by repenting. This therefore is the difference between doing evil before God, and departing from the sight of God.

(ORIG.) Not in vain did Jethro come to Moses, nor in vain did he eat bread with the elders of the people in the sight of God; he gives Moses a counsel approved and useful, that he should choose men, and appoint as princes of the people such as worship God, are able, and hate pride. For such 245Bthey ought to be as are not proud, and hate pride; that is, that not only should they themselves be without vice, but should hate the vices in evil men: I do not say the men, but the vices.

Before God, etc. (AUG., quaest. 66.) It is asked: where, seeing that there was neither a tabernacle, nor the ark of the testament, which were instituted afterwards? nor can we take this as said of the future, as it was said of the manna, which was placed in a vessel, a golden one, namely. Therefore we ought to take that as done before God which was done in honour of the Lord.

VERS. 16-18.-- I will show the precepts, etc. (ID., quaest. 67.) It can be asked how Moses here said, etc., as far as he could not judge justly.

VERS. 19, 20.-- But hear my words, etc. (ID., 245Cquaest. 68.) The first question is why God should have his own servant, etc., as far as whereby it becomes the fuller, the more freely it is extended into things heavenly and eternal.

Be thou to the people, etc. (ID., quaest. 69.) But as to what he adds, etc., as far as but whence this can be proved I know not.

VERS. 21-23.-- But provide, etc. Unto the keeping of humility he is instructed by a stranger, lest he who was learning so many secrets of God from God himself should be lifted up, and should think that he had no need of human counsel. It was also prefigured that the teaching of the Gentiles would surpass the knowledge and the pride of the Jews; and that the things which were lacking in the law, believers from among the Gentiles would suggest for their fulfilling, and would bring a spiritual understanding to the law of God. Moses also knew that the law would hear this people, and 245Dwould do whatsoever it should say, and that a lessening of the law would also come about at the suggestion of the Gospel.

And appoint, etc. (ORIG., hom. 11.) Thou shalt ordain them, he says, tribunes and centurions, and they shall point out [indicabunt; read judicabunt, "shall judge"] the people at every hour, but nevertheless what shall be of greater weight they shall refer to thee. Let the princes of the people and the elders of the commons hear, who ought at every hour to judge the people, always and without ceasing to sit in judgment, to settle suits, and to reconcile those at variance, to call back the discordant into favour. Let each one learn from the holy Scriptures his own office. Moses, he says, that he may be in those things which are unto God, and may set forth the word of God to the peoples. But the other princes, whom he called tribunes, because they are set over a tribe, or centurions, or captains of fifty, 246Awho are set over the lesser judgments, deciding those matters which pertain to individuals. And this figure I think is to be kept also in the world to come. Whence Matthew XIX: When the Son of man shall sit upon the throne of his majesty, you also shall sit upon twelve seats, etc. Thou seest therefore that not only does the Lord judge, to whom the Father has given all judgment (Joan. V), but he has appointed princes to judge of lesser causes; but the matter that shall be of greater weight, let them refer to him. Therefore the Lord says of one man, Matthew V: That he shall be in danger of the council; but of another, Matthew XII: He shall be in danger of hell fire. But also of every idle word they are said to render an account. And therefore he did not say that we shall render an account to God, as concerning perjury. But thou shalt render thy oaths to the Lord. Matthew XII: The queen also of the south 246Bshall rise in judgment with the men of this generation, and shall condemn it. [Vulg. Mt 12:42 has cum generatione ista, "with this generation"; cum viris generationis hujus, with its men, is the wording of Lc 11:31, which closes et condemnabit illos, "shall condemn them"] Behold another kind of judgment, Matthew XIII: He that hath ears to hear, let him hear: for all these things are a shadow of things heavenly and an image of things to come.

(IDEM.) As it is written in Ecclesiastes I: The eye shall not be satisfied with seeing, nor the ear with hearing, [Vulg. Ecl 1:8 has non saturatur oculus visu, nec auris auditu impletur, "the eye is not filled with seeing, neither is the ear filled with hearing"; the gloss takes up satiari from the form printed here] neither can we be satisfied in considering the things which are written, wherewith it may build us up, in how many ways it may instruct us. For when I behold that Moses, a prophet full of God, to whom God spoke face to face (Exod. XXXIII), received counsel from Jethro his kinsman, a priest of Madian, I am struck dumb with exceeding wonder. For Moses heard the voice of his kinsman, and did whatsoever he said to him. He did not say, Since God speaks to me what I ought to do, how shall I receive counsel from a man, and from a Gentile man, 246Ca stranger to the people of God? and he listens not to who it is that speaks, but to what is said. We therefore, if we find anything wisely said by the Gentiles, let us not, together with the author's name, despise the sayings also; nor, because we hold the law of God, does it become us to swell with pride and to despise the words of the prudent; but proving all things, let us hold fast that which is good. But they who today are set over peoples, I do not say if certain things have already been revealed to them by God, but if they have any merit in the knowledge of the law, disdain the counsel of an inferior, even of a priest, much less of a layman, much less of a Gentile: but Moses, who was meek above all men (Num. XII), received accepti the counsel of an inferior, that he might give to princes a pattern of humility, and might mark out the image of a mystery to come. He knew that one day it would come to pass that Gentiles would bring forth good 246Dcounsel, bringing to Moses a good and spiritual understanding for the law, and that the law would hear them, doing all things that they should say. For the law cannot do as the Jews say, because the law is weakened in the flesh, that is, in the letter, and can do nothing according to the letter. For the law brought nothing to perfection (Hebr. VII). But according to this counsel which we bring to the law, all things can be done spiritually, and sacrifices offered which now cannot be offered carnally; the law of leprosy can be kept spiritually. As therefore we understand, perceive and give counsel, the law does all things: but according to the letter not all things, but few.

VERS. 24.-- Moses did all things which he had suggested, 247Aetc. A man familiar with God, most wise, not blushing to be taught by a lesser man and a Gentile, gave a pattern to teachers, that they should not blush to be instructed by any whomsoever.

VERS. 26, 27.-- But whatsoever was of greater weight, etc. (AUG., loc. 88.) Others: But the weighty matter they referred upon Moses: for so the Greek speech has it, which the Latins have translated, to Moses. By that manner of speech indeed the very care itself is figured, whereby it was said upon Moses, as though a burden laid upon him is signified. And matter moreover weighty was said for a weighty question.

CAPUT XIX.

VERS. 1, 2.-- Mense tertio, etc. (ORIG.) Alia littera: Mensis autem tertii exitus filiorum Israel de terra 247BAegypti: hac die venerunt in eremum Sina, et profecti sunt ex Raphidim, et venerunt in eremum Sina, et applicuit ibi Israel contra montem. Et Moyses ascendit in montem Dei, et vocavit eum Dominus de monte dicens: Haec dices domui Jacob, et nuntiabis filiis Israel, etc. Et paulo post: Descende et contestare populo, et purificabis illos hodie et cras, et lavent vestimenta sua, et sint parati in diem tertiam. Tertia enim die descendet Dominus in montem Sina coram omni populo. Hoc die reperitur dari lex, quae in tabulis lapideis scripta est digito Dei, sicut sequentia docent. Dies autem iste tertius apparet tertii mensis ab exitu filiorum Israel de Aegypto; ex quo die pascha fecerunt, id est, agnum immolaverunt et ederunt, qui fuit quartus decimus primi mensis, usque ad 247Cistum quo lex datur, dies quinquaginta numerantur, septemdecim scilicet primi mensis, reliqui ab ipso quarto decimo. Deinde omnes triginta secundi mensis, qui fiunt quadraginta septem et tertius tertii mensis qui a solemnitate occisi agni quinquagesimus est; ac per hoc in ista umbra futuri secundum agni immolati diem festum; quia quinquagesimo die lex data est, scripta digito Dei. Ita in ipsa veritate Novi Testamenti a festivitate immolati Agni Jesu Christi quinquaginta dies numerantur, ubi Spiritus sanctus de altissimis datus sit, qui est digitus Dei.

In die hac, etc. Quadragesima scilicet septima die postquam egressi sunt de Aegypto, prima die, scilicet Junii, pervenerunt ad montem Sina, ibique peractis duobus diebus quibus sanctificati sunt, tertio, 247Did est quinquagesimo, legem acceperunt: et haec est Pentecostes.

(RAB.) In Sina una mansio fuit, sed inter omnes maxima. Ibi enim lex accepta, tabernaculum erectum. Ibi Exodus et maxima pars Levitici omnesque libri legis pene conscripti sunt.

VERS. 3.-- Moyses autem, etc. (GREG., lib. VI Moral., cap. 27.) Alia littera: Moyses autem ascendit in montem, et Dominus descendit ad eum. Mons altitudo contemplationis est, in quem ascendimus, ut ad ea quae ultra infirmitatem nostram sunt intuendo sublevemur. Sed Dominus descendit, quia, sanctis multum proficientibus, parvum aliquid de se aperit.

VERS. 4, 5.-- Alas aquilarum. Vocat doctrinas 248Aduorum Testamentorum, quae nos ab infimis ad coelestia portant.

VERS. 6.-- Eritis mihi in regnum sacerdotale, etc. Membra scilicet summi sacerdotis in regnum coelorum venturi.

VERS. 7-9.-- Exposuit omnes sermones, etc. (GREG.) Notandum quod lex non incautis vel nescientibus ingeritur: sed expositis praemiis quae debentur servantibus, et beneficiis praeteritis quae jam experti sunt, et mirabilibus cum sponte se legi servandae devoveant, lex petentibus datur: ut secundum promissionem suam, quasi voti rei, qui violaverint, judicentur, nec de violentia sibi illata causentur.

VERS. 10.-- Vade ad populum, etc. (ORIG.) Descende 248Bet protestare populum, et purifica illum hodie et cras, et lavent vestimenta sua, et sint parati in diem tertium, etc. Qui vult venire ad verbum Dei, audiat quod praecipit Dominus: Sanctificatus venire debet et lotis vestibus. Si enim sordida attuleris vestimenta, dicetur tibi Matth. XXII: Amice, quomodo huc intrasti non habens vestem nuptialem? Nemo ergo potest audire verbum Dei, nisi prius sanctificatus, id est sanctus corpore et spiritu et lotis vestibus: ingressurus est enim paulo post ad coenam nuptialem, manducaturus de carnibus agni, et potaturus poculum salutis. Nemo intrat ad coenam hanc sordidis vestibus; unde Eccle. IX: In omni tempore sint vestimenta tua munda. Lota sunt enim semel vestimenta tua gratia baptismatis. II Cor. VII: Purificatus es corpore, mundatus 248Cab omni inquinamento carnis et spiritus. Act. XI: Quae ergo Deus mundavit, tu immunda ne feceris. Audi sanctificationis genus: ne accesseris, inquit, ad mulierem hodie et crastino, ut die tertio audias verbum Dei; hoc est quod ait Apostolus I Cor. VII: Bonum est homini mulierem non tangere, salvo tamen infirmis remedio nuptiali. I Cor. VII: Sed quia tempus breve est, superest ut qui habent uxores, sint tanquam non habentes.

Sanctifica illos, etc. (RAB.) Qui ad conspectum Dei desiderat venire, sanctificet se fide et conversatione, et lavet vestimenta, id est opera hodie et cras, in praesenti scilicet et in futuro, ut ad gloriam resurrectionis perveniat, quam tertius dies insinuat.

VERS. 11.-- In die enim tertia, etc. Hac die data 248Dest lex in tabulis lapideis scripta digito Dei, hic est tertius dies tertii mensis ab exitu filiorum Israel de Aegypto. A die ergo quo pascha fecerunt, id est quarto decimo primi mensis, usquequo lex data est, quinquaginta dies numerantur, decem et septem scilicet primi mensis, reliqui a quarto decimo, et omnes triginta secundi, qui sunt quadraginta septem, et tertius tertii mensis, qui est ab occisione agni quinquagesimus: sicut a passione Christi, usquequo datur Spiritus sanctus, quinquaginta dies numerantur. digitus enim Dei Spiritum sanctum significat.

(ISID., Quaest. in Exod., cap. 28, tom. V.) Quinquagesimo die post pascha lex Moysi data est etc., usque ad vel quia veritatem legis infidelibus per caliginem 249Adixit, ut videntes non videant, et audientes nos intelligant (Isa. VI).

VERS. 12.-- Cavete, etc. (RAB.) Prius prohibet populum in montem ascendere, mortemque minatur: nunc jubet ut clangente buccina ascendant in montem, humanam scilicet compescens audaciam, et ad obediendum invitans: quia ad praedicationis vocem debemus nos ad superna erigere, nec sacramentorum investigatione sponte praesumere, sed Dei vocationem sequi.

VERS. 13.-- Buccina. Quasi vocina: a voce, vel a vocando, vel a bucca

VERS. 14-17.-- Cumque lavissent vestimenta sua, etc. Non sufficit ad virtutem contemplationis emundare opera poenitentiae lacrymis, nisi sequatur castitas 249Bmentis et corporis. Unde Matth. V: Beati mundo corde, etc. Et Sap. I: In malevolam animam non introibit sapientia.

VERS. 18, 19.-- Totus autem mons Sinai fumabat, etc. (GREG. lib. VI Moral., cap. 27.) Legem daturus Dominus in igne fumoque descendit, etc., usque ad vincunt quod sunt.

(ID., lib. II Moral., cap. 2.) Sacra eloquia in exordio narrationum qualitates exprimunt, terminosque causarum aliquando a positione loci, aliquando a positione corporis, aliquando a qualitate aeris, etc., usque ad punituri autem Sodomam vespere venisse memorantur.

VERS. 20-25.-- Descenditque Dominus, etc. (ID., lib. V Moral., cap. 26.) Moyses ascendit, etc., usque 249Cad eum aliquatenus balbutiendo resonamus.

CHAPTER XIX.

VERS. 1, 2.-- In the third month, etc. (ORIG.) Another reading: And the going out of the children of Israel from the land 247Bof Egypt was of the third month: on this day they came into the desert of Sina, and they set forward from Raphidim, and came into the desert of Sina, and Israel pitched there over against the mountain. And Moses went up into the mount of God, and the Lord called him from the mountain, saying: These things shalt thou say to the house of Jacob, and thou shalt announce to the children of Israel, etc. And a little after: Go down and charge the people, and thou shalt purify them today and tomorrow, and let them wash their garments, and let them be ready against the third day. For on the third day the Lord will come down upon mount Sina in the sight of all the people. On this day the law is found to be given, which was written on tables of stone by the finger of God, as the things that follow teach. And this third day appears to be of the third month from the going out of the children of Israel from Egypt; from the day on which they kept the pasch, that is, immolated and ate the lamb, which was the fourteenth of the first month, as far as 247Cthat on which the law is given, fifty days are numbered, seventeen namely of the first month, the rest from the fourteenth itself. Then all thirty of the second month, which make forty-seven, and the third of the third month, which from the solemnity of the slain lamb is the fiftieth; and by this, in that shadow of things to come, according to the feast day of the immolated lamb; because on the fiftieth day the law was given, written by the finger of God. So in the very truth of the New Testament, from the festival of the immolated Lamb Jesus Christ fifty days are numbered, wherein the Holy Spirit was given from the highest places, who is the finger of God.

On this day, etc. On the forty-seventh day, namely, after they went out of Egypt, on the first day, namely, of June, they came to mount Sina, and there, when the two days were passed in which they were sanctified, on the third, 247Dthat is, the fiftieth, they received the law: and this is Pentecost.

(RAB.) In Sina there was one station, but the greatest of all. For there the law was received, the tabernacle set up. There Exodus and the greatest part of Leviticus and all the books of the law were almost wholly written.

VERS. 3.-- But Moses, etc. (GREG., lib. VI Moral., cap. 27.) Another reading: But Moses went up into the mountain, and the Lord came down to him. The mountain is the height of contemplation, into which we go up, that by gazing we may be lifted to those things which are beyond our weakness. But the Lord comes down, because, to saints who make much progress, he opens some small thing of himself.

VERS. 4, 5.-- The wings of eagles. He calls the teachings 248Aof the two Testaments, which bear us from the lowest things to the heavenly.

VERS. 6.-- You shall be to me a priestly kingdom, etc. Members, namely, of the high priest who is to come into the kingdom of heaven.

VERS. 7-9.-- He set forth all the words, etc. (GREG.) It is to be noted that the law is not thrust upon the unwary or the ignorant: but when the rewards which are due to those who keep it have been set forth, and the past benefits which they have already experienced, and the wonders, when of their own accord they devote themselves to the keeping of the law, the law is given to those who ask for it: so that according to their own promise, as men bound by a vow, those who violate it may be judged, and may not complain of violence done to them.

VERS. 10.-- Go to the people, etc. (ORIG.) Go down 248Band testify to the people, and purify it today and tomorrow, and let them wash their garments, and let them be ready against the third day, etc. He who wishes to come to the word of God, let him hear what the Lord commands: he ought to come sanctified and with washed garments. For if thou bring soiled garments, it will be said to thee, Matthew XXII: Friend, how camest thou in hither not having on a wedding garment? No one therefore can hear the word of God, unless first sanctified, that is, holy in body and in spirit and with garments washed: for he is about a little after to enter to the marriage supper, to eat of the flesh of the lamb, and to drink the cup of salvation. No one enters to this supper in soiled garments; whence Ecclesiastes IX: At all times let thy garments be clean. [Vulg. Ecl 9:8 has Omni tempore sint vestimenta tua candida, "at all times let thy garments be white"] For thy garments were washed once by the grace of baptism. II Corinthians VII: Thou art purified in body, cleansed 248Cfrom all defilement of the flesh and of the spirit. [Vulg. 2Cor 7:1 has the first-person exhortation mundemus nos ab omni inquinamento carnis et spiritus, "let us cleanse ourselves from all defilement of the flesh and of the spirit"] Acts XI: What therefore God hath cleansed, do not thou make unclean. [Vulg. Act 11:9 has tu ne commune dixeris, "do not thou call common"; Act 10:15 Quod Deus purificavit] Hear the manner of sanctification: go not near, he says, to a woman today and tomorrow, that on the third day thou mayest hear the word of God; this is what the Apostle says, I Corinthians VII: It is good for a man not to touch a woman, saving nevertheless to the weak the remedy of marriage. I Corinthians VII: But because the time is short, it remaineth that they who have wives be as if they had none.

Sanctify them, etc. (RAB.) He who desires to come to the sight of God, let him sanctify himself by faith and by conversation, and wash his garments, that is, his works, today and tomorrow, that is, in the present and in the world to come, that he may come to the glory of the resurrection, which the third day intimates.

VERS. 11.-- For on the third day, etc. On this day was given 248Dthe law written on tables of stone by the finger of God, this is the third day of the third month from the going out of the children of Israel from Egypt. From the day therefore on which they kept the pasch, that is, the fourteenth of the first month, until the law was given, fifty days are numbered, seventeen namely of the first month, the rest from the fourteenth, and all thirty of the second, which are forty-seven, and the third of the third month, which from the slaying of the lamb is the fiftieth: as from the passion of Christ, until the Holy Spirit is given, fifty days are numbered. for the finger of God signifies the Holy Spirit.

(ISID., Quaest. in Exod., cap. 28, tom. V.) On the fiftieth day after the pasch the law of Moses was given etc., as far as or because he spoke the truth of the law to the unbelieving through a mist, 249Athat seeing they might not see, and hearing they might understand us nos (Isa. VI). [Vulg. Lc 8:10 has et audientes non intelligant, "and hearing they may not understand"; the Clementine Is 6:9 reads Audite audientes, et nolite intelligere]

VERS. 12.-- Take heed, etc. (RAB.) First he forbids the people to go up into the mountain, and threatens death: now he commands that at the sounding of the trumpet they go up into the mountain, restraining, namely, human boldness, and inviting to obedience: because at the voice of preaching we ought to raise ourselves to things above, and not of our own accord to presume upon the searching out of the mysteries, but to follow the calling of God.

VERS. 13.-- Trumpet. As it were vocina: from vox, the voice, or from vocare, to call, or from bucca, the cheek

VERS. 14-17.-- And when they had washed their garments, etc. It does not suffice, for the virtue of contemplation, to cleanse one's works with the tears of penance, unless there follow chastity 249Bof mind and of body. Whence Matthew V: Blessed are the clean of heart, etc. And Wisdom I: Into a malevolent soul wisdom shall not enter.

VERS. 18, 19.-- And all mount Sinai was smoking, etc. (GREG. lib. VI Moral., cap. 27.) The Lord, being about to give the law, came down in fire and smoke, etc., as far as they overcome what they are.

(ID., lib. II Moral., cap. 2.) The sacred words in the opening of their narratives express the qualities and the bounds of their subjects, sometimes from the position of a place, sometimes from the position of the body, sometimes from the quality of the air, etc., as far as but they are recorded to have come in the evening when they were to punish Sodom.

VERS. 20-25.-- And the Lord came down, etc. (ID., lib. V Moral., cap. 26.) Moses went up, etc., as far 249Cas we in some measure echo him by stammering.

CAPUT XX.

VERS. 2.-- Ego sum Dominus Deus tuus. (ORIG., hom. 8 in Exod.) Qui contemnit praesens saeculum, scilicet Aegyptum, et per verbum Dei translatus non invenitur quia ad futurum saeculum festinet, de eo dicit Deus: Ego sum Dominus Deus tuus, etc. Non ad illos tantum qui de Aegypto profecti sunt, sed ad eos qui ultra Aegyptiis non serviunt, de Aegypto educti de domo servitutis (Psal. LXXX). Negotia saeculi, actus carnis, domus servitutis est: sicut relinquere saecularia, et secundum Deum vivere, domus libertatis est. Unde Joan. VIII: Si permanseritis in sermone meo, agnoscetis veritatem, et 249Dveritas liberabit vos. Ergo Aegyptus est domus servitutis; Judaea et Hierusalem, domus liberalitatis. Unde Gal. IV: Quae sursum est Hierusalem, libera est. Sicut ergo terrena Aegyptus filiis Israel domus dicitur servitutis ad comparationem Judaeae et Hierusalem, quae eis est domus libertatis: ita ad comparationem coelestis Hierusalem, quae est mater libertatis, totus mundus et quae in eo sunt, domus dicitur servitutis: et quia de paradiso libertatis per peccatum ventum est ad servitutem mundi, primus sermo decalogi de libertate est: Ego sum Dominus Deus tuus qui eduxi te terra Aegypti, etc.; hanc vocem in Aegypto audire non poteris. Et si tibi injungatur ut facias pascha, accingas lumbos, accipias sandalia 250Ain pedibus, virgam teneas in manu, et azyma cum amaritudine comedas. Sed nec in prima mansione, nec in secunda, nec in tertia, nec cum transieris mare Rubrum, etiamsi ad mare veneris, et fuerit tibi amaritudo in dulcedinem versa, nec in Elim, ubi erant duodecim fontes aquarum et septuaginta arbores palmarum, etiamsi Raphidim praeterieris caeterosque profectus ascenderis, nondum idoneus judicaris, sed cum veneris ad montem Sina. Vix ergo mereberis aliquando suscipere praecepta libertatis, et audire: Ego sum Dominus Deus tuus, qui eduxi te de terra Aegypti, de domo servitutis. Hic tamen non est sermo mandati, sed quis sit qui mandat ostendit. Primum mandatum ergo est: Non erunt tibi dii alii praeter me.

250B(AUG. quaest. in Exod.) Quaeritur quomodo decem praecepta legis dividenda sint? etc., usque nisi ad id quod dictum est: Non erunt tibi dii alii praeter me?

(ISID.) Dat Deus Moysi legem innocentiae nostrae, et agnitionis suae, quae in decem verbis consistit, scripta in tabulis lapideis digito Dei. Tria sunt quae pertinent ad dilectionem et cognitionem Trinitatis, septem ad dilectionem proximi, quibus humana societas non laeditur. Primum pertinet ad Deum Patrem: Dominus Deus tuus Deus unus est, ut hoc audiens unum Deum colas Patrem, et in multos fictos deos fornicationem non effundas. Secundum ad Filium: Non assumes nomen Domini Dei tui in vanum; id est, non putes creaturam esse Christum Dei Filium, quia omnis creatura vanitati subjecta est, sed 250Caequalem Patri, Deum de Deo, lumen de lumine.

VERS. 3.-- Non habebis deos alienos, etc. (ORIG.) Primum mandatum est: Non erunt tibi dii alii praeter me; et sequitur: Non facies tibi idolum, neque ullam similitudinem, etc. Haec omnia quidam simul putant esse unum mandatum: quod si ita computetur, non complebitur numerus decem mandatorum. Est ergo primum mandatum: Non erunt tibi dii alii praeter me, etc. Secundum, Non facies tibi idolum, neque ullam similitudinem, etc. Incipiamus ergo a primo mandato: Non erunt tibi dii alii praeter me. Si dixisset, Non sunt, absolutior sermo videretur; sed quia dicit, Non erunt, non negavit quia sint, sed ne illi sint, cui haec praecepta dantur, inhibuit. Hinc Paulum sumpsisse puto I Cor. VIII: Si quidem 250Dsunt qui dicuntur dii sive in coelo, sive in terra. In aliisque locis invenies deos nominari sicut ibi: Rex magnus super omnes deos, et Deus Deo locutus est, etc. De dominis idem Apostolus dicit: Sive throni, sive dominationes, etc. (Col. I.) Dominationes vero non aliud sunt quam ordo quidam dominorum et multitudo. In quo videtur sensum legis aperire Apostolus; ac si dicat: Licet sint domini multi, qui aliis gentibus dominentur, et dii multi qui ab aliis colantur: sed tamen unus Deus est nobis, et unus est Dominus. Unde Deut. XXXII: Cum divideret Excelsus gentes et separabat filios Adam, statuit terminos gentium secundum numerum angelorum Dei. Angelos ergo, quibus gentes commisit Excelsus, dominos 251Avel deos appellat Scriptura: deos quasi a Deo datos, et dominos a Domino potestatem sortitos. Unde eis dicitur psal. LXXXI: Ego dixi: Dii estis et filii Excelsi omnes. Vos autem sicut homines moriemini, et sicut unus de principibus cadetis; imitantes scilicet diabolum, qui princeps omnium factus est ad ruinam. Unde constat quod exsecrabiles fecit eos praevaricatio, non natura. Tibi ergo, o Israel, qui pars Dei et funiculus ejus es, non erunt, inquit, tibi dii alii praeter me: quia vere Deus, unus est Deus, et vere Dominus, unus est Dominus; caeteris vero ab ipso creatis contulit hoc nomen non natura, sed gratia. Non haec tantum dicuntur ad Israel secundum carnem, imo ad te qui Israel effectus es mente Deum videndo, et circumcisus corde, non 251Bcarne; si tamen ita agas ut dignus sis esse pars Dei, et in funiculo haereditatis ejus metiri: alioquin exemplo tibi sint qui ad hoc vocati fuerant ut essent pars Dei, et exigentibus peccatis dispersi sunt per omnes gentes, et educti de domo servitutis jam non Aegyptiis solis, sed omnibus gentibus serviunt. Qui enim peccat, servus est peccati (Joan. V).

VERS. 4.-- Non facies, etc. (ORIG.) Non facies tibi ipsi idolum, neque omnem similitudinem, etc. Aliud sunt idola, aliud dii. De diis enim dicit Apostolus, I Cor. VIII: Si quidem sunt dii multi, et domini multi, etc. De idolis vero, quia nil est idolum in mundo; de similitudinibus autem non addidit, quia nil sunt. Hic autem dicitur: Non facies tibi idolum, neque similitudinem omnem. Aliud est ergo facere 251Cidolum, aliud similitudinem. Verbi causa, si quis in auro et argento, ligno vel lapide, faciat speciem quadrupedis, vel serpentis, vel avis, et statuat adorandum, non idolum sed similitudinem facit. Qui vero facit quod non est, speciem scilicet quam oculus non vidit, sed animus sibi fingit; ut si quis humanis membris caput canis aut arietis formet, vel in uno hominis habitu duas facies, aut humano pectori postremas partes equi aut piscis; qui hoc facit, non similitudinem, sed idolum facit: facit enim quod non est, nec habet aliquid simile sui. Ideo dicit Apostolus quia idolum nihil est in mundo (I Cor. VIII). Non enim aliqua ex rebus constantibus assumitur species, sed quod mens otiosa et curiosa reperit. Similitudo vero est cum aliquid ex his quae sunt vel in coelo, vel in 251Dterra, vel in aquis formatur: sed non sicut de his quae in terra sunt vel in mari similitudinibus promptum est pronuntiare: ita etiam de coelestibus; nisi quis dicat de sole, et luna, et stellis hoc posse sentiri, quorum formas exprimit gentilitas. Sed Moses eruditus omni sapientia Aegyptiorum etiam quae apud illos erant in occultis prohibere cupiebat, sicut Hecatem aliaque daemonum formas, quae Apostolus spiritualia nequitiae in coelestibus vocat Ephes. VI; de quibus forte dicitur: Inebriatus est gladius meus in coelo (Isa. XXXIV). His enim formis et similitudinibus invocabant daemonia ad repellenda vel vitanda mala. Sciendum tamen quia cum decreveris hoc servare mandatum, et habere unum tantum Deum et Dominum, 252Ahoc est bellum denuntiare omnibus aliis, quibus in baptismo renuntiasti, solum Deum confitens Patrem et Filium et Spiritum sanctum. Sed hoc confitentes nisi toto corde diligamus Dominum Deum nostrum, tota anima, tota virtute ei adhaerentes, non sumus effecti pars Domini, sed in confinio positi illos patimur offensos quos fugimus, et ad quem fugimus, propitium non efficimus. Quibus propheta dicit: Vae duplici animo (Eccli. II). Et iterum: Usquequo claudicatis utroque pede? (III Reg. XVIII.) Ab illis ergo quasi fugaces caedimur, et a Domino nostro quasi instabiles non defendimur, unde Jer. IV: Amatores tui inimici facti sunt tibi. Multi sunt animae amatores, capti decore ejus, cum quibus meretricata est, dicens. Post amatores meos ibo qui dant 252Bmihi vinum meum, etc. (Ose. II): hos offendit reversa ad virum priorem. Nisi ergo tota fide permaneas cum viro tuo, pro sceleribus quae commisisti suspectus es; omnes motus tuos, aspectus et incessus notat; nil ultra in te lascivum videre, nihil dissolutum vel prodigum potest. Si ergo acceptam gratiam negligis, et implicas te saecularibus negotiis, continuo immundus spiritus redit cum septem nequioribus (Matth. XII), ne iterum expelli possit, et fiunt novissima pejora prioribus. Nulla ergo societas templo Dei cum idolis. Si Dei sumus, tales esse debemus in quibus habitet et inambulet Deus; unde Isa. LII: Qui portatis vasa Domini, exite et immundum nolite tangere, et ego recipiam vos, etc. Dicit ergo: Non erunt tibi dii alii praeter me.

252CVERS. 5-11.-- Non adorabis, etc. Aliud est colere, aliud adorare. Potest quis invitus adorare, vel adulando regibus idololatris, vel tormentis victus, cum sciat quia idolum nil est. Colere vero est toto his affectu et studio mancipari. Utrumque ergo resecat, ut nec affectu colas, nec specie adores.

Ego sum Dominus, etc. Vide benignitatem Dei: ut nos doceat et perfectos faciat, fragilitatem humanorum affectuum non recusat; omnia agit et patitur propter nos, notis et usitatis nobis loquitur affectibus.

(ORIG., ubi supra.) Omnis mulier aut sub viro est, et subjecta est legibus viri (Eph. V), aut meretrix est, et utitur libertate peccandi. Qui ingreditur ad meretricem, scit quod ad prostitutam et cunctis patentem ingressus est, ideo de aliis indignari non 252Dpotest. Qui legitimo utitur matrimonio, non patitur uxorem peccare, sed accenditur zelo ad servandam castitatem matrimonii, ut possit legitimus fieri pater. Sic omnis anima aut prostituta est cum daemonibus, et habet plures amatores ut intrent ad eam, modo spiritus fornicationis, modo avaritia, post hunc veniet spiritus superbiae et alii plurimi; alter tamen alteri non invidet, nec zelotypia movetur, sed sese invicem invitant; unde, adducit secum alios septem spiritus nequiores se, etc. (Luc. XI.) Sic nullam zelotypiam amatorum suorum patitur anima quae daemonibus est prostituta; si vero conjuncta fuerit viro legitimo, id est Christo, etiamsi aliquando fuerit peccatrix, ultra eam peccare non patitur, nec 253Aferre potest ut alludat adulteris; excitatur super eam zelus ejus, et defendit conjugii castitatem; si autem viderit eam temerantem conjugii jura, et occasionem quaerere peccandi, dat ei libellum repudii; unde Isa. L: Propter iniquitates vestras dimisit matrem vestram; haec qui loquitur et zelans est et zelo commotus hoc dicit. Est etiam novum genus hoc bonitatis ejus, ut etiam post adulterium revertentem et toto corde poenitentem suscipiat. Unde: Nunquid mulier si exierit a viro, et dormierit cum alio viro, revertens revertetur ad virum suum? etc. Et alibi: Et postquam fornicata es in his omnibus, dixi: Revertere, etc. (Jer. III.) Hic ergo Deus zelans si requirit et desiderat animam tuam sibi adhaerere, si servat, corripit et indignatur, velut zelotypia quadam 253Berga te usus, spem tibi esse salutis agnosce. Si vero correptionem contemnis, discedit a te zelus ejus, secundum illud: Propterea discedit zelus meus a te, etc. (Ezech. XVI.) Vide misericordiam et pietatem Dei; quando vult misereri, indignari se dicit et irasci, sicut per Jeremiam dicit: Dolore et flagello castigaberis, Hierusalem, ut non abscedat anima mea a te (Jer. VI). Flagellat enim omnem filium quem recipit (Hebr. XII). Hoc autem terribile, hoc extremum, si non corripimur pro peccatis: cum enim modum peccandi excedimus, auferetur a nobis etiam zelus Dei, nec ultra irascitur.

Visitans iniquitatem, etc. (ORIG.) Reddens peccata patrum in filios, etc. Aiunt haeretici quod non boni Dei sermo sit, qui pro peccatis alterius alterum dicat 253Cplecti; Deum tamen legis justum dicunt, nec possunt ostendere quomodo injustus sit, si alium alio peccante punit. Sed interior homo noster aut dicitur habere Deum Patrem, si secundum Deum vivit; aut diabolum, si voluntatem illius facit; unde Joan. VIII: Vos de patre diabolo estis, et desideria patris vestri facere vultis. Sicut ergo semen Dei in nobis est, cum verbum Dei servantes non peccamus, secundum illud I Joan. III, Qui ex Deo est, non peccat, quia semen Dei manet in eo; ita cum a diabolo suademur ad peccandum, semen ejus suscipimus; cum facimus quod suadet, jam genuit nos, nascimur enim ejus filii per peccatum. Sed quia fere sine adjutorio non peccamus, sicut adulterium nemo solus committit, necesse est adulteram consortem 253Dfieri, et si non plures, necesse est aliquem vel aliquam ministerio sceleris adjungi: qui omnes, velut unus ex altero, secundum persuasionis ordinem, generati, ex diabolo progeniem ducunt; velut in traditione Domini diabolus intravit in cor Judae, et primum filium peccati genuit, qui solus hoc facere non potuit; abiit ergo ad Scribas et Pharisaeos et pontifices; nascitur ergo ex Juda generatio tertia, et quarta peccati. Videamus ergo quomodo peccata patrum reddit Deus in filios in tertiam et quartam progeniem, et non in ipso. De patribus enim nihil dicitur. Diabolus ergo, quia jam peccandi modum excessit, tanquam vestimentum concretum in sanguine non erit mundus in hoc saeculo; non flagellatur 254Apro peccato; omnia enim ei servantur in futurum. unde Matth. VIII): Quid venisti ante tempus torquere nos? Redduntur autem peccata in filios quos genuit per peccatum, qui in carne positi flagellantur. Non vult Deus mortem peccatoris, sed ut magis convertatur et vivat (Ezech. XXXIII). Misericors ergo reddit peccata patrum in filios, quia diabolus et angeli ejus, qui etiam peccati patres efficiuntur, indigni sunt quod in praesenti corripiantur: filii eorum, et qui per ipsos ad societatem peccati asciti sunt, recipiunt in praesenti quod gesserunt, ut, purgati, diabolo in poena socii non efficiantur. Unde psalmo LXXXVIII: Visitabo in virga iniquitates eorum, etc., misericordiam autem meam non auferam ab eis. Deus enim zelans est, et animam quam 254Bsibi despondet in fide non vult in contaminatione peccati permanere, sed cito purgari. Si autem noluerit purgari, in interitu eorum ridebit, dicens eis Matth. XXV: Discedite a me, maledicti, in ignem aeternum, etc. Sciamus ergo nos in praesenti corripi, ut in futuro quiescamus. Unde: Cum castigamur, a Domino corripimur, ne cum hoc mundo damnemur.

(ID.) In hoc sermone solent nos haeretici suggillare, quod non sit boni Dei sermo, qui pro peccatis alterius alium judicat. Sed qui Deum legis mandantem haec dicent non bonum, justum tamen dicunt: nec ipsi quidem possunt ostendere quomodo justitiae videatur convenire si alio peccante alius puniatur.

254C(ISID.) Primum mandatum prohibet subreptionem; secundum, errorem; tertium interficit saeculi amorem; quartum, impietatem; quintum, crudelitatem; sextum, fornicationem; septimum, rapacitatem; octavum perimit falsitatem; nonum, mundi cupiditatem; decimum, adulterii cogitationem. Et notandum quod sicut decem plagis percutiuntur Aegyptii, sic decem praeceptis scribuntur tabulae, quibus populus Dei regatur et daemones occidantur. In duabus enim tabulis scripta est lex propter duo Testamenta significanda: vel quia sunt praecepta duo dilectionis Dei et proximi, in quibus tota lex pendet et prophetae (Matth. XXII). In una enim tria praecepta, quae ad dilectionem Dei pertinent; in altera vero septem, quae ad societatem proximi. Lapideae vero fuerunt 254Dtabulae, quia cor Judaeorum erat lapideum et insensibile, durum et stolidum; unde Ezech. XXXVI: Auferam cor lapideum, et dabo eis cor carneum. Et Apostolus ait (II Cor. III): Non in tabulis lapideis, sed in tabulis cordis carnalibus, quae sentiant scilicet et intelligant.

VERS. 12.-- Honora patrem, etc. Hoc est primum septem mandatorum quae pertinent ad proximum. Bene autem septem sunt. Septenarius enim ex ternario constat et quaternario. Ternarius vero animae congruit, quae est irascibilis, concupiscibilis et rationalis. Quaternarius corpori.

VERS. 13.-- Non occides, etc. (AUG., quaest. 61 in Exod.) Et quod dictum est: non occides, non putandum 255Aest fieri contra hoc praeceptum quod lex occidit, vel occidi aliquem Deus jubet: ille enim facit, qui jubet quod ministrum negare non licet.

VERS. 14.-- Non moechaberis. (ISID.) Quaeritur quoque quomodo differat non moechaberis ab eo quod paulo post dicitur: Non concupisces uxorem proximi tui? In eo enim quod dictum est, non moechaberis, poterat et illud intelligi; nisi forte in illis duobus praeceptis non moechandi, et non furandi, ipsa opera notata sint, in his vero extremis concupiscentia ipsa: quae tantum differunt, ut aliquando moechetur qui non concupiscit uxorem proximi, cum aliqua alia causa ipsi miscetur. Aliquando vero concupiscat, nec ei misceatur poenam timens: et hoc fortasse lex ostendere 255Bvoluit, quod utraque peccata sunt.

(ID.) Item quaeri solet utrum nomine moechiae etiam fornicatio teneatur, etc., usque ad atque illorum membrorum non legitimus usus prohibitus debet intelligi.

VERS. 15.-- Non furtum facies, etc. (AUG., quaest. 71 in Exod.) Sed rursum quaeritur quomodo differat, non furtum facias, ab eo quod paulo post dicitur de non concupiscendis rebus proximi? Non quod omnis qui concupiscit rem proximi, furatur; sed omnis qui furatur, rem proximi concupiscit. Poterat illa generalitate, ubi de non concupiscenda re proximi praecipitur, etiam illud quod ad furtum pertinet contineri.

VERS. 16, 17.-- Non loqueris contra proximum, etc. (AUG., ubi supra. ) In eo etiam quod dictum est, etc., 255Cusque ad et hujusmodi.

VERS. 18.-- Cunctus autem populus videbat voces, etc. (AUG., quaest. 72 in Exod.) Solet quaeri quomodo populus videbat vocem, cum vox non ad visum, sed ad auditum pertinere videatur, etc., usque ad audi quid luceat, non solemus dicere.

VERS. 19.-- Loquere tu nobis, etc. (AUG., quaest. 73.) Significatur ad vetus testamentum timorem potius pertinere, sicut ad novum dilectionem; quamvis in veteri novum lateat, et in novo vetus pateat. Quomodo autem tali populo tribuatur videre vocem Dei, si 256Ahoc accipiendum est intelligere, cum sibi loqui Deum timeant ne moriantur, non satis elucet.

VERS. 20.-- Nolite timere, etc. (AUG., quaest. 74.) Sic illi cohibendi erant a peccatis, timendo scilicet ne poenas sensibiles paterentur; quia nondum poterant amare justitiam, et tentabantur a Domino, ut appareret cujusmodi essent; non ut Deo noti fierent qualescunque essent, quem non latebat, sed ut inter se et sibimetipsis.

VERS. 21, 22.-- Stetitque populus, etc. (GREG., homil. 6.) In quibusdam locis Scripturae historia servanda est, etc., usque ad Moses accessit ad caliginem, in qua erat Deus.

Non facietis deos, etc. (AUG., quaest. 76.) Repetitur quod in primo praecepto dictum est; et per argentea 256Bet aurea, omnia simulacra intelliguntur, sicut in psalmo CXIII: Simulacra gentium argentum et aurum, opera manum hominum.

VERS. 24.-- Altare de terra, etc. Ostendit ideo, interdictis simulacris, altare de terrena materia concedi, ut uni Deo hostia et sacrificia in eo offerantur.

(GREG., lib. III Moral., cap. 20.) Altare de terra Deo facere, est incarnationem Christi sperare. Tunc enim a Deo nostrum munus accipitur, cum in hoc altari nostra humilitas, id est super Dominicae incarnationis fidem posuerit quidquid operatur. In altari ergo de terra oblatum munus ponimus, si actus nostros Dominicae incarnationis fide solidamus.

VERS. 25, 26.-- De sectis lapidibus. (ISID.) Secti 256Clapides sunt qui unitatem scindunt, et seipsos dividunt a societate fratrum: hos in corpore suo non recipit Christus, cujus corporis figuram altaris constructio significat. Lapides non secti ex quibus altare construi jubetur, sunt qui per fidem in unitate solidantur; de quibus dicitur I Petri II: Vos estis lapides vivi, coaedificati in domos spirituales. His non est injectum ferrum, quia incorrupti sunt, et jacula maligni ignita non receperunt: omnes unum altare faciunt in unitate fidei et concordia charitatis ª In praeceptis decalogi enumerandis Origenes et Augustinus in testimoniis Glossae initio capitis allatis videntur contrarias sequi sententias. Nam Origenes existimat non posse computari decem mandata nisi secundum sit: Non facies tibi idolum neque ullam similitudinem, etc. Augustinus autem, licet illam Origenis enumerationem non rejiciat, aliam tamen diversam judicat aptiorem, ut prohibitionem de idolis ac simulacris adorationis causa faciendis, existimet esse primi praecepti explicationem: et deinde duo praecepta statuit ad vetita desideria cohibenda, unum quo concupiscentia uxoris alienae, alterum quo concupiscentia omnium aliarum rerum quae proximi sunt, refrenetur. Origenem sequuntur Athanasius in Synopsi Scripturarum; Gregorius Nazianzenus in carmine quo decem praecepta enumerat; Chrysostomus in secunda expositione Matthaei, hom. 49; Hieronymus et Ambrosius cap. vi Epistolae ad Ephesios. Augustini autem enumerationem eo sensu sequuntur doctores scholastici, quo ipsemet Augustinus, ita scilicet eam priori praeferentes, ut tamen priorem non reputent incongruam. Et revera repugnantia quam invenit pravus affectus in duabus illis concupiscentiis subjugandis, videtur potius expetisse duo distincta praecepta, cum sint qui concupiscant uxorem alterius, et dedignantur tamen divitias aut mancipia alterius concupiscere, et vice versa: at nemo est veri Dei cultor qui difficultatem patiatur in idolis spernendis. Caeterum haec diversitas enumerandi in re ipsa nulla est: non enim ponitur aliquod praeceptum ab Augustino quod negatur ab Origene, aut vice versa; sed unus praecepta ratione cujusdam subordinationis conjungit, quae alter ratione verbalis connexionis separat, et e converso. Nam conjungit Augustinus praeceptum non faciendi idola praecepto non colendi nisi unum Deum, quia illud huic subordinatur et est explicatior ejus etiam ad caeremonias gentilium vetandas extensio: Origenes autem ea in duo praecepta dispertitur, quia contextu ipso sermonis discerni videntur, sine ulla nota connexionis, sicut caetera sequentia: Non habebis deos alienos; Non facies tibi sculptile; Non assumes, etc. Rursus illas duas concupiscentiae duplicis prohibitiones in unum praeceptum conjicit Origenes, propter consequentiam contextus, quae videntur ad unam pertinere sententiam..

CHAPTER XX.

VERS. 2.-- I am the Lord thy God. (ORIG., hom. 8 in Exod.) Of him who despises the present world, that is to say Egypt, and, being translated by the word of God, is not found, because he hastens to the world to come, God says: I am the Lord thy God, etc. Not to those only who set out from Egypt, but to those who serve the Egyptians no longer, brought out of Egypt, out of the house of bondage (Psal. LXXX). The businesses of the world, the acts of the flesh, are the house of bondage: just as to leave the things of the world, and to live according to God, is the house of liberty. Whence John VIII: If you shall continue in my word, you shall know the truth, and 249Dthe truth shall make you free. Egypt, therefore, is the house of bondage; Judea and Jerusalem, the house of liberality. As, therefore, earthly Egypt is called for the children of Israel a house of bondage in comparison with Judea and Jerusalem, which for them is a house of liberty: so, in comparison with the heavenly Jerusalem, which is the mother of liberty, the whole world and the things that are in it are called a house of bondage: and because from the paradise of liberty men came, through sin, to the bondage of the world, the first word of the decalogue is concerning liberty: I am the Lord thy God who brought thee out of the land of Egypt, etc.; this voice thou wilt not be able to hear in Egypt. Even if it be laid upon thee to keep the pasch, to gird thy loins, to take sandals 250Aupon thy feet, to hold a rod in thy hand, and to eat unleavened bread with bitterness. But neither in the first station, nor in the second, nor in the third, nor when thou shalt have passed the Red Sea, even if thou come to the sea and the bitterness be turned for thee into sweetness, nor in Elim, where were twelve fountains of waters and seventy palm trees, even if thou pass Raphidim by and climb the remaining stages, art thou yet judged fit, but only when thou shalt have come to Mount Sinai. Scarcely, therefore, wilt thou at some time deserve to receive the precepts of liberty, and to hear: I am the Lord thy God, who brought thee out of the land of Egypt, out of the house of bondage. This, however, is not the word of a commandment, but it shows who he is that commands. The first commandment, therefore, is: There shall not be to thee other gods beside me. [Vulg. Ex 20:3 Non habebis deos alienos coram me, "Thou shalt not have strange gods before me"; Migne's own lemma at VERS. 3 below prints the Vulgate form, and the wording used throughout this gloss is the Septuagint's]

250B(AUG. quaest. in Exod.) It is asked: how are the ten precepts of the law to be divided? etc., as far as unless to that which was said: There shall not be to thee other gods beside me?

(ISID.) God gives to Moses the law of our innocence and of the knowledge of himself, which consists in ten words, written on tables of stone by the finger of God. There are three which pertain to the love and the knowledge of the Trinity, seven to the love of one's neighbour, by which human society is not injured. The first pertains to God the Father: The Lord thy God is one God, that, hearing this, thou mayest worship one God the Father, and mayest not pour out fornication upon many feigned gods. The second to the Son: Thou shalt not take the name of the Lord thy God in vain; that is, do not think that Christ the Son of God is a creature, because every creature is made subject to vanity, but equal to the Father, God of God, 250Clight of light.

VERS. 3.-- Thou shalt not have strange gods, etc. (ORIG.) The first commandment is: There shall not be to thee other gods beside me; and there follows: Thou shalt not make to thyself an idol, nor any likeness, etc. [Vulg. Ex 20:4 Non facies tibi sculptile, neque omnem similitudinem, "Thou shalt not make to thyself a graven thing, nor the likeness of any thing"; the distinction between idol and likeness drawn below rests on the word Migne prints] Some think all these together to be one commandment: but if it be so reckoned, the number of ten commandments will not be made up. The first commandment, therefore, is: There shall not be to thee other gods beside me, etc. The second, Thou shalt not make to thyself an idol, nor any likeness, etc. Let us begin, therefore, from the first commandment: There shall not be to thee other gods beside me. If he had said, They are not, the statement would seem more absolute; but because he says, There shall not be, he did not deny that they are, but forbade that they should be to him to whom these precepts are given. Hence I think Paul took it, I Cor. VIII: If indeed 250Dthere are those who are called gods, whether in heaven or on earth. And in other places thou wilt find gods named, as there: A great king above all gods, and God hath spoken to God, etc. [Vulg. Ps 49:1 Deus deorum Dominus locutus est, "The God of gods, the Lord, hath spoken"; the verse is adduced to show that Scripture names gods in the plural] Of lords the same Apostle says: Whether thrones, or dominations, etc. (Col. I.) But dominations are nothing else than a certain order and multitude of lords. In which the Apostle seems to open the sense of the law; as if he should say: Though there be many lords, who rule over other nations, and many gods who are worshipped by others: yet nevertheless there is one God to us, and one Lord. Whence Deut. XXXII: When the Most High divided the nations and separated the sons of Adam, he appointed the bounds of the peoples according to the number of the angels of God. [Vulg. Dt 32:8 juxta numerum filiorum Israel, "according to the number of the children of Israel"; the reading Migne prints is the Septuagint's, and the argument that follows stands on it] The angels, therefore, to whom the Most High committed the nations, Scripture calls lords 251Aor gods: gods as being given by God, and lords as having obtained power from the Lord. Whence it is said to them in psalm LXXXI: I have said: You are gods, and all of you the sons of the Most High. But you like men shall die, and shall fall like one of the princes; imitating, that is, the devil, who was made the prince of all unto ruin. Whence it is plain that transgression, not nature, made them accursed. To thee, therefore, O Israel, who art the portion of God and his cord, there shall not be, he says, to thee other gods beside me: because he who is truly God is one God, and he who is truly Lord is one Lord; but upon the rest, created by himself, he conferred this name not by nature, but by grace. Nor are these things said only to Israel according to the flesh, but rather to thee who hast been made Israel by seeing God in mind, and circumcised in heart, not 251Bin flesh; if only thou so act that thou be worthy to be the portion of God, and to be measured in the cord of his inheritance: otherwise let those be an example to thee who had been called to this, that they should be the portion of God, and, their sins requiring it, were scattered through all nations, and, having been brought out of the house of bondage, now serve not the Egyptians alone, but all nations. For he who sins is the servant of sin (Joan. V).

VERS. 4.-- Thou shalt not make, etc. (ORIG.) Thou shalt not make to thyself an idol, nor any likeness, etc. Idols are one thing, gods another. For of gods the Apostle says, I Cor. VIII: If indeed there are many gods, and many lords, etc. But of idols, that an idol is nothing in the world; of likenesses, however, he did not add that they are nothing. But here it is said: Thou shalt not make to thyself an idol, nor any likeness whatsoever. It is one thing, therefore, to make 251Can idol, another to make a likeness. For example, if a man in gold and silver, in wood or in stone, makes the figure of a fourfooted beast, or of a serpent, or of a bird, and sets it up to be adored, he makes not an idol but a likeness. But he who makes what is not, that is, a figure which the eye has not seen but the mind feigns to itself; as if a man should form a dog's head or a ram's upon human limbs, or two faces in the one shape of a man, or the hinder parts of a horse or of a fish upon a human breast; he who does this makes not a likeness, but an idol: for he makes what is not, nor has it anything like itself. Therefore the Apostle says that an idol is nothing in the world (I Cor. VIII). For no figure is taken from things that subsist, but what an idle and curious mind has invented. But a likeness is when something is formed out of those things which are either in heaven, or on 251Dearth, or in the waters: but as it is easy to pronounce concerning the likenesses of those things which are on earth or in the sea, it is not so concerning the heavenly ones; unless one should say that this can be understood of the sun, and the moon, and the stars, whose forms the heathen represent. But Moses, learned in all the wisdom of the Egyptians, desired to forbid also those things which among them were in secret, such as Hecate and the other forms of demons, which the Apostle calls the spirits of wickedness in the high places, Ephes. VI; of which perhaps it is said: My sword is inebriated in heaven (Isa. XXXIV). For by these forms and likenesses they used to invoke demons for the repelling or the avoiding of evils. Yet it must be known that when thou hast resolved to keep this commandment, and to have one God and Lord only, 252Athis is to declare war upon all the others, whom thou didst renounce in baptism, confessing God alone, Father and Son and Holy Spirit. But confessing this, unless we love the Lord our God with the whole heart, cleaving to him with the whole soul, with the whole strength, we are not made the portion of the Lord, but, set on the border, we suffer those whom we flee to be offended, and him to whom we flee we do not make favourable. To whom the prophet says: Woe to the double soul (Eccli. II). And again: How long do you halt upon both feet? (III Reg. XVIII.) [Vulg. 3 Kg 18:21 Usquequo claudicatis in duas partes?, "How long do you halt between two sides?"] From those, then, we are cut down as fugitives, and by our Lord we are not defended as unstable, whence Jer. IV: Thy lovers are made thine enemies. [Vulg. Jer 4:30 contempserunt te amatores tui; animam tuam quaerent, "thy lovers have despised thee, they will seek thy life"; the sentence Migne prints stands nearest Lam 1:2] Many are the lovers of the soul, taken with her beauty, with whom she has played the harlot, saying: I will go after my lovers who give 252Bme my wine, etc. (Ose. II): these she offends when she returns to her former husband. Unless, therefore, thou remain in whole faith with thy husband, thou art suspect for the crimes which thou hast committed; he marks all thy motions, thy looks and thy goings; he can see nothing further in thee that is wanton, nothing dissolute or prodigal. If, therefore, thou neglect the grace received, and entangle thyself in worldly businesses, straightway the unclean spirit returns with seven others more wicked (Matth. XII), that he may not be cast out again, and the last things become worse than the first. There is, then, no fellowship of the temple of God with idols. If we are God's, we ought to be such in whom God may dwell and walk; whence Isa. LII: You that carry the vessels of the Lord, go out and touch not the unclean thing, and I will receive you, etc. He says, therefore: There shall not be to thee other gods beside me.

252CVERS. 5-11.-- Thou shalt not adore, etc. It is one thing to worship, another to adore. A man can adore unwillingly, either flattering idolatrous kings, or overcome by torments, when he knows that an idol is nothing. But to worship is to be made over wholly to these with affection and zeal. He cuts off both, therefore, that thou mayest neither worship with affection, nor adore in outward show.

I am the Lord, etc. See the kindness of God: that he may teach us and make us perfect, he does not refuse the frailty of human affections; he does and suffers all things for our sake, and speaks to us by affections known and familiar to us.

(ORIG., ubi supra.) Every woman is either under a husband, and is subject to the laws of her husband (Eph. V), or is a harlot, and uses the liberty of sinning. He who goes in to a harlot knows that he has gone in to one prostituted and open to all, and therefore he cannot 252Dbe indignant about the others. He who uses lawful marriage does not suffer his wife to sin, but is kindled with jealousy to keep the chastity of the marriage, that he may be able to become a lawful father. So every soul is either prostituted with demons, and has many lovers that they may come in to her, now the spirit of fornication, now avarice, and after this will come the spirit of pride and very many others; yet the one does not envy the other, nor is moved with jealous rivalry, but they invite one another in turn; whence, he brings with him seven other spirits more wicked than himself, etc. (Luc. XI.) So the soul that is prostituted to demons suffers no jealous rivalry of her lovers; but if she has been joined to a lawful husband, that is, to Christ, even if at some time she has been a sinner, he does not suffer her to sin further, nor 253Acan he bear that she should dally with adulterers; his jealousy is stirred up over her, and he defends the chastity of the marriage; but if he sees her violating the rights of the marriage, and seeking an occasion of sinning, he gives her a bill of divorce; whence Isa. L: For your iniquities has he put away your mother; and he who speaks this is both jealous and, moved by jealousy, says this. There is also this new kind of his goodness, that he receives her even after adultery when she returns and repents with her whole heart. Whence: If a woman go out from her husband, and sleep with another man, shall she return again to her own husband? etc. And elsewhere: And after thou hast committed fornication in all these things, I said: Return, etc. (Jer. III.) Here, therefore, if God being jealous requires and desires thy soul to cleave to him, if he keeps it, rebukes and is indignant, using as it were a certain jealous rivalry 253Btoward thee, know that there is hope of salvation for thee. But if thou despise the rebuke, his jealousy departs from thee, according to that: Therefore my jealousy departs from thee, etc. (Ezech. XVI.) See the mercy and the loving-kindness of God; when he wishes to have mercy, he says that he is indignant and is angry, as he says by Jeremiah: Thou shalt be chastised with sorrow and with the scourge, O Jerusalem, that my soul depart not from thee (Jer. VI). For he scourges every son whom he receives (Hebr. XII). But this is a terrible thing, this is the utmost, if we are not rebuked for our sins: for when we exceed the measure of sinning, even the jealousy of God will be taken from us, and he is angry no more.

Visiting the iniquity, etc. (ORIG.) Rendering the sins of the fathers upon the sons, etc. The heretics say that it is not the word of a good God, who says that one is punished 253Cfor the sins of another; yet they say that the God of the law is just, and they cannot show how he is unjust [injustus; read justus, "how he is just"], if he punishes one when another sins. But our inward man is said either to have God for father, if he lives according to God; or the devil, if he does his will; whence John VIII: You are of your father the devil, and you will do the desires of your father. As, therefore, the seed of God is in us, when, keeping the word of God, we do not sin, according to that of I John III, He who is of God sins not, because the seed of God abides in him; so when we are persuaded by the devil to sin, we receive his seed; when we do what he persuades, he has now begotten us, for we are born his sons through sin. But because we scarcely ever sin without a helper, as no one commits adultery alone, it is necessary that a partner in the adultery be made, 253Dand if not more, it is necessary that some man or woman be joined to the service of the crime: all of whom, as it were one from another, begotten according to the order of the persuasion, draw their offspring from the devil; as in the betraying of the Lord the devil entered into the heart of Judas, and begot the first son of sin, who could not do this alone; he went therefore to the Scribes and the Pharisees and the chief priests; there is born, therefore, from Judas a third and a fourth generation of sin. Let us see, therefore, how God renders the sins of the fathers upon the sons unto the third and the fourth generation, and not upon the man himself. For of the fathers nothing is said. The devil, therefore, because he has already exceeded the measure of sinning, like a garment clotted in blood, will not be clean in this world; he is not scourged 254Afor sin; for all things are kept for him unto the future. whence Matth. VIII): Why art thou come to torment us before the time? But the sins are rendered upon the sons whom he has begotten through sin, who, being set in the flesh, are scourged. God wills not the death of the sinner, but rather that he be converted and live (Ezech. XXXIII). Mercifully, therefore, he renders the sins of the fathers upon the sons, because the devil and his angels, who are also made the fathers of sin, are unworthy to be rebuked in the present: their sons, and those who through them have been taken into the fellowship of sin, receive in the present what they have done, that, being purged, they may not be made partners with the devil in punishment. Whence in psalm LXXXVIII: I will visit their iniquities with a rod, etc., but my mercy I will not take away from them. [Vulg. Ps 88:34 misericordiam autem meam non dispergam ab eo, "but my mercy I will not scatter away from him"; Migne's plural is what carries the argument to the sons] For God is 254Bjealous, and the soul which he betroths to himself in faith he wills not to remain in the defilement of sin, but to be quickly purged. But if she will not be purged, he will laugh at their destruction, saying to them, Matth. XXV: Depart from me, you cursed, into everlasting fire, etc. Let us know, therefore, that we are rebuked in the present, that in the future we may rest. Whence: When we are chastised, we are rebuked by the Lord, that we be not condemned with this world.

(ID.) In this saying the heretics are wont to reproach us, that it is not the word of a good God, who judges one for the sins of another. But those who shall say that the God of the law who commands these things is not good, yet call him just: nor indeed can they themselves show how it should seem agreeable to justice if, when one sins, another is punished.

254C(ISID.) The first commandment forbids stealthy deceit; the second, error; the third slays the love of the world; the fourth, impiety; the fifth, cruelty; the sixth, fornication; the seventh, rapacity; the eighth destroys falsehood; the ninth, the coveting of the world; the tenth, the thought of adultery. And it is to be noted that as the Egyptians are struck with ten plagues, so the tables are written with ten precepts, by which the people of God may be ruled and the demons slain. For the law was written on two tables that the two Testaments might be signified: or because there are two precepts of love, of God and of one's neighbour, on which the whole law hangs, and the prophets (Matth. XXII). For on the one are three precepts, which pertain to the love of God; on the other seven, which pertain to the fellowship of one's neighbour. But the tables were 254Dof stone, because the heart of the Jews was stony and unfeeling, hard and dull; whence Ezech. XXXVI: I will take away the stony heart, and will give them a heart of flesh. And the Apostle says (II Cor. III): Not in tables of stone, but in the fleshly tables of the heart, which, that is, may perceive and understand.

VERS. 12.-- Honour thy father, etc. This is the first of the seven commandments which pertain to one's neighbour. And rightly are they seven. For the number seven consists of three and four. And three befits the soul, which is irascible, concupiscible and rational. Four, the body.

VERS. 13.-- Thou shalt not kill, etc. (AUG., quaest. 61 in Exod.) And as to what was said: thou shalt not kill, it is not to be thought 255Athat anything is done against this precept in that the law kills, or that God commands someone to be killed: for he does it who commands what it is not lawful for the minister to refuse.

VERS. 14.-- Thou shalt not commit adultery. (ISID.) It is also asked: how does thou shalt not commit adultery differ from that which is said a little after: Thou shalt not covet thy neighbour's wife? [Vulg. Ex 20:17 Non concupisces domum proximi tui, nec desiderabis uxorem ejus, "Thou shalt not covet thy neighbour's house, neither shalt thou desire his wife"; the wording Migne prints is Dt 5:21's] For in that which was said, thou shalt not commit adultery, that also could be understood; unless perhaps in those two precepts of not committing adultery and not stealing the deeds themselves are marked, but in these last the very concupiscence: which differ so far, that sometimes a man commits adultery who does not covet his neighbour's wife, when he is joined to her for some other cause. But sometimes he covets, and is not joined to her, fearing punishment: and this perhaps the law wished to show, 255Bthat both are sins.

(ID.) Again it is wont to be asked whether under the name of adultery fornication also is held, etc., as far as and the unlawful use of those members ought to be understood to be forbidden.

VERS. 15.-- Thou shalt not steal, etc. (AUG., quaest. 71 in Exod.) But again it is asked how thou shalt not steal differs from that which is said a little after concerning the not coveting of a neighbour's goods? Not that everyone who covets his neighbour's goods steals; but everyone who steals covets his neighbour's goods. In that generality, where it is enjoined not to covet a neighbour's goods, that also which pertains to theft could have been contained.

VERS. 16, 17.-- Thou shalt not speak against thy neighbour, etc. (AUG., ubi supra. ) In that also which was said, etc., 255Cas far as and things of this sort.

VERS. 18.-- And all the people saw the voices, etc. (AUG., quaest. 72 in Exod.) It is wont to be asked how the people saw a voice, since a voice seems to pertain not to sight but to hearing, etc., as far as hear what shines, we are not accustomed to say.

VERS. 19.-- Speak thou to us, etc. (AUG., quaest. 73.) It is signified that fear pertains rather to the Old Testament, as love to the New; although in the Old the New lies hidden, and in the New the Old lies open. But how it can be granted to such a people to see the voice of God, if 256Athis is to be taken as understanding, since they fear that God should speak to them lest they die, is not sufficiently clear.

VERS. 20.-- Fear not, etc. (AUG., quaest. 74.) Thus they were to be restrained from sins, by fearing, that is, lest they should suffer sensible punishments; because they were not yet able to love justice, and they were tempted by the Lord, that it might appear of what sort they were; not that they might become known to God whatever sort they were, from whom nothing was hidden, but that they might become known among themselves and to their own selves.

VERS. 21, 22.-- And the people stood, etc. (GREG., homil. 6.) In certain places of Scripture the history is to be kept, etc., as far as Moses went to the darkness in which God was.

You shall not make gods, etc. (AUG., quaest. 76.) What was said in the first precept is repeated; and by the things of silver 256Band of gold, all idols are understood, as in psalm CXIII: The idols of the nations are silver and gold, the work of the hand of men.

VERS. 24.-- An altar of earth, etc. He shows that therefore, idols being forbidden, an altar of earthly matter is granted, that to the one God victims and sacrifices may be offered upon it.

(GREG., lib. III Moral., cap. 20.) To make God an altar of earth is to hope for the incarnation of Christ. For then is our gift received by God, when upon this altar our humility, that is, has laid whatever it does upon the faith of the Lord's incarnation. Upon an altar of earth, therefore, we lay the offered gift, if we make our acts firm by faith in the Lord's incarnation.

VERS. 25, 26.-- Of hewn stones. (ISID.) Hewn 256Cstones are they who cut the unity asunder, and divide themselves from the fellowship of the brethren: these Christ does not receive into his body, of which body the building of the altar signifies the figure. The stones not hewn, of which the altar is commanded to be built, are they who through faith are made firm in unity; of whom it is said, I Peter II: You are living stones, built together into spiritual houses. [Vulg. 1 Pt 2:5 et ipsi tamquam lapides vivi superaedificamini, domus spiritualis, "be you also as living stones built up, a spiritual house"] Upon these no iron has been laid, because they are uncorrupted, and have not received the fiery darts of the evil one: all make one altar in the unity of faith and the concord of charity. In numbering the precepts of the decalogue, Origen and Augustine, in the testimonies of the Gloss cited at the head of this chapter, seem to follow contrary opinions. For Origen holds that the ten commandments cannot be reckoned unless it stand thus: Thou shalt not make to thyself an idol, nor any likeness, etc. Augustine, however, though he does not reject that enumeration of Origen's, yet judges another, a different one, more fitting, so that he reckons the prohibition of making idols and graven images for the sake of worship to be the explication of the first precept: and then he sets down two precepts for restraining forbidden desires, one whereby the coveting of another's wife, the other whereby the coveting of all other things that are one's neighbour's, is to be restrained. Origen is followed by Athanasius in the Synopsis of Scripture; Gregory Nazianzen in the poem in which he numbers the ten precepts; Chrysostom in his second exposition of Matthew, homily 49; Jerome and Ambrose, chapter six of the Epistle to the Ephesians. Augustine's numbering, in that sense, is followed by the scholastic doctors, who, even as Augustine himself, prefer it to the former, yet without holding the former to be unfitting. And indeed the contrariety which a perverse disposition finds in subduing those two covetings would seem rather to have wanted two distinct precepts, since there are those who covet another's wife, and yet disdain to covet another's wealth or servants, and the reverse: but no true worshipper of God finds it difficult to spurn idols. Yet this diversity of numbering is, in the thing itself, nothing at all: for no precept is laid down by Augustine that is denied by Origen, or the reverse; but the one joins precepts by a certain ordering of subordination, which the other separates by reason of a verbal connection, and conversely. For Augustine joins the precept of not making idols to the precept of worshipping no God but one, because the former is subordinate to the latter and is the fuller explication of it, extending also to the forbidding of the ceremonies of the Gentiles: but Origen divides that same matter into two precepts, because they seem to be distinguished by the very structure of the sentence, with no mark of connection, just as the rest that follow: Thou shalt not have strange gods; Thou shalt not make to thyself a graven thing; Thou shalt not take, etc. And again, those two prohibitions of the twofold coveting Origen joins into one precept, on account of the sequence of the context, which seem to belong to one and the same sentence.

CAPUT XXI.

  • 257A

VERS. 1. 6.-- Haec sunt judicia, etc. (AUG., loc. 91 in Exod.) Distinctius amodo explicantur ea quae ad proximum pertinent; notanda locutio cum dictum sit Moysi: Haec sunt judicia quae propones eis; caetera ad populum dicuntur.

Si emeris servum Hebraeum, etc. (AUG., quaest. 77 in Exod.) Ne Christiani hic flagitarent a dominis suis, apostolus jubet dominis servos esse subditos (I Petr. II), ne nomen Domini et doctrina blasphemetur: illud enim inde constat in mysterio esse praeceptum, quia pertundi subula ejus aurem ad postem praecepit Deus, qui libertatem recusasset.

(GREG., hom. 3 in Ezech., tom. II.) Sciendum quia 257Bcum activa et contemplativa vita ex Dei gratia sint, etc., usque ad quia illa quies intima in aenigmate videtur.

Sex annis. (ISID.) In sex aetatibus hujus saeculi servientes, septimo die, id est aeterno sabbato, liberabimur, si tamen voluerimus esse liberi dum in hoc saeculo servimus peccato. Si autem noluerimus, perforabitur auricula in testimonium inobedientiae, et cum uxore et filiis nostris, quos praetulimus libertati, id est cum carne et operibus ejus, peccato serviemus in aeternum.

VERS. 7-12.-- Si quis vendiderit filiam, etc. (AUG., quaest. 78 in Exod.) Non abibit ita ut recedunt ancillae, etc., usque ad intelligitur quod cum eo gratis exibit, non a marito separata.

VERS. 13-17.-- Qui autem non est insidiatus, etc. (RAB., in Exod., tom. II.) Homicidis non spontaneis, etc., usque ad Christi posuerit, salvus erit.

(AUG., quaest. 79 in Exod.) Si autem nolens, sed Deus tradiderit in manus ejus, etc. Ita hoc dicitur, etc., usque ad et Judas ideo juste tradidit.

VERS. 18.-- Si rixati fuerint, etc. (RAB.) Idem est, si quis cum proximo contendens duritia mentis, vel parcitate, spernendo scilicet vel non consulendo, exasperavit; et ille infirmatus tristitia, a fervore dilectionis languerit, reus per confessionem, operam poenitentiae et impensam promissae emendationis in medicos, id est doctores, impendat, qui infirmum baculo consolationis erigant, et fomentis praedicationum in pristinum dilectionis statum restituant. 257DIn correctione necessaria est discretio, ne qui corrigere debet, occidat, vel in desperationem vel odium mittat.

VERS. 22-28.-- Si rixati fuerint. (ID.) Si quis alicujus animam, post conceptum verbi, per incuriam vel fraudem laeserit, ut vitale germen boni operis sanum proferre non possit: etiam si ipsa adhuc in fide vivens perstiterit, ut damnum illatum per poenitentiam 258Arestituat, secundum Evangelii ministerium necesse habebit. Si autem ipsa anima per errorem necata, post prolatum abortivum, hoc est opus mortiferum, in infidelitate perseveraverit, sine dubio seductor illius, quasi verus homicida, aeterna morte reus erit.

(AUG., quaest. 80 in Exod.) Quod non formatum puerperium noluit ad homicidium pertinere, etc., usque ad et ideo nondum sensibus praedita.

(ORIG., hom. 10 in Exod.) Quod si litigabunt duo viri, et percusserint mulierem praegnantem, et exierit infans ejus nondum formatus, detrimentum patietur, quantum indixerit vir mulieris, et dabit pro anima sua. Quod si formatus fuerit, dabit animam pro anima, oculum pro oculo, etc. Primum videamus de eo 258Bqui nondum formatus exierit, quomodo jubetur damno pecuniae percuti unus ex litigantibus, cum duo dicantur. Quid etiam hoc quod vir mulieris imponit ei, et non eis; et dabit, et non potius dabunt. Quod si infans jam formatus exierit, animam dare pro anima facile intelligimus: morte scilicet vindicetur peccatum. Quae autem sequuntur, operae pretium est explicare. Nec enim videtur etiam infans formatus calce litigantis percussus, oculum in ventre perdidisse, dentes vero omnino non habuit. Si enim hoc ad matrem referamus, quomodo abortiens mulier oculum perdit, aut dentes dolet? Sed esto aut in oculo aut in dente percussa abortivit, livorem vel vulnus accepit, quid etiam combusturam dicimus pro combustura? Nunquid litigantibus 258Cviris assistens mulier comburi potuit, ut combustura pro combustura solvatur? In Deuteronomio quoque haec facile explicari non queunt. In Scripturis tamen iisdem vocabulis et officiis membra animae nominantur, quibus utuntur vel appellantur membra corporis. Unde Matth. VII: Vides festucam in oculo fratris tui, et trabem quae in oculo tuo est non vides. Et Matth. XIII: Qui habet aures audiendi audiat. Et Rom. X: Quam speciosi pedes evangelizantium pacem!
